--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page appears to be the beginning of a treatise on uroscopy, likely from a 15th-century medical manual. It follows the medieval tradition of diagnosing internal imbalances (the four humors) by observing the color, consistency, and contents of a patient's urine.</meta>

### <unclear>Concerning</unclear> the <unclear>examination</unclear> of urine

If it is yellow-brown and thick and watery, it signifies visible <note>original: 'sichtig'; likely referring to visible sediment or a specific degree of clarity</note> urine.

Also, if it has bubbles and is moist.

If it is red and thick, that signifies a rush <note>original: 'flucht', referring to a flux or abnormal flow of humors</note> or a surge of the blood.

Also, if it is thin and red and clear, that signifies a rush from the right <term>heart</term> <note>In medieval medicine, the heart was believed to be the source of vital heat; 'right' may refer to the right ventricle or a specific side of the body’s heat balance</note> caused by heat and dryness.

Also, if it is white and pale, so that it is like glass, that is the <term>quartz</term> <note>likely a term for a specific crystal-clear or glassy diagnostic category</note> caused by heat and dryness.

Also, if it is bluish <note>original: 'orchiel', referring to the color of orchil, a purple-blue dye made from lichens</note> and is black, that signifies an illness of the brain and the heart; it is much to be feared, and it signifies death. 

But if the bluish and the black turn into a red and chestnut color, then know that the person will recover from the sickness. 

However, if it becomes black like the <note>original: 'laubikait', likely meaning leaf-green or a livid, greenish-black color</note>, then there is great fear <note>a prognosis of impending death</note>.

[A large gray rectangle containing a color checker and a resolution target is placed over the center of the page, obscuring a significant portion of the text.]

->[Stamp: BAVARIAN STATE LIBRARY MUNICH]<-

Inc. Typ. No. 1622.

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page appears to be a practical diagnostic guide from a late medieval medical manual. It continues the study of uroscopy—the art of diagnosing illness by examining a patient's urine—which was a primary diagnostic tool for physicians of the 15th and 16th centuries.</meta>

->**On the ailments of the urine**<- <margin>faint note: On the ailments of the urine</margin>

If the urine is brown and thick and watery, it signifies an ailment in the loins, or perhaps stems from cold and from dampness. <note>In medieval medicine, health was a balance of "humors" (fluids). Cold and dampness were qualities associated with the humor of phlegm.</note>

If it is red and thick, that signifies a flux or dysentery <note>original: "flucht oder rutsinn"</note> from superfluous blood. 

If it is small <note>referring to low volume</note> and red and thin, that signifies an ailment of the right side from heat and from dryness.

If it is white and pale, then the ailment is in the left side <note>original: "glencke seyten"</note> of the head, caused by heat and by dryness.

If it is lead-colored <note>original: "plenar", likely meaning a dark, leaden hue</note> and dark, that signifies a dangerous sickness of the brain and of the stomach; death is always to be feared. If it turns from this leaden darkness and blackness into a redness and clarity, then know that the person has recovered from the sickness. But if it turns from clarity to black, then his death is before his eyes.

If it becomes green like grass, that signifies that the person is to fear for his life.

If a film <note>original: "pel"</note> or little bubbles appear on top of the urine, as happens in water when it rains, and they do not quickly dissolve, that signifies a long-lasting illness and an ailment of the loins, as well as a foul mouth and body <note>original: "posse mund leib"</note> and a sick head.

If a foam appears in the urine that looks like grains, that signifies an ailment of the head on the right side or in the lungs; if it is red on the left side, it indicates the chest; if so, there is great pain <note>original: "wern"</note> in the chest.

If a cloud appears on top of the urine like a spiderweb, that signifies an ailment of the chest and lungs.

->**Concerning the foam of the urine**<-

If the foam is always on top, that signifies a sickness of the chest and at the heart, which comes from the concealment of things <note>This likely refers to "occult" or hidden internal blockages that the body cannot easily expel.</note>. But if the foam is split like water <note>meaning the bubbles are unstable or breaking apart</note>, that signifies cravings <note>original: "gelüscht", often associated with pregnancy or abnormal appetite</note>.

If it is redder in the glass <note>the *matula*, or uroscopy flask</note>, that signifies the place of the child or the seed <note>original: "stätte d' kind od' pfaff"; this phrase is obscure but likely relates to reproductive health or the "priest" as a metaphor for a vital organ.</note>. If the urine stinks and the pain is located below the navel, it comes from the liver; in this case, the sediment sinks and the pain is located more on the right side.

->**Concerning cloudy urine**<-

When many things <note>sediments or particles</note> appear in it: that which is at the top of the urine signifies an illness of the chest. If it is in the middle of the urine and sinks down toward the bottom, that signifies a sickness of the stomach. If it is at the very bottom and the urine is red, it signifies an ailment of the bladder.

[Stamp: BAVARIAN STATE LIBRARY MUNICH]

<insert>Inc. Typ. No 1622.</insert> <note>A library shelfmark or inventory number.</note>

<summary>This page serves as a diagnostic key for uroscopy, correlating the color, clarity, and sediment of urine with specific internal ailments and the balance of bodily humors. It provides a grim prognosis for dark or "leaden" urine while offering signs of recovery through changes in color.</summary>
<keywords>uroscopy, medieval medicine, diagnostics, humors, liver health, bladder ailments, Early New High German, urinary sediment</keywords>

<meta>This page contains handwritten bibliographical notes regarding a censored or mutilated manuscript. The text transitions from Latin to German, documenting the removal of specific pages related to the legend of Pope Joan.</meta>

...the whole of chapter 99 is missing, concerning Pope Joan <note>original: "de Joanna Pap:". Pope Joan is a legendary female figure who, according to popular medieval lore, reigned as pope for a few years until her gender was discovered when she gave birth during a procession.</note>: namely [crossed out: leaves] leaves

folios 107 and 108 are missing: 107 and 108 were undoubtedly cut out on purpose by the monks in Thierhaupten <note>original: "à Monachis in Thierhaupten studio excisa". Thierhaupten was a Benedictine monastery in Bavaria, Germany. The word "studio" implies they did this with specific intent or zeal.</note>. In the index, however, it still remains: C. A.

folio 107 verso <note>original: "f 107ᵇ". In manuscript studies, "b" denotes the "verso" or back side of a leaf.</note> would have contained the image of the Pope giving birth.

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This is the first page of Giovanni Boccaccio’s *De Mulieribus Claris* (Concerning Famous Women). As the opening page, it establishes the author’s dedication and the purpose of the work.</meta>

1.

Giovanni Boccaccio of Certaldo, to the most famous woman,
Andrea de' Acciaiuoli of Florence, Countess of Altavilla.

**P**esterday, excellent lady, while I was removed a little from the lazy crowd and almost free from other cares, I wrote a small book <note>Original: *libellum*. Boccaccio often refers to his works modestly as "little books."</note> for the exceptional praise of the female sex and the solace of friends, rather than for any great benefit to the public. But while I was turning over in my mind to whom I should first send it—lest it wither in idleness with me, and so that, supported by another's favor, it might go more securely into public—I noticed that it should not be destined for a leading man, but rather, since it speaks of women, for some illustrious woman.

While searching for one more worthy than others, there came to mind that very bright Italian radiance, the singular glory not only of women but of kings: Joanna, most serene Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily. <note>Joanna I of Naples (1325–1382), a powerful and controversial monarch during Boccaccio's time.</note> Having weighed both the splendors of her famous lineage and ancestors, as well as the new praises she has sought for herself with a brave heart, I fell into the desire of sending this humble and devoted work before the throne of her highness.

At last, because the royal splendor is so vast and the little spark of my slender work is almost half-extinguished, I feared lest the lesser light be entirely driven into the darkness by the stronger one. I gradually withdrew my plan, and after searching many others with a new investigation, I finally turned my vow from the illustrious queen to you—and not without merit. For while I turned over in my mind your mild and famous manners, your exceptional honesty (the highest honor of matrons), and the elegance of your words; and when I noticed the generosity of your soul and the strengths of your intellect, by which you far exceed the typical gifts of women; and when I saw that what nature took away from the "weaker sex," <note>Boccaccio reflects the medieval view of women as the "weaker sex" (*sexui infirmiori*), though he argues Andrea is an exception.</note> God in His liberality has poured over and supplemented in your heart with wonderful virtues, and wished it to be designated by the very name with which you are distinguished—since <term>Andreas</term> <note>Boccaccio is making a pun on her name, Andrea, connecting it to the Greek word *andros* meaning "man" or "manly," suggesting she possesses masculine strength of character.</note> in Greek...

<summary>In this opening dedication, Giovanni Boccaccio introduces his collection of biographies of famous women, explaining that he chose to dedicate the book to Andrea de' Acciaiuoli because her "manly" virtues and intellect make her more suitable than even Queen Joanna of Naples.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Boccaccio, Andrea de' Acciaiuoli, Joanna I of Naples, Famous Women, dedication, medieval literature, gender roles, Altavilla</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page appears to be the conclusion of a formal dedication, likely from Giovanni Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women), addressed to a noblewoman—historically identified as Andrea Acciaioli, Countess of Altavilla. It transitions from praising her lineage to encouraging her to read the following biographies for moral improvement.</meta>

...as we say in Latin, that men might name you as one to be equalled with the most upright women, even those of the most ancient times. And therefore, since in our own era—filled as it is with many who perform splendid deeds—you are a most clear example of antiquity, I wished to add the gift of the title of this little book to your well-deserved brilliance. I believe that these few small letters will add no less beauty to your name among posterity than the county of Monte Odorisio <note>A town in the Abruzzo region of Italy.</note> once did, or the county of Altavilla <note>A territory in the Kingdom of Naples; the dedicatee was the Countess of Altavilla.</note> does now, for which fortune has made you illustrious.

Therefore, I send to you and dedicate to your name what I have written so far concerning **famous women**. I pray, renowned lady—by that sacred name of modesty <note>original: "pudicicie"</note> in which you stand out most among mortals—that you receive with a joyful spirit this small gift from a man of learning <note>original: "scolastici hominis"</note>. If you believe me at all, I urge you to read it sometime; for it will surely soothe your leisure hours with its merits, while you delight in feminine virtue and the charm of these histories.

Nor do I think this reading will be done in vain if, as a woman rivaling the deeds of the past, you rouse your excellent spirit toward better things. And though you may sometimes find wantonness mixed with sacred things (which the necessity of recounting these stories has forced me to do), do not skip over or shudder at it. Rather, persevering, just as one entering a garden extends ivory hands to pull a flower only after pushing aside the sharp thorns, so you should set aside the obscene and gather what is praiseworthy. 

Whenever you read of something in a pagan woman <note>original: "gentili muliere"; literally a "gentile" or non-Christian woman of antiquity.</note> worthy of the Christian religion I have professed, which you do not feel within yourself, let a blush rise in your mind and rebuke yourself: that you, who have been anointed with the chrism <term>Chrism: a consecrated oil used in Christian baptism and confirmation, symbolizing a spiritual seal.</term> of Christ, are surpassed in integrity, modesty, or virtue by a foreigner. 

With your intellect (in which you are most strong) called to its full strength, do not only refuse to be surpassed, but strive to surpass any woman in excellent virtue. Just as you are conspicuous for your body’s joyful youth and flowering beauty, so may you become more outstanding than not only your contemporaries but even the women of old in the integrity of your soul. Remember that beauty is not to be decorated with makeup <note>original: "pigmentis"; literally pigments or paints used for cosmetics.</note> (as most of you women do), but adorned...

<summary>In this dedicatory preface, the author presents his collection of biographies of famous women to a noblewoman, urging her to use the lives of ancient pagan women as a moral mirror to improve her own Christian virtue.</summary>
<keywords>Andrea Acciaioli, Altavilla, famous women, feminine virtue, moral education, pagan vs Christian virtue, cosmetics, modesty</keywords>

<meta>This page contains the conclusion of Giovanni Boccaccio's dedicatory epistle to Andrea Acciaioli, followed by the formal beginning of the Preface to his work "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women).</meta>

·1·

...by your **honesty**, **holiness**, and **pious works**, so that when you have pleased Him who bestowed these gifts <note>referring to God</note>, you may not only be among the radiant ones in this fleeting mortality, but by that same Giver of Graces, shedding your human form, you may be received into eternal brightness. Furthermore, if you deem it worthy, O most excellent of women, grant this work the boldness to go forth into the world. It will go (as if sent under your protection) safe from the insults of the malicious, and it will carry your name, together with those of other illustrious women, splendidly through the mouths of men. It will make you known to those present through your merits (since you cannot be present everywhere in person) and preserve your memory eternally for posterity.

### The book of Giovanni Boccaccio of Certaldo <note>Certaldo was Boccaccio's ancestral home and place of death</note> concerning famous women, dedicated to Andrea Acciaioli of Florence, Countess of Altavilla, begins happily in the name of God.

[Decorative initial **S** containing a dragon/serpent] <note>The letter "S" begins the word "Scripsere" (They have written)</note> Some of the ancients wrote books long ago in brief about **illustrious men**. And in our own time, in a more extensive volume and with a sharper style, the distinguished man and excellent poet **Francesco Petrarch** <note>original: "Franciscus petrarcha"</note>, our teacher, writes—and worthily so. For those who put all their effort, substance, blood, and life itself—when the opportunity demanded—to surpass others with famous deeds, certainly deserve that their name be carried into the perpetual memory of posterity. 

Truly, I have wondered much why women have had so little importance among such writers that they have attained no favor of memory in any special individual description, since it is clearly evident from more extensive histories that some women have acted as vigorously as they have bravely. And if men are to be praised when they perform great things with the strength granted to them, how much more should women be praised? To almost all women, a softness of nature <note>original: "mollicies"</note>, a weak body, and a slow mind have been given by the nature of things. If they should then take on a **manly spirit** <note>original: "virilem... animum." In the 14th century, "virtue" (virtus) was etymologically linked to "manliness" (vir); Boccaccio uses this to emphasize the extraordinary nature of their achievements.</note> and, with a famous mind and conspicuous virtue, dare and complete even things most difficult for men, they are to be extolled. And therefore, lest by merit...

<summary>This page concludes Boccaccio's dedication to Andrea Acciaioli and begins the preface to his collection of biographies of famous women, where he notes that while many have written about famous men (including his mentor Petrarch), women have been unfairly neglected in historical records.</summary>

<keywords>Giovanni Boccaccio, Famous Women, Andrea Acciaioli, Petrarch, female virtue, biography, Latin literature, Renaissance humanism</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page is part of Giovanni Boccaccio’s preface to "Concerning Famous Women" (De Mulieribus Claris). He is currently defining his criteria for which women he has chosen to include in this collection of biographies.</meta>

...[lest they] be cheated of their due; it came to my mind to gather together into one volume those women whom memory recalls for the honor of their glory, and to add to them certain others from among many who were made notable either by boldness, the power of their intellect and industry, a gift of nature, or the favor or injury of fortune. To these I have added a few who, even if they did not perform anything worthy of remembrance themselves, nevertheless provided the greatest causes for significant events <note>The author uses the Latin *facinoribus*, which can imply both great feats and notorious crimes.</note>.

Nor (I wish) should it seem inconsistent to the reader if they find Medea, Flora, and Sempronia—whose talents were perhaps great but destructive—or others like them mixed with Penelope, Lucretia, and Sulpicia, who were most chaste matrons. For it is not my intention to define "renown" <term>Renown: (Latin *claritas*) The quality of being widely known; Boccaccio argues that fame does not always equal moral goodness.</term> so strictly that it always seems to equate to virtue; rather, with the readers' kind permission, I intend to use the term in a broader sense. I will include as "renowned" those women whom I know to be most famous throughout the world for any deed whatsoever. I do this because I remember often reading, alongside such illustrious men as Leonidas, the Scipios, Cato, and Fabricius <note>These were all famous Roman and Greek leaders known for their integrity and military skill.</note>, accounts of the rebellious Gracchi <note>Tiberius and Gaius Gracchus were Roman reformers whose political actions led to great social upheaval.</note>, the wily Hannibal, the traitor Jugurtha, the bloodstained Sulla and Marius of the civil wars, the equally rich and greedy Crassus, and others of their kind.

Truly, since praising what is worthy of remembrance and condemning what is unspeakable with rebukes will sometimes not only drive the noble to glory and the cowardly away from ill-fated deeds, but will also restore what seems to have been taken away from the beauty of this little work by the shame of some women, I have dared at times to insert into these histories some pleasant encouragements to virtue and to add stings to drive people to flight from and detestation of crimes. In this way, it will happen that, within the mixed delight of these histories, a sacred utility will enter the mind. And lest I seem to have touched only the highlights of things in the ancient manner, I have judged it not only useful but opportune to draw out into a somewhat longer narrative those things I was able to learn from trustworthy sources.

<summary>Boccaccio explains that his book includes both virtuous and wicked women because "renown" applies to anyone widely known for their actions. He justifies including "bad" examples by claiming they serve as a moral warning to readers.</summary>
<keywords>Boccaccio, Famous Women, Renown, Virtue vs Vice, Roman History, Moral Education, Historical Biography</keywords>

thinking that the deeds of these women will please women no less than

who have been presented in no such special volume (as has already
been said) and have been described by no one else, as if we were
rendering to them some kind of reward. May God himself, the king
and father of all, be present for this pious work, and as the
favorer of the labor undertaken, may He grant that what I write
be written to His true praise.

# Concerning Eve, our first mother. Chapter One.

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the manuscript, focusing on the creation of Eve as the first subject in a series of biographies of famous women.</meta>

[The page features a large, ornate woodcut border on the top and left sides. The top border contains several figures intertwined with foliage. The left border depicts a man with a sword in a circular medallion at the top, and below it, a scene of the Fall of Man: a serpent with a human head is coiled around a tree, and Eve is shown handing the forbidden fruit to Adam.]

As I prepare to write about the splendors through which famous women have shone, it will not seem unworthy to have taken my beginning from the Mother of All <note>original: "matre omniū" — referring to Eve as the common ancestor of humanity.</note>. Indeed, that most ancient parent, being the first, was distinguished by magnificent splendors. For she was not produced in this sorrowful vale of miseries in which we other mortals are born to labor; nor was she fashioned by the same hammer or anvil <note>The author uses metaphors of metallurgy to contrast the messy, "industrial" nature of human birth with Eve's divine origin.</note>, nor did she come into the world wailing, lamenting the "crime" of being born, or weak in the manner of others. 

On the contrary—something heard to have happened to no other person—when the Great Creator of all things had already joined Adam with His own hand from the mud of the earth, and had transferred him from the field that was later given the name **Damascus** <note>A common medieval tradition held that Adam was created from the red clay of a field near Damascus before being moved to the Garden of Eden.</note> into the garden of delights, and had released him into a peaceful sleep; then, by a craft known only to Himself, He led her forth from the sleeper's side. She was mistress of herself, mature for a husband, and filled with joy at the beauty of the place and the sight of her Maker. She was an immortal mistress of things, a companion to the waking man, and named **Eve** by him. 

What greater, what more splendid thing could ever have happened to one being born? Furthermore, we may judge her to have been marvelous in bodily beauty; for what is fashioned by the finger of God that does not exceed all other things in beauty? And although this beauty is destined to perish with age, or is liable to fail in the flower of youth at the slight pulse of sickness, nevertheless, because women count it among their chief gifts, and have already gained much glory from it (in the undiscerning judgment of mortals), it has been included among their excellences as a principal luster, and will continue to be included in the accounts that follow. This woman, moreover, made a citizen of Paradise by right of origin as well as residence, and clothed in a splendor unknown to us, while together with her husband <insert>she enjoyed</insert> the delights of the place...

<summary>This opening page introduces Eve as the primary figure of female fame, emphasizing her divine creation from Adam's side and her residence in Paradise. The author establishes that while physical beauty is fleeting, it is a significant "splendor" that defines the history of famous women.</summary>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Eve (the first woman) and transitions to the story of Semiramis, the Queen of Assyria. The Latin text is from Giovanni Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women).</meta>

-> . 3 . <-

While she was greedily enjoying [Paradise], the envious enemy of her happiness—with wicked persuasion—suggested to her mind that if she were to go against the single law imposed on her by God, she could attain even greater glory. When she, with a feminine levity <note>The author attributes Eve's actions to "levitas feminea," a common medieval trope suggesting women were more easily swayed or less constant than men.</note> more than was appropriate for her or for us, believed him and foolishly thought she would ascend to higher things, she first used a certain flattering suggestion to draw her husband (who was otherwise hardly flexible) into her own opinion. Acting against the law, once they had tasted the fruit of the tree of good and evil with reckless daring, they brought themselves and all their future descendants out of peace and eternity into anxious labors, misery, and death; and from a delightful homeland, they were led away among thorns, clods of earth, and rocks. For when the glittering light with which they were clothed had departed, and they were rebuked by their troubled Creator, they were girded with aprons <note>original: *perizomatibus*; a reference to the fig leaves used to cover their nakedness in Genesis 3:7</note> and driven out from that place of delights into the fields of Hebron <note>Medieval tradition often claimed that after the expulsion, Adam and Eve lived and were buried in the "Field of Damascus" near Hebron.</note> to live as exiles.

There, this distinguished woman, famous for these deeds—since she was the first (as some believe) to invent the art of spinning with a distaff <term>distaff: a tool used in spinning to hold unspun fibers</term> while her husband turned the earth with hoes—frequently experienced the pains of childbirth. She suffered equally miserably from the mental anguish that tortures the soul over the death of children and grandchildren; and enduring the bitter cold, the heat, and other hardships, weary from her labors and destined to die, she eventually reached old age.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of famous women, following the introductory chapters. This section focuses on Semiramis, the legendary Queen of Assyria, transitioning from the mythological origins of goddesses to historical (albeit legendary) rulers of the ancient Near East.</meta>

### Concerning Semiramis, Queen of the Assyrians. Chapter Two.

**Semiramis** was a distinguished and most ancient queen of the Assyrians. However, the passage of time has stripped away the knowledge of which parents she was descended from <note>original: "annositas abstulit," literally "old age has carried it away."</note>, except for what pleases the ancients in their myths. They claimed she was the daughter of **Neptune** <note>The Roman god of the sea, often identified with the Greek Poseidon.</note>, whom they affirmed with mistaken belief to be the son of Saturn and the god of the sea. Even if this should not be believed, it serves as evidence that she was born of noble parentage.

This woman married **Ninus**, the exceptional king of the Assyrians, and by him gave birth to an only son, **Ninyas**. Indeed, after Ninus had subdued all of Asia and finally the **Bactrians** <note>An ancient Iranian people inhabiting the region of modern-day Afghanistan and Uzbekistan.</note>, he died from the strike of an arrow. At that time, Semiramis was still a young woman and her son was but a boy. Thinking it not at all safe to leave the reins of such a vast and wicked Eastern empire to such a tender age, she possessed such a great spirit that she—a woman—dared to take up the rule of those nations. These were peoples whom a fierce man had subdued with weapons and coerced with strength, yet she intended to rule them through skill and wit.

For by a certain craftiness, a great deception devised by a woman's mind, she first of all deceived her dead husband’s army. It was not surprising, for the features of Semiramis’s face were very similar to her son’s: both had smooth cheeks, her womanly voice was not different from a boy’s voice due to her age, and in her physical stature she differed little or only slightly in height from her son. Assisted by these traits, and lest any incident might hinder her by detecting the fraud, she covered her head with a **tiara** <term>tiara: a high, often conical headpiece worn by ancient Eastern monarchs</term>, while hiding her arms and legs with veils. 

And because this style of dress was unusual for the Assyrians until then, she arranged it so that the novelty of the garment would not cause wonder among the inhabitants by ordering that all the people should use similar attire. And so, the former wife of Ninus, pretending to be his son—a woman simulating a boy—attained royal majesty with wonderful diligence. She maintained both the empire and military discipline, and having lied about her sex, she performed many great and extraordinary deeds even compared to the strongest men. 

And while sparing no labor, nor being terrified by danger, she overcame envy through unheard-of deeds. She was not afraid to eventually reveal to everyone who she was, or what she had simulated through womanly fraud. She wished to show that it is not the sex, but the spirit that is suited to **rule** <note>original: "imperio," referring to the power of command or the empire itself.</note>.

<summary>This chapter recounts how Queen Semiramis disguised herself as her young son to maintain control of the Assyrian Empire after the death of her husband, King Ninus, proving her capability as a ruler regardless of her gender.</summary>
<keywords>Semiramis, Assyria, King Ninus, Ninyas, gender disguise, ancient history, leadership, Neptune, Bactrians</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Queen Semiramis, following her rise to power through the clever deception of disguising herself as her son to secure the Assyrian throne.</meta>

.v.

This act, which brought so much wonder to those observing it, greatly increased the renowned majesty of the woman <note>Semiramis, the legendary Queen of Assyria</note>. To bring her deeds further into the light: after her remarkable deception <note>original: "insigne figmentum"; this refers to her disguise as her son, Ninyas, which allowed her to rule in her own right</note>, she protected the empire her husband had acquired with a manly spirit and by force of arms. Not only did she hold what was already won, but she also added Ethiopia to her realms after provoking and defeating them in a fierce war. From there, she turned her powerful armies against the Indians, whom no one except her husband had ever approached before.

Furthermore, she restored Babylon—originally the ancient work of Nimrod <note>the biblical figure often credited with building the Tower of Babel</note>—which was a thriving city in those days on the plains of Shinar <note>original: "Senaar"; the geographical region of Mesopotamia</note>. She surrounded it with walls made of baked brick / sand / pitch and bitumen <term>bitumen: a naturally occurring tar-like substance used in antiquity as a powerful adhesive or mortar</term> all bound together; these walls were a marvel for their height, thickness, and vast circumference.

And to highlight one event from the multitude of her deeds most worthy of memory: historians assert as a certainty that once her affairs were settled and she was resting in leisure / on a certain day while she was busy with feminine skills alongside her handmaidens <note>the text breaks off here; the story usually continues with her rushing to suppress a revolt while her hair was only half-braided</note>

<summary>This page details the military expansions of Queen Semiramis into Ethiopia and India, as well as her massive architectural project to rebuild the walls of Babylon using brick and bitumen.</summary>
<keywords>Semiramis, Assyria, Babylon, Nimrod, Ethiopia, Ancient India, Architecture, Bitumen, Shinar</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
The obscene woman defiled herself with allurements. For since, among other things—as if the unhappy woman burned with a constant itching of lust—it was believed she had joined herself in the beds of many: and among her adulterers (something more bestial than human) her son Ninyas is numbered, a youth of most excellent form. He, as if he had exchanged sex with his mother, wasted away in the leisure of the bedchambers while she sweated in armor against enemies. O wicked crime! (to leave quiet matters aside) among the anxious cares of kings, among bloody struggles, and—what is like a monster—among tears and exiles, this plague flies out with no distinction of time being made. Gradually occupying heedless minds and dragging them into a precipice, it stains every honor with a mark of shame.

Having been thus defiled, and while Semiramis thought to use cunning to abolish what she had despoiled through lewdness, they say she issued that famous law by which it was granted to her subjects that they might do whatever they pleased regarding sexual matters <note>This refers to the legendary "Law of Semiramis" which supposedly legalized incest or general licentiousness to justify her own behavior.</note>. Fearing also that she might be cheated of her son's bed by the household women, she (as some would have it) first devised the use of breeches <note>original: "femoralium" — these were thigh-coverings or trousers, often cited in medieval texts as an invention of Semiramis to hide her identity or control the harem</note>, with which she bound all the courtly women under lock and key, which as it is told is still observed among the Egyptians and Assyrians.

Others however write that when she had fallen into desire for her son, and had summoned him, now advanced in age, into her embraces, she was killed by him after she had reigned for thirty-two years. From this others dissent, asserting that she mixed cruelty with her lust, and was accustomed to order those she had called to fulfill the vow of her burning desire to be killed immediately after intercourse so that the crime might be hidden. Truly, when she had conceived at some time, she revealed her adulteries by the birth of a child: to excuse which, they say she brought forth that extraordinary law of which mention was made a little while ago. Nevertheless, even if it seemed that such a foolish crime touched him only slightly, it could by no means take away the son’s indignation; whether because he saw the incest (which he thought was his alone) shared with others and bore it with an ill will, or because he considered his mother's luxury to be his own shame, or perhaps because he dreaded a child about to be born for the succession of the empire, he—driven by anger—did away with the seductive Queen.

.iv.

...placed in a temple as if she were a sublime deity and a savior of the state. For many centuries, she was worshiped with numerous ceremonies among the Romans and us Italians. Truly, it is a marvelous mockery of fortune—or rather, the blindness of men, or perhaps better called the fraud and snare of demons. By their power, it was brought about that a woman, worn down by long hardships, who eventually died as an old woman and was turned to ash and bound in the underworld, should be believed to be a goddess. Almost the entire world honored her with divine rituals as if she were the source of life throughout the ages <note>The author is likely using a "Euhemerist" argument here—the historical theory that the gods of mythology were actually real people who were later deified by their followers.</note>.

### Concerning Juno, Goddess of Kingdoms — Chapter Four

[Woodcut illustration showing three figures: a woman on the left, a crowned woman in the center labeled "**Juno**", and a man on the right. They are standing in front of architectural structures. Above them are a crescent moon, stars, and a sun.]

**Juno**

**Juno**, the daughter of Saturn and Ops <note>Ops was an ancient Roman goddess of the earth and fertility, the wife of Saturn.</note>, was made most famous throughout the whole world—more than all other women stained by the blight of <term>heathenism</term> <note>original: "gentilitatis"; a term used by Christian writers to refer to non-Abrahamic, polytheistic religions</note>—through the songs of poets and the errors of the pagans. This fame is so great that the silent teeth of time, though they gnaw away at all things, have not been able to consume such a notorious legacy; her name has not failed to remain well-known even to our own day. However, in her case, we can recount a remarkable stroke of fortune rather than record any deed truly worth remembering. For she was brought forth in the same birth as that Cretan Jupiter <note>original: "Iove illo Cretense"; refers to the myth that Jupiter (Zeus) was born on the island of Crete.</note>, whom the deceived ancients fashioned into the god of heaven; and from her infancy...

<summary>This page concludes a critical reflection on how mortal women were falsely elevated to goddesses by ancient peoples and begins a biographical account of the goddess Juno, emphasizing her parentage and her legendary status despite the author's skepticism of her "divinity."</summary>
<keywords>Juno, Saturn, Ops, Jupiter, Crete, Roman religion, paganism, Euhemerism, idolatry, mythology</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the goddess Juno, moving from her upbringing on Samos to her marriage with Jupiter and her eventual deification by the ancients.</meta>

### VII

She was sent to Samos, and there she was raised with great care until she reached womanhood. Finally, she was married to her brother Jove <note>original: "ioui" — Jupiter, the king of the gods in Roman mythology</note>, a union that was commemorated for many centuries by a statue that remains in the temple of Samos. Because the Samians <note>The inhabitants of the island of Samos</note> believed it brought no small glory to themselves and their descendants that the noble and betrothed Juno—whom they considered the queen of heaven and a goddess—had lived among them, they took care that this memory should not easily fade. They constructed a massive temple, more marvelous than almost any other in the world, and dedicated it to their deity. Within this temple, they placed an image of her sculpted from Parian marble <note>A high-quality, fine-grained white marble highly prized in antiquity</note>, depicting her in the attire of a bride. After she finally married the great king <note>Jupiter</note>, and as his power grew daily and his fame spread his name far and wide, she herself attained a great deal of splendor.

Indeed, after she was transformed from a mortal queen into a queen of heaven through poetic fictions and the "insane generosity" of the ancients, they endowed her with the kingdoms and riches of Olympus. They also entrusted to her the laws of marriage and the protection of those in labor, along with many other attributes that are more ridiculous than believable. Because of these myths (and at the prompting of the enemy of the human race <note>A common medieval reference to the Devil, suggesting that pagan worship was a result of demonic deception</note>), many temples were built for her everywhere, including primary altars, priests, games, and sacred rites established according to ancient custom. 

To say nothing of other places, after her time in Samos, she was long honored with famous devotion by the people of Argos in Achaia <note>A region in Greece</note> and by the Carthaginians. Finally, she was brought from these cities to Rome and placed on the Capitoline Hill in the inner chamber of Jupiter "Best and Greatest." There, she was positioned just like a wife joined to her husband, under the title of "Juno the Queen" by the Romans, the masters of the world. She was worshipped with many ceremonies for a long time, even after God appeared as a man on earth <note>A reference to the birth of Christ, marking the transition from the pagan to the Christian era</note>.

iv

[Woodcut illustration depicting agricultural activities. On the left, a crowned figure labeled "Ceres" stands holding a scepter. In the foreground, a man plows a field with a pair of oxen. In the background, figures are shown harvesting grain and carrying sacks into a building.]

Ceres, as it pleases some <note>original: "nōnullis"</note> to believe, was the most ancient queen of the Sicilians. She possessed such great intelligence that when she had devised the cultivation of the fields, she was the first among her people to tame oxen and accustom them to the yoke. Having invented the plow and the <term>plowshare<gloss>the cutting blade of a plow</gloss></term>, she cleaved the earth with their help and entrusted the seeds to the furrows.

When these had grown into a very bountiful harvest, she taught men—who were then accustomed to eating acorns and wild fruits—how to remove the grain from the stalks, grind it with stones, prepare it with <term>leaven<gloss>a substance like yeast used to make dough rise</gloss></term>, and turn it into food. Because of this merit, although she was a mortal woman, they considered her the goddess of crops <note>In Roman mythology, Ceres is the goddess of agriculture, grain crops, fertility, and motherly relationships.</note> and exalted her with divine honors; they believed her to be the daughter of Saturn and Cybele. 

They say, moreover, that she had an only daughter, Proserpina, fathered by her brother Jove <note>Jupiter</note>. They claim she was abducted by Orcus, the king of the Molossians <note>The author here uses "Euhemerism," a method of interpretation which treats mythological gods as actual historical kings or people whose stories were exaggerated. Orcus is traditionally the god of the underworld.</note>, causing her mother the greatest distress; Ceres searched for her for a long time, giving rise to many fables. There was, furthermore, another Ceres at Eleusis, a city in the region of Attica, famous among her own people for the same merits. They wish to believe that <term>Triptolemus<gloss>a figure associated with the Eleusinian Mysteries who spread the knowledge of agriculture</gloss></term> was obedient to her, because antiquity treated her equally with deity and honors...

<summary>This page describes the origins of the goddess Ceres, rationalizing her as an ancient Sicilian queen who revolutionized human civilization by inventing the plow and bread-making. It also mentions the abduction of her daughter Proserpina and her association with the city of Eleusis.</summary>
<keywords>Ceres, agriculture, invention of the plow, Proserpina, Orcus, Eleusis, Triptolemus, Sicilian history, mythology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the transition of humanity from a primitive state to a civilized one, likely following a description of legendary figures or goddesses who introduced agriculture.</meta>

->.viii.<-

...exalted; it seemed sufficient to have reported the natures of both under a single name only. By Pollux <note>original: "edepol"; an ancient oath used to emphasize a point, similar to saying "Truly" or "By heaven"</note>, I know not whether to praise or to curse the character of these women. 

For who would condemn men, once wandering and wild, for being led from the forests into cities? Who would condemn those living like beasts for being called to a better harvest? Who would condemn the exchange of acorns for grain? By these means, the body is made brighter, the limbs more vigorous, and food more suited to human use is provided. Who would condemn the transformation of a world once covered in moss, briars, and tangled thickets into one of beauty and public utility? Who would condemn the change from a crude age to a civilized one, or the awakening of minds from sluggishness into contemplation? 

Who would condemn men, once sluggish in caves, being drawn toward the industry of the city or the farm? Through these efforts, so many cities have been expanded, so many built anew, so many empires increased; so many admirable customs have been discovered and cultivated, and the knowledge of the art of grain-growing <term>frumentarie artis</term> <note>The Roman "frumentaria" referred to the complex management and distribution of grain supplies</note> has been devised. Since all these things are good in themselves, I believe a person would be most foolish to judge them otherwise. 

Yet, on the other hand, who might praise the scattered multitude dwelling in the woods, accustomed to acorns, wild fruit, the milk of wild animals, herbs, and running water? Their hearts were free from care, content with only the law of nature, sober, modest, and knowing no deceit, an enemy only to wild beasts and birds. Now they have been called toward softer and unfamiliar foods, from which (unless we deceive ourselves) we see that a path was opened for vices—vices that were previously hidden and lurking, fearing to emerge—and security was provided for them to advance. 

From this point, fields that were once communal began to be marked off by boundaries and ditches; the cares of the farmers arose, and labors began to be divided among mortals. From this came the concepts of "mine" and "thine" <note>original: "meum et tuum"; a philosophical shorthand for the invention of private property</note>—terms truly hostile to peace, both public and private. From this followed poverty and theft, as well as lawsuits, hatreds, and bloody wars, and...

<summary>The author weighs the benefits of civilization and agriculture against the lost innocence of the primitive "state of nature," concluding that progress brought about the concept of private property and subsequent social conflict.</summary>
<keywords>civilization, agriculture, state of nature, private property, meum et tuum, social evolution, ethics of progress</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of modern civilization, contrasting the perceived decadence of the "Iron Age" with the simplicity of the "Golden Age."</meta>

.4.

From this, the seas were plowed, and the Eastern lands became known to the West, and the Western to the East. From this came the softening of bodies, the stuffing of the belly, the decoration of garments, and more elaborate tables; all things splendid for the body and for leisure arrived. And that which had remained cold until those days, <term>Venus<note>original: "Venus"; used here to represent carnal desire or lust.</note></term> began to grow warm, to the greatest disadvantage of the world. And (what is perhaps worse) if, as the years glide by (as often happens), the cultivated fields do not respond due to neglect or the rage of wars, there immediately enters a scarcity of provisions <note>original: "anonae"; the state-managed grain supply or harvest.</note>. Then, harsher fasts arise for the common people; cruel hunger, a gluttony never known in the wild woods, enters the homes of the poor, which is not without danger to the multitude of the wealthy. 

From this arise foul and exhausted thinness, a hellish paleness, and weakness walking with a faltering step; thus, manifold causes of disease and premature death emerge. Having considered these things along with countless others, I hardly know—or rather, I know well—that those long-ago golden ages, though they were rough and rustic, are far to be preferred to these iron ages of ours and all others.

## Concerning Minerva, who is also called Pallas. Chapter Six.

[A woodcut illustration depicting Pallas Athena (Minerva). To the right, two women are shown near a tree, one standing and one seated, engaged in textile work like spinning or weaving.]

<keywords>Golden Age, Iron Age, decadence, famine, Minerva, Pallas Athena, textile arts, social decay, Venus, gluttony</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
.viii.

**Minerva**, who is also called Pallas, was seen with such great brilliance that foolish men did not believe she possessed a mortal origin. Some say that in the time of King Ogyges, she was first seen and known on earth near Lake Tritonis, not far from the Lesser Syrtis <note>The "Lesser Syrtis" refers to the Gulf of Gabès off the coast of Tunisia.</note>. Because over time people saw her doing many things never seen before—not only among the unrefined Africans but even among the Greeks, who at that time surpassed all others in wisdom—it was believed she was born without a mother from the brain of Jupiter and sent down from heaven. This ridiculous error gained more credit the more hidden her true origin remained. 

Above all else, people wished her to have flourished in perpetual virginity. To make this more believable, they invented the story that Vulcan, the god of fire—by which they mean the heat of carnal desire—struggled with her for a long time and was overcome <note>The author is using "Euhemerism," a historical method that treats myths as exaggerated accounts of real people and interprets gods as symbols for natural forces or human traits.</note>. 

Furthermore, they claim that the craft of wool-working, previously unknown to everyone, was invented by her. For having shown the order in which wool is purged of impurities, softened by iron teeth <note>A reference to "carding" wool with metal combs.</note>, placed upon the distaff, and finally drawn into thread by the fingers, she devised the art of weaving. She taught the method by which threads are intertwined, joined by the stroke of the comb, and how the woven fabric is made firm by the treadle <note>original: *calce*, literally "the heel." This refers to the foot-operated part of a loom.</note>. In praise of this craftsmanship, that famous contest between her and Arachne of Colophon is often recounted. 

Moreover, she discovered the use of oil, which was unheard of by mortals until then; she taught the Athenians to grind the olives <note>original: *bacchas*, literally "berries."</note> in a mill and press them in oil-presses. Because this was seen to bring such great utility, it is believed she was granted victory against Neptune in the naming of Athens after herself. 

They also claim it was her work—having already been the first to find a use for four-horse chariots—to skillfully convert iron into weapons, cover the body with armor, organize the line of battle, and establish all the laws by which men are trained for combat. They say, besides, that she invented numbers and organized them into the system which we still use today. Furthermore, from the leg bone of a bird, or rather from a marsh reed, she [fashioned] flutes or pastoral...

<summary>This page recounts the legendary origins and many inventions of the goddess Minerva (Pallas), interpreting her not as a literal deity but as a brilliant mortal woman who brought weaving, olive oil, armor, and mathematics to the ancient world.</summary>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the goddess Minerva, concluding the discussion of her symbolic attributes and the spread of her worship before transitioning to the life of Venus.</meta>

...they believed she was the first to compose music for the flutes, and that she cast them down from heaven to the earth because they made the blower's throat swell and deformed the face. <note>original: "turgidum guttur & ora deformia." This refers to the myth where Minerva (Athena) invented the aulos (double flute) but discarded it after seeing her distorted reflection in a stream.</note> Why say more? Because of so many discovered wonders, the antiquity that was so generous in creating deities attributed to her the very power of wisdom. Driven by this view, the Athenians—who were named after her—took her as their guardian because their city seemed so well-suited for the studies through which everyone becomes prudent and wise. They dedicated their citadel to her and, having constructed a vast temple consecrated to her divinity, they fashioned her image within it.

They depicted her with **stern eyes**, because it is rarely known toward what end the intention of a wise person aims. They chose to show her **helmeted** to signify that the plans of the wise are protected and shielded. She was shown **clothed in a breastplate**, because a wise person is always armed against whatever blows fortune might strike. She was also armed with a **very long spear**, to show that it is understood that the wise man fixes his sights on distant goals. Furthermore, she was protected by a **crystalline aegis** <note>The Aegis was a magical shield or animal-skin cloak associated with Zeus and Athena.</note> with the **head of the Gorgon** <note>Medusa, the snake-haired monster whose gaze turned onlookers to stone.</note> fixed upon it. From this, they understood that for the wise, all defenses are clear and transparent, and that they are always so well-fortified by serpentine cunning that the ignorant seem like stone when caught in their gaze.

As her guardian animal, they chose the **owl**, affirming that just as the prudent see in the light, they also see in the darkness. Eventually, the fame of this woman and the reverence for her divinity spread so far and wide—and the errors of the ancients favored her so much—that temples were built and sacred rites were celebrated in her honor almost throughout the entire world. Her status rose so high that on the Capitoline Hill, near Jupiter Greatest and Best, a shrine was dedicated to her so that she might be held as an equal among the most powerful Roman gods, alongside Queen Juno. However, there are some very serious men who assert that these deeds were not achieved by one Minerva, but by several different women; I willingly agree with this, so that the number of famous women may be even greater.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of famous women, transitioning from the early mythological figures of antiquity to the complex figure of Venus. The author, Giovanni Boccaccio, treats her not as a literal goddess, but as a historical woman whose immense beauty led to her deification.</meta>

50

->[Woodcut illustration depicting Venus standing with a scepter and crown, Cupid with a bow, Vulcan at an anvil, and Mars lying in a bed with a female figure. Labels within the woodcut: **Venus**, **Vulcan**, **Mars**, **Cupid**.]<-

**V**enus the Cyprian was a woman, according to the opinion of some. However, there is some doubt regarding her parents. For some wish her to be the daughter of a certain **Cyprian** <note>original: "cpri", referring to a king of Cyprus</note> and **Syria**. Yet others say she was born to **Cyprian** and **Dione**, a woman of Cyprus. Some (I believe) to exalt the fame of her beauty, assert she was the daughter of **Jove** <note>Jupiter</note> and the aforementioned **Dione**. Certainly, regardless of which father she was born to, I have decided that she should be described among famous women more for her illustrious beauty than for her honorable merit. She shone with such beauty of face and charm of her whole body that the belief of those who looked upon her was often deceived. For some said she was that very star of heaven which we call **Venus**; others that she was a heavenly woman who had fallen to earth from the lap of **Jove**. And in short, everyone—obscured by a foul darkness—asserted she was an immortal goddess, even though they knew she was born of a mortal woman. They affirmed with all their might that she was the mother of that ill-fated love <term>Cupid<note>The Roman personification of desire; Boccaccio distinguishes between the human woman and the mythological personification of lust.</note></term>. Nor did she lack the arts to ensnare the minds of foolish onlookers through various gestures. By which...

<summary>This page introduces Venus as a historical woman of Cyprus whose extraordinary beauty and charms led her contemporaries to mistakenly worship her as an immortal goddess and the mother of Cupid.</summary>
<keywords>Venus, Cyprus, Cupid, Jove, Dione, Euhemerism, Greco-Roman mythology, beauty vs. virtue, deification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the historical origins of pagan deities, specifically focusing on the mortal woman behind the myth of Venus. The narrative follows an "Euhemeristic" approach, treating mythological figures as actual historical people whose lives were later embellished into godhood.</meta>

...through her merits, things reached such a point that, because they could not resist the woman’s obscenities—not all of which I am prepared to write down here—she came to be regarded as the daughter of Jupiter and one of the most venerable goddesses. 

And it was not only at Paphos, the most ancient town of the Cypriots, that she was appeased with incense alone; for it was believed that this dead and incestuous woman delighted in that fragrance, she who while living had taken pleasure in the filth of brothels <note>original: *pstibuloꝝ* or *prostibulorum*, referring to houses of prostitution</note>. 

This was true not only there, but also among other nations and the Romans, who once raised a temple to her under the titles of **Venus the Mother** <note>original: *veneris genitricis*</note> and **Changer of Hearts** <note>original: *verticorde*, a reference to Venus Verticordia, who was believed to turn women's hearts toward chastity</note> along with other honors. 

But why say more? It is believed that she married two men. It is not quite certain which one was the first. Some prefer the idea that she first married Vulcan, the king of the Lemnians and the son of the Cretan Jupiter. After he was removed <note>meaning after his death</note>, she married Adonis, the son of Cinyras and the king of the Cypriots. This seems more likely to me than saying Adonis was her first husband, because—whether due to a flaw in her own temperament or the corruption of the region, where lewdness <note>original: *lasciuias*</note> seems to be most powerful...

<summary>This page argues that the goddess Venus was originally a mortal woman of scandalous character who was deified after her death, eventually becoming a major figure in Roman and Cypriot worship. The author attempts to reconcile her mythological marriages to Vulcan and Adonis by placing them in a historical, chronological order.</summary>

## Chapter 8: Concerning Isis, Queen and Goddess of the Egyptians

[A woodcut illustration depicts the mythological scene: **Jupiter** is shown embracing **Io**; nearby, **Mercury** holds his caduceus <note>A staff entwined with snakes, the symbol of Mercury</note> over the sleeping, many-eyed **Argus**. In the distance, a ship is visible with the image of a cow upon its sail.]

Isis, whose name was previously Io, was not only the most famous Queen of the Egyptians, but eventually their most sacred and venerable deity. However, in what times she lived, or from which parents she was born, is debated among illustrious historians. 

There are those who say she was the daughter of Inachus, the first king of the Argives, and the sister of Phoroneus—who, it is established, reigned during the time of Jacob, son of Isaac <note>The author is synchronizing Greek mythology with the biblical chronology of the Patriarchs.</note>. Others assert she was the daughter of Prometheus <note>The Titan known in myth for creating man and stealing fire</note> while Phorbas reigned at Argos, which occurred long after the first-mentioned period. Some affirm she lived in the times of Cecrops, King of Athens, and indeed others say she flourished during the times of Lynceus, King of the Argives. 

These differences among celebrated scholars are not without their reasons. She was outstanding among the women of her age and most worthy of remembrance. But, setting aside the disagreements of writers, my intention is to follow what the majority believe—namely, that she was the daughter of King Inachus—even if the ancient poets might invent stories <note>original: "fingant"</note> about her...

<summary>This page begins the biography of Isis, identifying her with the Greek Io and attempting to reconcile various historical and mythological accounts of her lineage and the era in which she lived.</summary>
<keywords>Isis, Io, Egypt, Inachus, Argives, Greek mythology, Biblical chronology, Jupiter, Mercury</keywords>

that because of the beauty of her form she pleased Jove and was overpowered by him,
and to hide the crime she was transformed into a cow and, upon her

was driven in a rapid course into Egypt; and there she recovered her

...whom mistaken antiquity considered the son of Jupiter and Niobe, the daughter of Phoroneus. <note>original: "Iouis & Niobis phoronei filie"</note> They say that after he yielded the kingdom of Achaia to his brother Aegialeus, and after he had reigned in Argos for thirty-five years, he withdrew into Egypt. # Of Europa, Queen of the Cretans. Chapter .ix.

->[Woodcut illustration: The scene depicts the abduction of Europa. In the center, **Europa** stands near a bull. To her left is a man in traveling clothes. To her right, a crowned figure labeled **Jupiter** stands near a ship. The image suggests the historical interpretation that the "bull" was actually a ship with a bull's figurehead.]<-

Some believe that **Europa** was the daughter of Phoenix; however, far more say she was the child of Agenor, the King of the Phoenicians. It is said she possessed such marvelous beauty that **Jupiter**, a powerful man of Crete, was seized by love for her before even seeing her. When he was plotting his schemes for her abduction, it is claimed he used the seductive flattery of a certain messenger's words. Thus, the playful maiden was lured from the mountains down to the Phoenician shore, following her father’s herds. From there, she was immediately snatched away and placed upon a ship which bore the image of a **white bull** <term>insigne</term><note>A figurehead or standard used to identify a ship.</note> as its emblem, and was carried off to Crete. To wander with excessive freedom and to lend a ready ear to the sweet words of any stranger is a habit least to be praised in young women, as one may often read of the many misfortunes that have followed such behavior.

<summary>This page concludes the account of Apis, identifying him with the Egyptian Osiris, and begins the story of Europa, interpreting her abduction not as a divine miracle but as a historical kidnapping involving a ship named "The White Bull."</summary>
<keywords>Apis, Osiris, Isis, Europa, Jupiter, Phoenicia, Crete, abduction, Agenor, ship emblems, moral conduct for women</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Europa from the previous page, focusing on the historical rationalization of her myth and the naming of the continent, before moving to Chapter 10 regarding Queen Libya.</meta>

...often disgraceful marks are imprinted on the honorable by those acting <note>original: "bis agentibus"; likely referring to those who repeat or instigate such acts</note> shamefully, which even the beauty of subsequent chastity could not later wash away. From these events, it is clearly evident that the fable was derived in which it is read that Mercury drove the Phoenician herds to the shore, and Jupiter—having turned into a bull and swimming to Crete—carried off the maiden Europa. However, the ancients disagree on the time of this abduction. For those who place it earlier want it to have happened while Danaus ruled at Argos; others while Acrisius ruled; and those who are most recent say it was when King Pandion was ruling the Athenians, which seems to better suit the times of Minos, the son of Europa. 

Some simply believe she was forced by Jupiter and then married Asterius, King of the Cretans, and from him bore sons Minos, Rhadamanthus, and Sarpedon, whom most say were the sons of Jupiter. Some even assert that Asterius and Jupiter were the same person. While this dispute concerns others, many wish Europa to be considered famous for her marriage to such a great god. Some further affirm—either because she was of extraordinary nobility, or because through the <term>Phoenicians</term><note>An ancient seafaring civilization located in the Levant, known for their trade and the spread of the alphabet.</note>, by many merits, she shone more than others in her time with the distinctions of her ancestors, or out of veneration for her divine husband, or by the favor of her royal sons, or by the exceptional virtue of Europa herself—that the third part of the world was forever named <term>Europe</term> after her name. Indeed, I judge her to be a woman distinguished for her virtues, not only because of the name granted to the world, but from the remarkable bronze statue dedicated to the name of Europa by Pythagoras, the illustrious philosopher of Tarentum <note>Pythagoras of Rhegium was a famous sculptor; the author here may be conflating him with the philosopher or a specific Tarentine tradition.</note>.

### Of Libya, Queen of Libya. Chapter Ten

Libya, as the most ancient authors maintain, was the daughter of Epaphus, King of the Egyptians, by his wife Cassiopeia. She married <term>Neptune</term>—that is, a foreign and powerful man <note>The author uses "Euhemerism," a method of interpretation which treats mythological gods as historical kings or heroes who were later deified.</note>. His proper name has not come down to us, and by him she bore the monstrous Busiris, who was later the tyrant of Upper Egypt. His magnificent works are believed to have been consumed by the passing years, but that she was a woman of great significance is sufficiently proven by this argument: that she held such authority among her people that the part of Africa which she ruled was all called **Libya** after her name.

<summary>This page rationalizes the myth of Europa's abduction as a historical event involving a king and discusses why the continent of Europe bears her name, then introduces Queen Libya as the namesake of the African region.</summary>
<keywords>Europa, Jupiter, Mercury, Minos, Crete, Phoenicia, Pythagoras of Tarentum, Libya, Epaphus, Busiris, Etymology, Euhemerism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the origins of the Amazon nation, following the slaughter of their husbands by neighboring tribes and their subsequent decision to take up arms for survival and vengeance.</meta>

...to be sufficient for wars and arms, so that they did not seem to have had 
kinder gods <note>original: "mitiores deos"</note> than the others. From these women, fortune had saved those whose husbands were lost to the slaughter of their neighbors. 
They rushed upon them all with total resolve, 
and then, with their fury turned against the enemy, as if the deaths of their husbands were their own life, 
they crushed them so thoroughly 
that they easily obtained peace from them. 
Once peace was secured, in order to ensure a succession, they took turns 
associating with their neighbors, and as soon as they had conceived, 
they would immediately return to their own lands. Eventually, any males who were born 
were killed on the spot; the young girls were raised with great care 
for military service. While they were still tender, through fire or some other medicine, 
the growth of the right breast was removed 
so that it would not hinder the use of the bow; in the adults, the left breast was left 
intact, so that they might provide nourishment 
to future infants. From this, they were given the name of <term>Amazons</term> <note>The author derives "Amazon" from the Greek 'a-' (without) and 'mazos' (breast), a common but debated folk etymology in antiquity.</note>. 
Nor was there the same care in nourishing their young girls as there is in our own customs; 
for, having cast aside the spinning distaff, wool-baskets, and other womanly 
duties, they hardened the older girls through hunting, 
scouting, the taming of horses, hard labors, constant practice with

Indeed, so that their strength would not lack leadership, they appointed

they greatly expanded their empire. These women, being distinguished in 
military discipline, divided the duties between them, 
so that while one remained at home for the protection of the kingdom, the other, 
taking a portion of the forces, focused on subjugating neighbors to their 
rule; and thus, by turns, through the greatest spoils acquired, 
they grew their republic for some time.

### On Thisbe, the Babylonian Maiden. Chapter Twelve

-> [A woodcut illustration depicts the story of Pyramus and Thisbe. A woman labeled **Thisbe** stands with her arms raised in distress over a man labeled **Pyramus** who lies on the ground. A lion is visible in the background near a fountain and a tower.] <-

Thisbe, a Babylonian maiden, became famous among mortals more for the outcome of her unhappy love than for any other deed. Although we have not received word from our ancestors as to who her parents were, it is well enough believed that she lived in Babylon in a house adjoining that of **Pyramus**, a boy of her own age. Because they were constant companions by right of being neighbors, a childhood affection developed while they were still young. Unkind fate decreed that as their years increased (and as both were remarkably beautiful), this childhood love grew into a great fire. They would occasionally reveal this to one another through nods and signs as they approached the age of maturity.

Indeed, as Thisbe grew into a young woman, her parents began to keep her at home in preparation for a future marriage <term>hymeneos (the Greek and Roman god of marriage, here referring to wedding rites)</term>. Since both of them bore this separation with great difficulty, they anxiously searched for a way they might at least speak to one another. Their common devotion eventually discovered a way...

<summary>This page concludes the story of the Amazon Queen Lampedo and introduces the tragic legend of Pyramus and Thisbe, describing their childhood love in Babylon and their eventual separation by their parents.</summary>
<keywords>Thisbe, Pyramus, Babylon, Lampedo, Amazons, tragic love, forbidden romance</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
...in a secluded spot, a small crack <note>original Latin: "rimulam"</note> through which they met in secret more often <note>The OCR reads "se pius," which is a transcription error for "saepius" (more often).</note>. By the habit of speaking for short periods—and with their sense of devotion acting as a barrier <note>original: "pietate etiam obice"; this suggests that their shared values or family loyalty paradoxically made them feel less ashamed of their growing intimacy</note> which allowed them to blush less—they had increased the liberty they took in expressing their affections. They often shared all their sighs, tears, burning desires, and passions; sometimes they even prayed for the peace of each other's souls, longing for embraces, kisses, devotion, faith, and eternal love.

Finally, as the fire of their passion grew, they devised a plan to flee. They decided that on the following night, as soon as each could deceive their guardians, they would leave their homes. Whichever of them escaped first would go into the woods closest to the city and wait for the other by the fountain near the tomb <term>bustum: a funeral monument or burial mound</term> of King Ninus <note>Ninus was the legendary founder of Nineveh and husband of Semiramis; his tomb is the traditional meeting place in the myth of Pyramus and Thisbe.</note>. 

Thisbe, being perhaps the more burning of the two, was the first to deceive her family. Wrapped in a cloak, she left her father’s house alone in the dead of night. With the moon showing the way, she went fearlessly into the forest. # On Hypermnestra, Queen of the Argives and Priestess of Jupiter. Chapter 13.

->[Woodcut illustration showing a dormitory with several beds. In the foreground, a woman leads a man out of a doorway on the right. Labels: "Hypermnestra", "Lynceus"]<-
<note>The original woodcut labels the man as "Linus," a common medieval variant for Lynceus.</note>

**Hypermnestra** was famous for her lineage and dignity; she was the royal daughter of Danaus of the <term>Argives</term><note>The people of Argos, a powerful city-state in ancient Greece.</note> and the wife of Lynceus. It is gathered from the histories of the ancients that there were once two brothers in Egypt, the illustrious sons of Belus, who were preeminent in their remarkable power. One of these was named Danaus, and the other was called Aegyptus. 

The fortune of their offspring was not equal for both, even though their number was the same. For Danaus had fifty daughters, and Aegyptus had just as many sons. Indeed, since Danaus had received an <term>oracle</term><note>A prophecy or message believed to come from the gods.</note> stating that he would be killed by the hand of a nephew <note>original: "manu nepotis"; in this context, the son of his brother.</note> born of his brother, he was secretly tormented by a terrible fear. Because of such a vast multitude of nephews, he did not know whose hands he ought to suspect. 

It happened that as all the children reached puberty, Aegyptus requested that all the daughters of Danaus be joined in marriage to his sons. Danaus, having devised a cruel and wicked plan, granted this of his own accord. Once the daughters were betrothed to his nephews and the marriage rites were being prepared, he warned them all most urgently that if they wished for their own safety, each one must [kill] her husband on the first night, while he was soaked with wine and the feast, and bound by sleep...

<summary>This page introduces Hypermnestra, daughter of King Danaus, and sets the stage for the myth where Danaus orders his fifty daughters to murder their husbands (their cousins) on their wedding night to avert a prophecy.</summary>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>Continuing the account of Danaus and his fifty daughters. The previous page likely described the conflict between Danaus and his brother Aegyptus, leading to the forced marriage of their children.</meta>

And how much of a detestable enormity this was to become—leaving behind a ruinous example for ill-fated women—he caused the faith of marriage to be trampled by treachery. Where a pious parent ought to have ordered sacred torches <note>The "faces" or wedding torches were a standard symbol of a Roman or Greek marriage ceremony</note> to be brought into the bedchambers, he commanded wicked swords instead. Where we are accustomed to encouraging daughters toward conjugal love, he incited them to hatred and certainly to slaughter. Indeed, what he would not have dared against the group as a whole, he set his daughters upon each man individually. What he would not have attempted by day, he wished to be perfected by night; what he would not have presumed to do in a military camp, he ordered to be fulfilled in the bedchambers. He did not realize that for every year of vigorous youth he stole from his nephews <note>original: "nepotum"; the sons of his brother Aegyptus</note> through crime and fraud, he was reserving for himself as many ages of his own defiled and ignominious deed. And he who by right could have had fifty sons-in-law was left with only one, an enemy who was spared to his own detriment. From whose hands at last (by the willing judgment of a just God) the savage old man could not escape; indeed, that harmful blood was eventually poured out—the very life he had sought to buy with the blood of so many young men.

Whether driven out, a fugitive, or summoned, he at length crossed the sea into Greece and held the kingdom of the Argives, occupied by his wits and by legal claims. There are those who would have it that the aforementioned crime was perpetrated by Danaus <note>The author notes a variation in the mythological tradition regarding when or where the murders took place</note>; but however it happened, he fell in death, leaving an eternal memory of his savagery. In his place, Linus <note>Commonly known as Lynceus in Greek mythology; the only husband spared</note> himself reigned over the Argives. Having led Hypermestra <note>original: "perpermestra"</note> out of prison, he joined her to himself in a marriage of better omen and made her a leader of the kingdom. She not only shone as queen, but having been made a priestess of Argive Juno, she appeared adorned with the brilliance of a double splendor <note>The "double splendor" refers to her dual status as a secular ruler and a high-ranking religious official</note>. And while her sisters descended into shameful infamy, she, on account of her commendable devotion <term>pietatem: a Roman virtue signifying duty and loyalty to family, gods, and country</term>, sent down to us her name, distinguished and worthy of praise.

# Concerning Niobe, Queen of the Thebans. Chapter 14.

<meta>This page begins the biography of Niobe, following the previous accounts of famous women of antiquity. It establishes her noble lineage and the source of her tragic pride.</meta>

.XVII.

[Woodcut illustration of a woman and a man standing over several dead children. Above the woman is the name **Niobe** and above the man is **Amphion**. A sun with a face and rays is positioned in the center above the children.]

**Niobe** is widely known among the most distinguished women. As the daughter of Tantalus, the most ancient and famous king of Phrygia, and sister to Pelops, she married **Amphion**, King of the **Thebans**, who was highly celebrated at that time. This was both because he was the offspring of Jupiter <note>original: "iouis ples"; in mythology, Amphion was a son of Zeus/Jupiter</note> and because he was particularly gifted in eloquence <term>facundia</term> <note>Classical rhetoric and the power of persuasive speech</note>. From him, while the glory of the kingdom endured, she bore seven sons and as many daughters. Truly, what ought to have benefited a wise woman was the cause of destruction for a proud one. For, puffed up by the brilliance of her numerous offspring and the fame of her ancestors, she even dared to speak ill against the gods.

Indeed, at the command of a certain **Manto**, daughter of the prophet **Tiresias**, the Thebans were occupied with holy days and the sacred rites of **Latona** <note>The Roman name for the Greek goddess Leto</note>, mother of **Apollo** and **Diana**—deities to be worshipped according to ancient superstition. Then, as if driven by the Furies, Niobe leaped into their midst. She was surrounded by the ranks of her children and adorned with royal insignia. She cried out, asking what madness possessed the Thebans to perform sacrifices to Latona—a foreign woman born of the Titan Coeus, who had brought forth only two children conceived in adultery—and to prefer her to Niobe, who was of royal descent from King Tantalus.
