...that she had born fourteen <note>Original: *xiiij*</note> children from her husband before their very eyes,

from the avenging deities <note>original: *numinum*</note> for her insolence. Niobe,
however, surviving so many funerals, a mournful widow,
fell into such a great and obstinate silence that she
seemed more like an unmoving rock than a woman.

and unbearable, since nature has for the most part produced 
the former <note>men</note> with a burning and elevated spirit;

mild virtue, suited for elegance...

.xviii.

[Woodcut illustration depicting several figures. Two women are attacking a man on the ground with a sword. To the right, a man and a woman stand together. Labels in the woodcut: "Thoas", "Hypsipyle" <note>original: "Ypsiphiles"</note>]

Hypsipyle was a distinguished woman, as much for her <term>piety<note>The Latin *pietas* refers to "filial duty"—the deep respect and devotion a child owes to a parent.</note></term> toward her father as for her unhappy exile, the death of her foster-child Archemorus, and the timely help she received from her sons. She was the daughter of Thoas, king of the Lemnians, who reigned at the time when a certain madness seized the minds of the women there, driving them to withdraw their untamed necks from the yoke of men entirely. For, having scorned their duty to the old king, and with Hypsipyle <insert>seemingly</insert> joined with them, they came to a unanimous decision: that on the following night they would rage with swords against every male. Indeed, the deed was carried out according to their plan. However, while the others were raging, a milder thought occurred to Hypsipyle’s mind.

For, believing it would be inhuman to be defiled by her father’s blood, she revealed the others’ crime to her parent. She sent him away on a ship so that he might escape the public fury by fleeing to Chios. Immediately, she built a huge pyre and pretended to perform the final funeral rites for her father. Since everyone believed her, she was placed on her father’s throne in the king’s place, and was made queen by these wicked women. <margin>AD</margin> Truly, the devotion of children toward their parents is most holy; for what is more fitting? What is more just? What is more praiseworthy than to serve them with humanity and—

...to repay these favors with honor? By whose labor did we, in our weakness, receive nourishment? We are strong in both talent and character because of them. Indeed, nothing is too great <note>original: "nil equidem"</note>, for since Hypsipyle <note>The OCR 'p̄pphile' is a contraction for Hypsipyle, the mythological Queen of Lemnos known for saving her father</note> performed these duties with the care of a parent, she is rightly included among illustrious women.

While she was reigning, Jason—traveling with the Argonauts toward Colchis <note>The land of the Golden Fleece</note>—was brought to her shores, whether driven by the force of the winds or by intent. Although the other women tried in vain to stop them, the Argonauts occupied the shore, and Jason was welcomed by the queen into her home and her bed. 

After Jason's departure, when she had given birth to twin sons in due time, the law of the Lemnian women <note>The women of Lemnos had murdered all their male relatives and forbidden any men to live on the island</note> forced her to send them away. As some authors believe, she ordered them to be taken to the island of Chios to be raised by their grandfather.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of famous women, following the previous accounts of mythological and historical figures. We are now on page 19, beginning the entry for Medea.</meta>

.xix.

[Woodcut illustration depicting Jason and Medea on horseback, with Medea holding a severed head. To the right, a group of men on horseback and a figure on the ground being dismembered. Labels in the woodcut: "Jason", "Medea", and "Absyrtus" (written vertically).]

**M**edea, the most cruel example of ancient perfidy <note>original: "perfidie", meaning treachery or a betrayal of faith</note>, was the daughter of Aeëtes, the most famous king of the Colchians, and his wife Perse. She was beautiful enough, but by far the most learned in <term>sorceries<note>The Latin *maleficium* refers specifically to "dark magic" or skills used to cause harm.</note></term>. For (by whichever master she was instructed) she held the powers of herbs so familiarly that no one knew them better. She fully knew how to disturb the heavens with a chanted song, to summon the winds from their caves, to move storms, to stop the flow of rivers, to fashion poisons, and to compose elaborate fires for any conflagration, performing all such things.

Nor (which was much worse) was her soul discordant with her dark arts; for when those arts failed her, she considered it a very light thing to use the sword. This woman loved Jason the Thessalian—a youth conspicuous for his virtue in that age—who had been sent to Colchis by his uncle Pelias (who was plotting against Jason's integrity under the pretext of a most glorious expedition) to recover the <term>Golden Fleece<note>The fleece of the winged ram Chrysomallos, which Jason was tasked to retrieve to reclaim his inheritance.</note></term>. Having been captured by his excellence, she loved him ardently. She acted to earn his favor so that, once a rebellion had arisen among the inhabitants, a war might first be stirred up, thereby providing Jason the time and space to fulfill his vow. Who, even a sensible man, would have thought that from a single glance of the eyes, the extermination of a most wealthy king would follow? Therefore...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Medea’s flight from Colchis with Jason and her subsequent arrival in Thessaly.</meta>

Having committed the crime, once she had earned the embraces of the beloved young man <note>Jason, leader of the Argonauts</note>, she secretly took flight, taking with her all the wealth of her fatherland. Not content with so great a deed, she turned her cruel mind toward even worse things! She judged that Aeetes <note>Medea's father, the King of Colchis</note> would surely pursue the fugitives. To stop him, at the island of Tomis <note>original: "thomitania"</note> in the Phasis river, through which the pursuer would have to pass, she ordered that her brother Absyrtus (also known as Aegialeus), the boy she had brought with her as a companion in flight, be slaughtered and his limbs scattered everywhere across the fields. 

She did this so that while the wretched father gathered the scattered pieces to give them his tears and a burial mound, it would provide the fugitives time for their escape. Her plan did not fail her, for it happened just so. At last, when after many wanderings she had arrived in Thessaly with her Jason, she filled her father-in-law Aeson with such great joy—both from the return of his son and from the hard-won victory, the plunder, and the illustrious marriage—that he saw himself restored to the bloom of youth <note>According to myth, Medea used her magic to rejuvenate the elderly Aeson by replacing his blood with a herbal potion</note>. Seeking to prepare a kingdom for Jason, she used her dark arts to sow discord <term>zizaniam: literally "tares" or weeds, a metaphor for spreading strife or trouble</term> between Pelias <note>Jason's uncle, who had usurped the throne</note> and his children, and she wretchedly armed them against their father. Furthermore...

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues a moral discourse on the senses, specifically focusing on the dangers of sight and how the eyes can lead the soul astray.</meta>

->.ff.<-
<note>The mark ".ff." often indicates a new section or folio (page) in legal or academic manuscripts.</note>

Through the actions of the eyes, boldness is stirred in everyone: beauty is praised, while squalor and poverty are unworthily condemned. And since the eyes are unlearned judges—believing only in the surfaces of things—they present shameful things as sacred, fictions as truths, and anxieties as joys to the common people. While they recommend things that ought to be rejected and things that flatter for only a short time, they occasionally infect souls with a most foul stain. 

These ignorant people are captured by even dishonest beauty, by lewd gestures, and by the biting vices of youthful insolence; they are dragged, seized, and held fast. And since the eyes are the gateway to the heart, <term>lust</term><note>original: "libido"</note> sends its messengers to the mind through them; through them desire breathes and sighs, kindling blind fires; through them the heart sends forth its groans and displays the allurements of its passions. 

If anyone were truly wise, they would either close their eyes, lift them toward heaven, or cast them down to the earth; there is no safe middle path for them. But if they must be used at all, they must be restrained with a sharp <term>bridle</term><note>A common metaphor in Renaissance and Medieval literature for self-control and temperance.</note> to prevent them from running wild.

<summary>This text warns of the moral dangers posed by the sense of sight, describing the eyes as unreliable judges that allow lust and desire to corrupt the heart. It concludes that the eyes must be strictly disciplined or directed toward heaven to avoid spiritual ruin.</summary>
<keywords>morality, eyes, temptation, lust, beauty, vice, self-control, allegory of the senses</keywords>

## Concerning Arachne, the Woman of Colophon <note>original: "De Aragne colophonia muliere"</note>. Chapter 18

<margin>1</margin> **A**rachne was a woman of Asia and of common birth <note>original: "plebeia"; referring to her status as a non-noble or commoner.</note>. She was the daughter of Idmon of Colophon <note>The OCR reads "idomo in p̄", likely a corruption of "Idmonis", the name of Arachne’s father in classical mythology.</note>, a dyer of wool. Although she was of less than noble origin in her manner of life, she is nonetheless not to be praised for any moral merits <note>The author distinguishes between her technical skill and her character, which in myth was marred by pride.</note>. Certain ancient writers assert that the use of linen <note>original: "lini"</note> was discovered through her industriousness, and that she was the first to devise nets—though whether they were intended for bird-catching <note>original: "aucupatoria"</note> or fishing remains uncertain. And since her son, to whom Clo— <note>The text cuts off here, likely referring to Closter, whom some traditions credit with the invention of the spindle.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Arachne of Colophon, identifying her as the daughter of a humble wool-dyer and crediting her with the invention of linen-working and various types of nets.</summary>
<keywords>Arachne, Colophon, textiles, linen, net-making, inventions, women in antiquity, Idmon</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Arachne—the weaver who challenged a goddess—and transitions to the history of the Amazon queens Orithyia and Antiope. The text continues the moralizing tone regarding human pride and the cultivation of talent.</meta>

.21.

...provides talents to all. Just as these talents may become sluggish through idleness and sloth, they are made bright and capable of the greatest things through study and practice. Driven by this same nature, we are carried by desire toward the knowledge of all things—even if we do not all possess the same cleverness or luck. If this is so, what stops many people from becoming equals in the same pursuit? Therefore, for anyone to believe that they alone, among such a vast multitude of mortals, can outrun all others in the race for glory is the sign of a foolish mind <note>The author is critiquing intellectual hubris, suggesting that no one is so uniquely talented that they cannot be surpassed or equaled</note>. I would truly wish that Arachne <note>original: "aragnes"; in Greek mythology, Arachne was a mortal weaver who challenged the goddess Minerva to a contest and was transformed into a spider as punishment for her pride</note> were our only example of such ridicule; however, there are countless people ensnared in such madness. As they hurl themselves into the abyss of foolish pride, they make Arachne's own story seem less laughable.

**On the Erythraean or Eriphila Sibyl. Chapter XIX.**

[Woodcut illustration: On the left, a woman in a long, patterned dress and elaborate headdress stands with her hands raised. To her left, the text "Erythraean Sibyl" <note>original: "Sibilla erithrea"</note> is written vertically. In the background is a walled city with towers. On the right, a semi-nude figure representing Christ, labeled "Christ" <note>original: "xps," a common Greek-derived abbreviation for Christus</note> above his head, stands with hands raised, accompanied by a smaller figure in a hat. The ground is depicted with small stones or mounds.]

**E**rythraea, or Eriphila, was one of the Sibyls and was exceptionally distinguished. It is generally thought that there were ten such Sibyls in total, and they were identified by their own specific names. Since they were all very powerful in the gift of prophecy, they are known by this collective name. For in the Aeolic dialect, <term>sios</term> <note>The author is referencing a dialectal form of the Greek word "theos" (god).</note> sounds like "god" in Latin, and <term>biles</term> <note>Derived from the Greek "boule," meaning counsel or will.</note> is said to mean "mind." Therefore, the Sibyls are so named as if they were "of a divine mind" or "possessing the mind of God." Among all these venerable women, it is said that this one was the most celebrated. Some report that her origin was among the Babylonians some time before the Trojan War, <note>A legendary conflict between the Greeks and the city of Troy, traditionally dated to the 12th or 13th century BCE.</note> but others believe she prophesied during the time of Romulus, the first King of the Romans.

According to some, her name was Eriphila, but she was called "Erythraean" because she lived for a long time on the island <note>Erythrae was actually a city in Ionia (modern Turkey), though medieval texts often referred to these locations as islands or remote regions.</note> of Erythrae, and most of her songs <note>The prophecies of the Sibyls were traditionally written in dactylic hexameter verse, hence they are called "songs" or "poems."</note> were composed there. Such was the power of her intellect, or her speech, or the merit of her devotion in the sight of God, that through her vigilant study—and not without a divine gift—she deserved to describe future events with such clarity (if what is read was truly said by her) that her work seems more like a Gospel than a prophecy.

<summary>This page introduces the Erythraean Sibyl, explaining the Greek etymology of the word "Sibyl" as "the mind of God" and discussing her historical origins and the Christ-centered clarity of her prophecies.</summary>

<keywords>Erythraean Sibyl, Eriphila, etymology, Aeolic dialect, prophecy, Trojan War, Romulus, Erythrae, Sibylline Oracles</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the Erythraean Sibyl, likely from Giovanni Boccaccio’s "Of Famous Women," transitioning into the story of Medusa.</meta>

.22.

Nor indeed, when the Greeks inquired of her, did she describe her labors and the destruction of Ilium <note>original: "ylionis"; another name for Troy</note> in verse so calmly that nothing known after the event was clearer than what she had said before. Thus she also encompassed the Roman Empire and its various fortunes in a few words, long before its beginning, so that in our own age she seems to have written a brief summary <term>epitome|a condensed account or summary of a larger work</term> rather than to have predicted the future. 

And there is this, which is a much greater mystery of the divine mind (in my judgment): not only through the symbols of the ancients and the hidden sayings of the prophets, but by the primary words of the prophecy of the Holy Spirit, she revealed the predicted mystery of the Word made flesh—the life and works of Him once born, the betrayal, the capture, the mockeries, the shameful death, the triumph of the resurrection, the ascension, and the return for the final judgment—so that she appears to have dictated a history rather than predicted future events.

By these merits, I judge her to have been most beloved by God and worthy of veneration above all other women of the Gentiles <note>The author uses "Gentiles" here to refer to the pre-Christian or non-Jewish pagan world</note>. There are some who further assert that she flourished in perpetual virginity, which I easily believe; for such a great light of future things could not have shone forth in a defiled breast. At what time or in what part of the world she passed away has been lost to time.

<meta>This page continues the historical or "euhemeristic" reinterpretation of classical myths, where supernatural stories are explained as misunderstood historical events. Here, the focus shifts to the origin of Medusa.</meta>

<margin>AB</margin>

# The History of Medusa

[Decorative initial M containing two birds] <note>The letter M begins the name Medusa</note> **Medusa** was the heir and daughter of Phorcys, a very wealthy king. To her belonged a most opulent kingdom by the Atlantic Sea, which some believed to be the islands of the Hesperides <note>In mythology, the Hesperides were nymphs who tended a garden of golden apples at the edge of the western world</note>. Nor (if I can trust the accounts of antiquity) was she merely of such admirable beauty that she surpassed all other women, but rather, as something wonderful beyond the laws of nature, she moved many men to travel just to see her. She possessed abundant hair like gold, which was the principal glory of her face, and her body was tall and noble in stature. 

But among her other features, there was such a great and yet calm power in her eyes that those she looked upon kindly were rendered almost motionless and lost to themselves <note>The author is suggesting that Medusa’s "stony gaze" was actually just a metaphorical way of describing how men were transfixed by her beauty</note>. Furthermore, some assert that she was highly skilled in **agriculture**, and that the name "Gorgon" followed from this skill. With wonderful wisdom, she used this knowledge not only to preserve her father’s wealth but to increase it immensely—so much so that those who knew of her believed she surpassed all the kings of the West in her treasures. Thus, through her exceptional beauty as well as her wealth and wisdom, she achieved the widest fame even among distant nations.

Hardly had this famous rumor reached the Argives <note>The people of Argos, a city in Greece</note>, among whom was Perseus—the most brilliant of the Achaean <note>Greek</note> youth. Having heard these reports, he was seized with a desire both to see this most beautiful woman and to seize her treasures. And so, boarding a ship which bore the image of the horse **Pegasus** as its emblem, he was carried to the West with wonderful speed. There, using both his wisdom and his weapons, he overcame the queen and returned to his people laden with gold and rich plunder.

From these events, poetic fiction found its inspiration. Through these myths, we read that Medusa the Gorgon was accustomed to turn those she looked upon into stone, and that her hair was turned into snakes by the wrath of **Minerva** <note>The Roman name for the goddess Athena</note> because she had defiled Minerva’s temple by lying with **Neptune** <note>The Roman name for the sea-god Poseidon</note> and giving birth to Pegasus. The poets also claim that Perseus flew into her kingdom sitting upon a winged horse and overcame her using the <term>aegis|A protective shield or garment associated with the goddess Athena/Pallas</term> of Pallas. Unhappy is the possession of gold! For if it remains hidden, it is of no use to its owner; if it shines, it attracts a thousand <note>The text cuts off here at "mille," likely intending to say "a thousand dangers" or "a thousand envious eyes"</note>

<summary>This page presents a rationalized version of the Medusa myth, portraying her as a wealthy, beautiful queen and master of agriculture whose "stony gaze" was merely her captivating beauty. It identifies Perseus as a historical Greek adventurer who raided her kingdom using a ship named Pegasus.</summary>
<keywords>Medusa, Phorcys, Perseus, Gorgons, Hesperides, euhemerism, Pegasus, Minerva, Neptune, Achaeans</keywords>

...from the lust for wealth, ambushes are born; and even if riches remain out of the hands of violent men, the anxious cares of the owner never cease. For peace of mind is driven away, sleep is stolen, great fear enters, faith is diminished, suspicion increases, and in short, every enjoyment of life is hindered for the wretched man. If by any chance his wealth should perish, he is tormented <note>The original Latin *excarnificatur* literally means "to be flayed" or "torn to pieces," reflecting the psychological agony of losing one's fortune.</note> by anxieties upon becoming poor. The miser praises the loss, the generous man laughs, the envious man offers hollow consolation to the destitute, and the whole common crowd turns the grieving man's story into a mocking tale.

### Concerning Iole, daughter of the King of the Aetolians. Chapter 21.

**Iole** was the daughter of Eurytus, the King of Aetolia <note>A region in central Greece.</note>. There are those who claim that this maiden, the most beautiful in all that region, was loved by **Hercules**, the conqueror of the world. They say that although Eurytus had promised her to him in marriage, he later denied Hercules' request at the persuasion of his son. 

Angry at this, Hercules waged a fierce war against the King; he killed him, captured the province, and carried off his beloved Iole. She, however, was more affected by the slaughter of her father than by love for her suitor. Greedy for revenge, and with marvelous and constant cunning, she hid her true feelings behind a mask of false love. Through flatteries and a certain artful playfulness, she led him into such a burning desire for her...

<summary>This page concludes a warning about the miseries of greed before introducing the story of Iole, who used Hercules' infatuation with her to humiliate him as revenge for her father's death.</summary>
<keywords>Iole, Hercules, Eurytus, Aetolia, greed, revenge, spinning wool, gender roles, Greek mythology</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
[322]

She drew Hercules into such affection that she clearly perceived he would deny her nothing she might ask. But then, as if shuddering at a lover in such a rough appearance, she commanded the fierce man, before all other things, to set aside the club with which he had tamed monsters. She made him lay down the Nemean lion’s skin—the badge of his strength—as well as his poplar wreath, his quivers, and his arrows. Since these things did not sufficiently satisfy her mind, she leaped more boldly upon her unarmed enemy with well-calculated weapons of deceit. 

First, she ordered his fingers to be adorned with rings; his head, sprinkled with Cyprian ointments, to be smoothed; his shaggy hair to be divided with a comb; his rough beard to be anointed with nard; and for him to be marked with girlish garlands and even a Maeonian <note>Maeonia is an ancient name for Lydia, a region in modern-day Turkey</note> miter <note>A soft, decorative headdress or turban associated with Eastern luxury</note>. She ordered him to be dressed in purple robes and soft garments; the young woman, armed with deceits, judged it a much greater glory to have effeminated such a robust man through wantonness than to have killed him with a sword or with aconite <note>A deadly poison derived from the monkshood plant</note>. 

Furthermore, since she did not think her indignation was yet sufficiently satisfied by these things, she drove him, now devoted to softness, to this: that even sitting in the manner of a woman among common girls, he would tell the little tales of his labors, and having taken up the assigned tasks, spin wool from a distaff. Those fingers which, as an infant in his cradle, he had hardened to crush snakes, he now employed in his weak and failed age <note>The text suggests his "age" or state has been broken/weakened by luxury</note> to draw out thin threads. 

Truly, for those wishing to observe it, this is no small proof of human weakness and womanly cunning. Thus, by this ingenious punishment, the young woman—with a perpetual mark of shame upon Hercules—avenged her father's death not with weapons, but with deceits and lust, and she made herself worthy of an eternal name. For however many victorious triumphs Alcides <note>original: "alcides"; a common epithet for Hercules, referring to his grandfather Alcaeus</note> led over whatever monsters for Eurystheus <note>The king who assigned Hercules his twelve labors</note>, the victorious Iole <note>In some versions of the myth, Iole is the captive princess who causes Hercules's downfall, while Omphale is the queen who makes him spin wool; this text attributes the revenge to Iole</note> herself triumphed even more gloriously over just as many.

<summary>This page recounts the mythological episode where Hercules is humbled and effeminated by Iole (or Omphale) as revenge for her father's death, forcing the hero to trade his club and lion skin for jewelry, perfumes, and the domestic task of spinning wool.</summary>
<keywords>Hercules, Iole, Alcides, Eurystheus, spinning wool, gender roles, revenge, Greek mythology, effeminacy, Nemean lion</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
.24.

It is a monstrous thing that those very things which he himself had often subdued were [the ones that overcame him]. It ought to strike no small fear into those concerned for their salvation and shake off their lethargy, since it is clear how strong and powerful an enemy looms over them. We must therefore be watchful, and our hearts must be armed with the greatest strength <margin>handwritten B</margin>; for the enemy does not settle upon those who are unwilling.

One must therefore resist the very beginnings; the eyes must be restrained so they do not see vanities; the ears must be blocked in the manner of the <term>asp</term> <note>original: "aspidis". A reference to Psalm 58, where the "deaf asp" stops its ears to the charmer; a common medieval metaphor for closing one's mind to temptation.</note>; lust must be suppressed by constant labor. For indeed, it offers itself enticingly to the unwary and seems peaceful at first sight. And if it is received with joyful hope, it delights at its first entry; it encourages bodily finery, refined manners, city wit, dances, songs and poems, games, and banquets and the like.

But after it has entirely occupied the person through foolish trial, and liberty has been subdued by casting chains and fetters upon the mind, it stirs up sighs for desires that are deferred beyond hope. It forces the intellect into deceptive arts, making no distinction between virtues and vices, as long as it achieves what is desired, counting as enemies whatever stands in its way. From here, with flames burning the breasts of the wretched, one goes back and forth, and with tireless striving, the beloved thing is sought; and by looking repeatedly, new fires of passion are always contracted.

And when there is no room for repentance, it turns to tears; prayers smoothed with honeyed flatteries are dictated; <term>go-betweens</term> <note>original: "lenae", meaning procuresses or intermediaries in a romantic or illicit pursuit.</note> are instructed; gifts are promised; money is given and thrown away; and sometimes the guardians are deceived, and hearts guarded by watchfulness are captured, and eventually they come to the embrace of the bed. Then the enemy of shame and the persuader of crime—modesty and honor having been put to flight—pours out the grunting allurements of intercourse in a wallow prepared for pigs.

Then, with sobriety cast aside, <term>Venus</term> <note>The goddess of love, used here to represent sexual desire.</note> is summoned, burning with <term>Ceres</term> <note>The goddess of grain, representing food or gluttony.</note> and <term>Bacchus</term> <note>The god of wine, representing drunkenness.</note>; and entire nights are consumed in filthy excess. Nor is that madness always extinguished by this; rather, it is very often increased into a greater insanity. From which it happens that into that obedience...

<summary>This page provides a moralizing warning against the gradual descent into lust and vice, describing how seemingly innocent social pleasures lead to total moral corruption and physical excess.</summary>
<keywords>temptation, lust, vice, Venus, Bacchus, Ceres, morality, spiritual warfare, medieval ethics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a moralizing discourse on the destructive nature of earthly lust, likely following the account of Hercules’ downfall due to his love for Iole. It then transitions into Chapter 22, which focuses on Hercules' wife, Deianira.</meta>

May the detestable Hercules <note>The text uses "Alcides," a common patronymic for Hercules, referring to his grandfather Alcaeus.</note> fall; let honors be forgotten; let fortunes be squandered; let hatreds be stirred up; and let the perils of life be very often endured. Nor are these things free from sorrows; brawls are encountered, and peace is precarious; then again, there is suspicion and jealousy—the consumer of both souls and bodies. 

But if one's desire is not fulfilled, then love—devoid of reason—exaggerates anxieties as if spurred on by a rod. It heaps up desires and inflicts almost intolerable pains, for which no remedy is applied except that which is cured by tears, lamentations, and sometimes even death. Old hags and soothsayers <note>original: "caldei," literally Chaldeans, who were famously associated with astrology and the occult in the ancient world</note> are consulted; the potency of herbs, incantations, and sorceries is tested. Flattery turns into threats; violence is readied; and frustrated affection is cursed. Nor does that architect of evils <note>A reference to Cupid or the personification of Lust.</note> fail to instill such fury at times that he drives the wretched into nooses and swords. 

Oh, how sweet and pleasant is this love!—the very thing we should flee in horror, yet we extol it as a god. We worship it; we entreat it as suppliants; and we perform sacred rites composed of sighs and tears. We offer up debauchery, adultery, and incest, and we bestow crowns woven from our own obscenities.

### Concerning Deianira, daughter of Oeneus, King of the Aetolians. Chapter 22.

<summary>This page concludes a scathing moral critique of how romantic passion destroys noble men like Hercules before introducing the specific history of his wife, Deianira.</summary>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of famous women, following the previous chapter. The text focuses on Deianira, the wife of Hercules, and the tragic circumstances of his death caused by her attempt to secure his love.</meta>

### .xxv.

**D**eianira <note>The text reads "Depanira," a common transcription error for Deianira</note>, the daughter of Oeneus, king of the Aetolians <note>original: "etholorum"</note> (as some assert), was the sister of Meleager. She was a maiden of such distinguished beauty that a contest arose between Achelous <note>A river god who wrestled Hercules for Deianira's hand</note> and Hercules to obtain her in marriage. When she had been yielded to the victor, Hercules, she was loved by Nessus the centaur. While Hercules was transporting her from Calydon back to his homeland, he was delayed by the river Evenus in Calydon, which was swollen by the previous day's rains. There he encountered the amorous Nessus, who, because he was a horseman <note>Nessus was a centaur, a mythological creature with the upper body of a human and the lower body of a horse</note>, offered himself of his own accord to Hercules to be helpful in transporting Deianira across. 

When Hercules had consented to this, intending to swim across after his wife, Nessus—as if he had achieved his heart's desire—took flight with the beloved woman once he had crossed the river. Since Hercules could not overtake him on foot, he struck the fleeing centaur with an arrow infected with the venom of the Hydra <note>original: "lerna infecta tabe"; Hercules had dipped his arrows in the poisonous blood of the Lernaean Hydra</note>. Sensing this and realizing he was dying, Nessus immediately handed over a garment stained with his own blood to Deianira. He claimed that if Hercules were to wear this blood-soaked robe, it would have the power to draw him back from any outside love and return him to her own. 

Deianira, being gullible and taking this as a very great gift, kept it hidden for some time. Later, when Hercules was in love with Omphale or Iole <note>Rival women in the legends of Hercules; Omphale was a queen of Lydia, and Iole was a princess of Oechalia</note>, she cautiously sent the robe to him by way of Lichas <note>original: "plicā"; likely a corruption of the name Lichas</note>, a servant. However, when the heat of Hercules's sweat dissolved the blood infected with the poison and his pores absorbed it, he was driven into a madness and gave himself up of his own accord to be consumed by fire. And thus Deianira, widowed of such a great husband, lost him while hoping to win him back, and in doing so, she even fulfilled the revenge of Nessus.

<summary>This page recounts the Greek myth of Deianira, whose accidental poisoning of her husband Hercules with the "Blood of Nessus" leads to his agonizing death and her own tragic widowhood.</summary>
<keywords>Deianira, Hercules, Nessus, Centaur, Achelous, Oeneus, Aetolia, Lernaean Hydra, Iole, Omphale, Lichas, mythology</keywords>

# On Jocasta, Queen of the Thebans. Chapter 23.

[Woodcut illustration: A scene divided into three parts. On the left, a woman labeled **Jocasta** stands in a state of grief. In the center, a man and a woman stand over an infant abandoned on the ground near a dog, representing the exposure of the child. On the right, a man labeled **Oedipus** stands with a sword near another figure, representing the tragic encounter on the road. Labels **Jocasta** and **Oedipus** are integrated into the art.]

**J**ocasta, Queen of the Thebans, was more famous for her misfortune than for her merits or her kingdom. Although she traced a splendid lineage from the first founders of Thebes, as a maiden she married Laius, King of the Thebans. Having conceived a son by him, and because of an adverse response Laius had received from an oracle <note>The Delphic oracle famously prophesied that any son born to Laius would kill his father and marry his mother.</note>, she reluctantly <note>original: "egra," implying she did so with a heavy or sick heart</note> handed the newborn over to be cast out to wild beasts by her husband's command. 

While she believed he had been immediately devoured, he was instead raised as a son by the King of the Corinthians. When he had reached full adulthood—and after Laius had been killed by this same man in Phocis—the widowed Jocasta took him as her husband, not knowing who he was. By him, she bore sons Eteocles and Polynices, and as many daughters, namely Ismene and Antigone. 

And just when she seemed happy in both her reign and her offspring, she learned through a response of the gods that the man she considered her lawful husband was actually her son. Even if she herself bore this most bitterly, he bore it even more so; so much so that, out of shame for the crime he had committed, and desiring eternal night, he cast away <note>original: "obiecit." This refers to the famous moment where Oedipus blinds himself, rejecting the light of day.</note> his eyes and his kingdom. 

Their sons, taking up this discord, entered into war after their treaties were broken. Although Jocasta, in her profound sadness, often descended into the midst of their struggle to stop them, they eventually engaged in a great duel with mutual... <note>The text cuts off here, but historical accounts conclude with the brothers killing each other and Jocasta taking her own life.</note>

<summary>This page recounts the tragic life of Queen Jocasta of Thebes, focusing on her unwitting marriage to her son Oedipus and the subsequent downfall of her family.</summary>
<keywords>Jocasta, Oedipus, Thebes, Laius, Greek Mythology, Eteocles, Polynices, Antigone, Oracle of Delphi</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the tragic conclusion of the story of Thebes, specifically the fate of Jocasta, before transitioning to a new chapter on the Sibyl of Cumae.</meta>

.26.

...received those slain by their wounds. Unable to endure such sorrow, the wretched mother and grandmother <note>Jocasta, who was both mother and wife to Oedipus</note>—even as she saw her brother Creon now established as king, her son bereft, her husband a captive, and her daughters Ismene and Antigone caught in the snares of failing fortune—the old woman at last drove out her reluctant soul, weary of misfortunes, with a blade, ending her anxieties along with her life. There are some, however, who maintain that she could not have borne her shameful errors for so long, but rather that as soon as she saw Oedipus gouging out his own eyes, she immediately turned her violence upon herself.

### On Amalthea, also known as Deiphobe the Sibyl
### Chapter 27

->[Image: A woman labeled "Amalthea" stands on the left, gesturing toward a fire in which books labeled "Books" are burning. On the right, King "Tarquinius Priscus" sits upon his throne, also gesturing toward the fire.]<-

**A**malthea was a virgin whom some call Deiphobe, the daughter of Glaucus. Since she was one of the <term>Sibyls</term> <note>prophetic women in ancient Greece and Rome who were believed to possess divine knowledge of the future</note>, she flourished at the time of the destruction of Troy, and...

<meta>This page continues a discussion regarding the lifespan and divine inspiration of a Sibyl, likely the Cumaean Sibyl, transitioning from her longevity to her prophetic gifts and her physical legacy in Italy.</meta>

...attained many years and longevity. I indeed believe that by merit of her virginity, she received the light of prophecy from the true Sun himself—who enlightens every man coming into this world <note>original: "qui illuminat omnem hominem venientem in hunc mundum" — a reference to the Gospel of John 1:9, here attributing the Sibyl's pagan prophecy to the Christian God</note>—through which she foretold and wrote of many future things. Furthermore, they say there was a famous oracle on the shore of Baiae <note>A Roman resort town on the Gulf of Naples</note> near Lake Avernus <note>A volcanic crater lake near Naples, which the Romans believed was the entrance to the underworld</note>, which I myself have seen and heard of, and which retains its name from her to this very day. Although it is deeply worn by great age and half-collapsed from neglect, even in ruins it preserves its ancient majesty and still offers a sense of wonder to those observing its sheer scale. There are some, moreover, who say she provided guidance to the underworld for the refugee Aeneas <note>The hero of Virgil’s "Aeneid"; the Sibyl acts as his guide through the land of the dead</note>, though I do not believe this. But more on this elsewhere. Those, however, who maintain she lived through many centuries assert that she came to Rome and brought nine books to Tarquinius Priscus <note>Tarquin the Elder, the fifth king of Rome; these books were the famous Sibylline Books which were consulted by the Roman state in times of crisis</note>.

## On Nicostrata, or Carmenta, daughter of King Poneus. Chapter 25

[Woodcut illustration of a woman, **Nicostrata**, holding a scroll and teaching three men. Text within the woodcut:
- Vertical on the left: **Nicostrata**
- On the scroll: She instructs the unrefined people <note>original: "Rudi p̄lo" (rudi populo)</note> in the Latin language with the alphabet <note>original: "a b c / linguā erudit latinā"</note>
- Near the men's feet: x y z]

**N**icostrata—who was later named Carmenta <note>The name Carmenta is derived from the Latin word "carmen," meaning song, poem, or oracle, reflecting her prophetic gifts.</note> among the Italians—was the daughter of Poneus, King of the Arcadians <note>Arcadia was a mountainous region in ancient Greece, legendary for its rustic simplicity and as the home of the god Pan.</note>. According to some, she married Pallas the Arcadian; according to others, her marriage was of a different sort. She was distinguished not only by the splendor of her royal rank, but indeed was most learned in Greek literature. She possessed such a versatile intellect that through tireless study she attained the gift of prophecy and became a most famous seer <term>vates</term> <note>A "vates" is a divinely inspired poet-prophet who speaks the truth of the gods.</note>. Because she would reveal the future in verse—sometimes to those who came seeking her counsel and sometimes of her own accord—the Latins eventually set aside her original name, Nicostrata, and called her Carmenta instead. She was the mother of Evander, King of the Arcadians, whom the myths of the ancients claim was conceived by the god Mercury, either because he was an eloquent and well-spoken man, or because he was particularly clever <note>In mythology, Mercury was the god of both eloquence and cunning, so a person with these traits was often metaphorically called a "son of Mercury."</note>. He <meta>The text breaks off here; the following page likely describes Evander and Carmenta’s journey to Italy and the creation of the Latin alphabet.</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Nicostrata, an Arcadian princess and prophetess also known as Carmenta, highlighting her profound learning in Greek literature and her role as the mother of Evander.</summary>
<keywords>Nicostrata, Carmenta, Evander, prophecy, vates, Arcadia, Greek literature, Mercury, Latin origins, royal women</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the founding of early settlements in Italy, specifically focusing on the arrival of the hero Evander and his mother Carmenta at the site that would eventually become Rome.</meta>

With a portion of his people and a favorable wind, he arrived at the mouth of the Tiber from the Peloponnese. Guided by his mother, he settled on the Palatine Hill—which he named after either Pallas his father or Pallas his son—where the great city of Rome was later established. There, he settled with his followers and his mother and built the town of Pallanteum <note>named after Pallas, a figure from Greek mythology; Virgil also mentions this town in the Aeneid</note>.

Indeed, when Carmenta realized that the natives were forest-dwelling, wild men—even though they had long since learned to sow crops under the influence of the exiled god Saturn <note>original: *saturni profugi numem*; Saturn was a mythical titan-king who, according to legend, fled to Italy and taught the inhabitants agriculture and law</note>—she saw they had no use for letters, or at best a slight acquaintance with Greek ones. Foreseeing with her divine mind how great the fame of this region would be in the future, she felt it was unworthy for their future triumphs to be recorded using the "crutch" of foreign scripts. 

She applied herself with all the strength of her intellect to provide the people with their own letters, entirely distinct from those of other nations. God did not fail this undertaking; for by His grace, she invented new characters <term>caracteribus</term><note>The physical shapes of the letters themselves.</note> according to their own language and taught how they should be joined together. She was content to have produced only sixteen figures, just as long ago Cadmus <note>original: *cadinus*; Cadmus was the legendary Phoenician prince who was credited with bringing the alphabet to the Greeks</note>, the founder of Thebes, had invented for the Greeks. These we call "letters" even to this day, and we hold them as her gift, though some wise men have appropriately added more to them without removing any of the old ones.

Although the Latins greatly marveled at this woman’s prophecy, this invention of writing seemed so miraculous that the unrefined people believed Carmenta was not a human, but rather a goddess. For this reason, while she lived, they celebrated her with divine honors; and when she died at the foot of the Capitoline Hill where she had lived, they built a shrine <term>sacellum</term><note>A small, unroofed enclosure or shrine dedicated to a deity.</note> in her name. To her perpetual memory, they called the adjacent places the "Carmentalia." Even when Rome grew great, it did not allow this memory to be abolished; instead, they called the city gate that the citizens built there out of necessity the "Carmental Gate" for many centuries, after Carmenta's name.

In times past, Italy was flourishing with many gifts beyond all other regions of the world, shining almost with a heavenly light. Nor was such a brilliant splendor sought only under its own skies. For from another wealth...

<summary>This page recounts the arrival of Evander and his mother Carmenta at the Palatine Hill and details Carmenta's legendary invention of the Latin alphabet to ensure Italy's future deeds were recorded in its own script.</summary>
<keywords>Evander, Carmenta, Palatine Hill, Pallanteum, Latin Alphabet, Saturn, Cadmus, Carmental Gate, Roman Origins, Writing</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues a list of cultural and scientific origins, likely following a discussion of other civilizations' contributions to the arts and sciences. It transitions into a defense of the Latin alphabet as the primary vehicle for European knowledge and Christian faith.</meta>

->.27.<-

...and royal furnishings; the nobility of blood (even if the Greeks added much to the claim) was held to come from the Trojans. First, the Egyptians gave the arts of arithmetic and geometry. Philosophy and eloquence, and almost all mechanical work, were taken from those same Greeks. Agriculture, known to few until then, was introduced by Saturn <note>original: "Saturnus"; in Roman mythology, Saturn was an exiled god who fled to Italy and taught the inhabitants agriculture and civilization.</note> while in exile. The ill-omened worship of the gods was maintained by the Etruscans and Numa Pompilius <note>The legendary second king of Rome, credited with establishing the city’s early religious laws and rituals.</note>. Athens first provided public laws; then the decrees of the Senate and the Caesars provided them. Simon Peter brought the highest priesthood and sincere religion from Jerusalem. But the ancient Romans devised military discipline, by which—through the strength of their arms and bodies, and through their uncorrupted love for the Republic—they sought the empire of the whole world for themselves. 

As for the characters of letters <note>meaning the Latin alphabet</note>, it is clear enough from what has been said that Carmenta <note>A prophetic goddess who, according to legend, adapted the Greek alphabet to create the Latin letters after arriving in Italy from Arcadia, Greece.</note> granted them to our ancestors when she arrived in Italy from Arcadia. Thus, she is also believed to have sown the first seeds of the faculty of grammar, which the ancients cultivated into a more bountiful harvest as time passed. God was so favorable to these letters that, although the greatest share of glory was taken from Hebrew and Greek letters, almost all of Europe across a wide expanse of lands uses our own. 

Through these letters, the infinite, shining volumes of every branch of knowledge, the deeds of men, and the great works of God are preserved in perpetual memory—so that we may recognize those things we were unable to see for ourselves, with the help of these writings. Through these, we transmit our prayers and receive those of others with faith. Through these, we form friendships at a distance and preserve them through mutual correspondence. They describe God to us (as much as is possible); they represent the heavens, the lands, the seas, and all living creatures. Nor is there anything you might seek that you cannot perceive through these while keeping watch. In short, by their help, whatever cannot be fully grasped or held by the breadth of the human mind is entrusted to their most faithful custody. And although some of these benefits may fall to others, nothing praiseworthy is taken away from our own letters. Furthermore, out of so many excellent gifts, we have lost some, added others, and some we still hold more by name than by actual effect. Truly...

<summary>This page outlines the historical origins of various arts, laws, and religions, attributing the Latin alphabet to the goddess Carmenta and celebrating its role in preserving European knowledge and the Christian faith.</summary>
<keywords>Latin alphabet, Carmenta, Saturn, Numa Pompilius, Roman Empire, history of writing, transmission of knowledge, Simon Peter</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page concludes a defense of the Latin language's cultural supremacy before transitioning into a collection of mythological or historical biographies, beginning here with the story of Procris.</meta>

In whatever way it has gone for the rest of our people, whether by crime or by fortune, neither German rapacity, nor Gallic <note>original: "gallicus"; referring to the French</note> fury, nor English cunning, nor Spanish ferocity, nor the unrefined barbarity or assault of any other nation has ever been able to snatch away this so great, so remarkable, and so opportune glory from the Latin name: namely, that they could or would dare to call the first elements of letters <note>The alphabet</note> their own by right, or claim that grammar had been refined by them for a long time. These things, just as we ourselves hoped, we have also given freely, yet they are always marked with our name. From this, it happens that the further these letters are carried, the more the praises and honors of the Latin name are expanded; they bear a clearer testimony of our most ancient beauty, nobility, and genius, and they preserve an uncorrupted proof of our foresight <note>original: "perspicacitatis"</note>, even while the barbarians <note>A common term used by Renaissance humanists to describe non-Latinate or non-Italian cultures.</note> resent us. For this most extraordinary brilliance, although we ought to give thanks to God the giver, we nevertheless owe much praise, love, and faith to Carmentis <note>A prophetess in Roman mythology credited with adapting the Greek alphabet into the Latin one.</note>. Therefore, so that we may not be justly accused by anyone of being ungrateful, it is a most pious duty to offer her name into eternal memory to the best of our ability.

.xxviii.

**P**rocris, the daughter of Pandion, King of Athens, and wife to Cephalus, the son of King Aeolus, is as much hated by modest matrons for her greed as she is accepted by men, because through her the vice of other women was laid bare. <note>The author uses Procris's story as a moralizing example of how greed can undermine even a seemingly perfect marriage.</note> For while husband and wife lived enjoying a happy and pious love, it happened by their misfortune that Cephalus was desired by Aurora <note>The goddess of the Dawn; the text notes some call her "Aura," meaning a breeze.</note>—a woman of remarkable beauty, according to some—who tried for some time in vain to draw him to her side, as he was held by his passion for his Procris. Whereupon Aurora said indignantly: "You will repent, Cephalus, of having loved Procris so fervently; you will find that if someone tests her with a bribe, she will prefer gold to your love." <note>original: "aurum amori pposuisse tuo"</note>

Hearing this, the young man, eager to put her to the test, went away pretending to go on a long journey. But he turned back toward his homeland and, in disguise, tested his wife’s constancy with gifts through an intermediary. No matter how great the promises were, she could not be swayed at the first impulse. Finally, as he persevered and offered jewels, he turned her wavering mind; a night and the desired embraces were promised to him, provided he gave the pledged gold.

Then Cephalus, filled with grief and sorrow, revealed that he had intercepted Procris's frivolous love through this trick. Flushed with shame and driven by the conscience of her crime, she immediately fled into the woods and gave herself to solitude. The young man, however, impatient in his love, granted her pardon of his own accord and, with prayers, called her back into favor even though she resisted him.

But what does it matter? The power of forgiveness has no strength against the stings of conscience. Procris was driven by various shifting emotions and by jealousy, fearing that perhaps her husband might do to her—through the charms of Aurora—the very thing she herself had bartered for with gold. Secretly, through crags and steep mountain ridges and the hidden places of the valleys, she began to follow the hunters. While doing this, it happened that while Procris was moving, hiding among the grassy valleys and marshy reeds, she was mistaken for a wild beast by her husband and died, pierced by his arrow. I do not know (what I should rather say) whether there was nothing <unclear>illegible</unclear>

->d<-

<meta>This page concludes the story of Eriphyle (who betrayed her husband for a necklace) and begins the account of Argia, the daughter of Adrastus. The previous page likely detailed Eriphyle's greed.</meta>

...nothing on earth is more powerful than gold, or more foolish than to seek that which you would wish you had never desired. While that foolish woman <note>Eriphyle, mentioned in the preceding chapter</note> sought after both of these, she earned for herself an everlasting stain on her reputation and a death she certainly did not go looking for. 

But—to put an end to this discussion of the immoderate desire for gold, by which almost all foolish people are drawn—I ask those seized by such stubborn zeal to tell me: what benefit do they feel they gain from it? What honor? What praise or glory do they achieve? In my judgment, this is a ridiculous sickness of the mind taking its origin from the cowardice of the sufferer. We see this trait only in those who consider their own virtue so lowly that they easily concede that anyone else should be preferred over them.

## Concerning Argia, Daughter of King Adrastus. Chapter 27.

<margin>24.</margin> 
->**1473**<-
<note>This year is handwritten in the margin of the original 1473 Ulm edition by Johann Zainer.</note>

[**Argia** and **Polynices** embrace over a field of fallen soldiers. Argia is a Greek noblewoman; Polynices is the son of the tragic King Oedipus.]

**A**rgia was a Greek woman who traced her noble lineage back to the ancient kings of the Argives <note>The people of Argos, a powerful city-state in ancient Greece</note>. She was the daughter of King Adrastus. Just as she provided a joyful sight to her own contemporaries through her remarkable beauty, she also left to future generations a most pure and famous perennial testimony of marital love. Because of this, her shining name has come down even to our own day with exceptional brilliance. 

This woman was married to **Polynices**, the son of **Oedipus** of Thebes <note>Oedipus was the legendary king of Thebes who unknowingly killed his father and married his mother; Polynices was his son who fought his brother Eteocles for the throne</note>...

<summary>This page concludes a moral warning against greed and begins the biography of Argia, praising her as a historical model of devoted marital love.</summary>
<keywords>Argia, Adrastus, Polynices, marital devotion, greed, virtue, Oedipus, Thebes, Argos</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
.FFF.

Of the king and the exile <note>Argia was the daughter of King Adrastus of Argos and wife of the exiled Polynices of Thebes.</note>; when she had already borne a son, Thersander, by him, and noticed that he was being agitated by gnawing cares due to his brother’s treachery, she shared in his anxieties. She not only entreated her now-elderly father with tears and prayers, but even spurred him to take up arms against Eteocles <note>Polynices' brother, who refused to give up the throne of Thebes as previously agreed.</note> because of the violation of their pact, as Eteocles was occupying the Theban kingdom like a tyrant. 

And so that a fatal response <note>An oracle or prophecy regarding the war.</note> should not lead to ruin, she became generous—prompted by her feminine nature—and offered that precious necklace, once so ill-fated for Theban matrons <note>The Necklace of Harmonia, which brought misfortune to all who owned it.</note>, to **Eriphyle**, the wife of the seer **Amphiaraus**. By this bribe, the hidden Amphiaraus was revealed and went to Thebes, though under an unlucky omen. 

For after a great slaughter in the battles, the other leaders being slain, and Adrastus being stripped of reinforcements and nearly put to flight, the anxious wife heard that the body of **Polynices** lay unburied among the corpses of the common rabble. Immediately, she cast off her royal splendor and the comforts of her chamber. Setting aside the perceived weakness of the female sex, she snatched up a journey toward the camp with only a few companions. Neither the impious bands of marauders lying in wait on the road, nor the wild beasts, nor the birds following the bodies of the slain men, nor the ghosts of the fallen fluttering about (as the foolish believe) could terrify her. 

Nor—what seemed even more terrible—was she deterred by the edict of the ruling **Creon**, which warned of the penalty of death for anyone who provided funeral rites for any of the slain. Instead, with a burning and mournful soul, she entered the field of battle in the middle of the night. She turned over one body and then another, reeking with the stench of decay, so that by the help of a small torch she might recognize the wasting face of her most beloved husband. She did not stop until she found what she was seeking. 

O, what a marvel! His face was already half-eaten, covered in the rust of armor and filthy dust, and spattered with clotted blood. Though by heaven he was recognizable to no one else, he could not be hidden from his most loving wife. Neither the filth of his stained face, nor his voicelessness, nor the great impiety of Creon could keep away his wife’s kisses or her tears. For when she had repeatedly sought for a breath of life through kisses on his mouth, and had washed his wounds with her tears...

<summary>This page recounts the devotion of Argia, who instigates a war to restore her husband Polynices to the Theban throne and later risks death to find and mourn his body on the battlefield against Creon's orders.</summary>
<keywords>Argia, Polynices, Eteocles, Adrastus, Eriphyle, Amphiaraus, Creon, Seven Against Thebes, Necklace of Harmonia, funeral rites</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Argia, the daughter of King Adrastus and wife of Polynices, as found in Boccaccio’s "Famous Women" (De Mulieribus Claris). The previous page likely detailed her journey to the battlefield of Thebes to find her husband's body.</meta>

->.XXIX.<-

...limbs, and after she had repeatedly called him back into her embrace with her voice, though he was already lifeless, she finally surrendered him to the already blazing flames (lest any part of her pious duty be left undone). Once he was consumed, she gathered the remains into an urn. When her pious deed was revealed by the light of the fire, she paid the penalty under the sword and chains of the cruel king <note>King Creon of Thebes, who had forbidden the burial of the "Seven Against Thebes" under penalty of death</note>. 

Women often weep for the illnesses, imprisonments, poverty, and many misfortunes of their husbands as long as hope for a kinder fortune remains and the fear of a harsher fate is removed. Although such behavior is indeed praiseworthy, it cannot be called the ultimate proof of love, as Argia’s devotion can. She did not seek out hostile fields only so long as she could weep in her own fatherland; she handled a stinking corpse when she could have ordered others to do so. She bestowed the royal honor of the funeral pyre when, considering the danger of the time, a secret burial would have been sufficient. She let out womanly wails <note>original: "vlulatus"</note> where she might have passed in silence, and she had nothing to hope for from her slain, exiled husband, even while there was everything to fear from the enemy close at hand. Thus true love, thus wholehearted faith, thus the sanctity of marriage and unsullied chastity were able to persuade her. For this merit, Argia deserves to be praised, honored, and extolled with splendid proclamation.

### On Manto, Daughter of Tiresias. Chapter XXVIII. <note>original: "De Manthone tprefie filia" - 'tprefie' is a typographical error for 'Tiresiae'</note>

[Woodcut illustration: A woman labeled **Mantho** stands on the left, gesturing toward a pile of entrails or perhaps snakes on the ground. On the right, two demonic or satyr-like figures with horns and animalistic features watch her ritual.]

--- Page 70 ---
.31.

[Decorative woodcut initial 'M' containing two birds facing each other]
**Manto**, daughter of Tiresias, the greatest seer of Thebes, was illustrious during the time of King Oedipus and his sons. Indeed, under the tutelage of her father, she possessed such a prompt and capable intellect that she learned <term>pyromancy</term> <note>The art of divination by observing the behavior of fire and flames.</note>—an ancient invention of the Chaldeans or, as others would have it, Nimrod—so excellently that in her time no one better understood the movements, colors, and cracklings of flames. It is said that within these signs (by some diabolical aid) the demonstrations of future events are contained. 

Furthermore, she recognized with a sharp eye the entrails of sheep, the livers of bulls, and those of any animal whatsoever. Most often (as it is believed), she drew forth unclean spirits by her arts and compelled the ghosts of the underworld to speak and give answers to those who inquired of them. 

Truly, when the Argive kings who had besieged Thebes had already fallen in war, and Creon had seized the command of the city, she (as it pleases some to believe) fled the new king and withdrew into Asia. There, she established a shrine to the Clarian Apollo, which later became most famous for its divinity; she also gave birth to Mopsus, the renowned seer of his age (though antiquity does not record by whom he was conceived). 

Others think differently and say that she wandered for a long time with certain of her companions after the Theban war and at last arrived in Italy. There, having joined with a certain Tiberinus <note>The god or personification of the Tiber River.</note>, she conceived by him and bore a son whom they called Ocnus <note>original: "oitheonū"; a legendary founder of Mantua.</note>, also called Bianor by some. From there, she crossed with him into <term>Cisalpine Gaul</term> <note>The region of Northern Italy between the Alps and the Apennines.</note>. There, she found marshy places bordering Lake Benacus <note>modern Lake Garda</note> which were naturally secluded <note>original: "opissa sui nata"; likely a corruption of "shady and naturally fortified."</note>. Whether because she wished to be free to devote herself to her incantations, or so that she might avoid...

# Of the Wives of the Minyans. Chapter 29.

[Woodcut illustration showing a group of women in long robes and veils, labeled "Minyans". To the left, a man in a short tunic and hose stands near a barred window through which several faces are visible. Below the window is the label "Wives of the Minyans".]

The number and the names of the wives of the Minyans <note>The Minyans were a group of Greek heroes, many of whom sailed with Jason on the Argo</note> have been lost to us, whether through the neglect of contemporary writers or the ravages of time. This is indeed a shame, as they deserved to be raised to perpetual glory for a deed that was no ordinary feat. But since it has seemed fit to envious Fortune to keep them hidden, we shall adorn these unnamed women with what skill we can through worthy praise; we shall strive with all our power to bring them into the memory of posterity as women who served their people well. 

The Minyans, then, were among the companions of Jason and the Argonauts, most illustrious young men of no small nobility. When the expedition to Colchis <note>The land of the Golden Fleece</note> was finished and they had returned to Greece, they left their old homeland and chose a place for themselves among the Spartans <note>original: "lacedemōes"</note>. To them, citizenship was not only amicably granted by the Spartans, but they were even accepted among the leaders presiding over the republic. 

However, the successors of these men—less mindful of such splendid generosity—dared to attempt to subject the public liberty to a shameful servitude <note>This refers to a historical political uprising or attempted coup by the descendants of the Minyans against the Spartan state</note>. For they were at that time wealthy young men, not only shining with their own brilliance, but also the noble blood of the Spartans...

<summary>This page introduces the Wives of the Minyans, lamenting their lost names while praising their character. It sets the historical stage, explaining how the Minyans (descendants of the Argonauts) settled in Sparta and eventually turned against the state that welcomed them.</summary>
<keywords>Minyans, Argonauts, Sparta, Jason, Colchis, female heroism, political rebellion, Greek mythology</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of a group of noblemen who, having gained excessive pride through their social connections, attempted a coup and were sentenced to death. This page focuses on the transition from their downfall to the daring rescue attempt by their wives.</meta>

.xxxij.

They shone with a sevenfold light because of their high-ranking alliances. For among other things, they had most beautiful wives who were descended from the most noble citizens—and indeed <note>original: "nō edepol," a common Latin exclamation invoking the god Pollux</note>, these women were not the least part of their worldly glory. Large numbers of followers and clients further added to their prestige. 

Yet, they did not feel gratitude toward the public well-being of the fatherland for these blessings; instead, attributing them to their own merits, they allowed themselves to be carried away by such foolishness that they believed they should be preferred over everyone else. Because of this, they fell into a lust for power, and from there they rashly launched attempts to seize control of the state. 

For this reason, once their crime was discovered, they were captured, thrown into prison, and condemned to death by public authority as enemies of the fatherland. And while on the following night, death was to be inflicted upon them by executioners according to the ancient custom of the Lacedaemonians <term>Lacedaemonians: the people of Sparta, known for their strict laws and customs</term>, their grieving and weeping wives devised an unheard-of plan for the rescue of the condemned men. 

Once the plan was conceived, they did not delay in carrying it out. Therefore, dressed in mourning clothes with their faces veiled and eyes full of tears, as the day was already fading into night—and because they were noble women—they were granted permission to enter the prison to see those about to die...

<summary>A group of noblemen are condemned to death for attempting to seize power in Sparta, but their wives devise a daring plan to enter the prison and rescue them on the eve of their execution.</summary>
<keywords>Sparta, Lacedaemonians, conspiracy, treason, noblewomen, prison rescue, capital punishment</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
With the bond strengthened, some believe that no hatred is more ruinous than the yoke of those who disagree; likewise, the love of those who are in agreement exceeds all others. For, kindled by the fire of reason, it does not burn to the point of madness, but warms with a sense of delight, and joins hearts with such great charity <note>original: *caritate*, implying a selfless, spiritual love</note> that they always desire or reject the same things. Being so calmly accustomed to unity, this love omits nothing and does nothing half-heartedly or remissly for its own preservation; and if fortune proves to be an enemy, it undergoes labors and dangers of its own accord. Most watchful for safety, it meditates on plans, seeks remedies, and forges deceptions if necessity demands.

This most sweet love, now strengthened by the calm shared life of the spouses, so stirred the spirits of the Minyan women <note>The Minyae; referring to a classical story where the wives of the Minyan descendants of the Argonauts rescued their husbands from Spartan imprisonment</note> with such fervor that, though they could not have foreseen it before, while their husbands were in danger, they used the powers of their wit and strength to find snares, prepare tools, and organize the timing and methods of their actions to deceive the watchful and severe guards. Having removed the cloud of sensuality and burning with the realization that nothing honorable should be omitted for the safety of a friend, and with piety excited from the innermost depths of the heart, they went forth with reckless daring to rescue the men from danger. Thus, that which public authority had condemned, a modest conjugal love absolved. Those whom authority had handed over to prison, love released; those whom it had long seen held for capital punishment, love snatched from the hands of the executioners and granted them security and life.

And what seemed most impressive of all: with the power of the laws mocked, the public decree and authority of the fathers set aside, and the will of the whole city frustrated, in order to fulfill what they desired, they did not fear to shut themselves up in the place of the condemned in place of their husbands, under the watch of the deceived and wicked guards. Truly <note>original: *edepol*, an ancient Latin oath "by Pollux"</note>, I cannot sufficiently admire such sincere faith and such whole love; and for that reason, I hold it certain that if they had loved half-heartedly, or if they had been bound by only a thin bond, they would have remained idle at home in leisure, as was their right, and would not have performed these great deeds. But yet, to conclude many things in few words: I dare to assert that these women proved themselves to be true and certain men <note>A common rhetorical trope suggesting the women displayed "manly" courage</note>, while the Minyan youths whom they were pretending to be were actually the women.

<summary>This page discusses the transcendent power of conjugal love and reason, using the classical example of the Minyan women who risked their lives to rescue their husbands from prison by swapping clothes with them.</summary>
<keywords>conjugal love, Minyan women, loyalty, sacrifice, legal authority, reason vs madness, gender roles</keywords>

## Concerning Penthesilea, Queen of the Amazons. Chapter 130.

[Woodcut illustration of a battle scene. A female warrior on horseback, labeled "penthesilea", is engaged in combat with several soldiers. She holds a spear and wears armor. The background shows a simple landscape with a tree.]

**Penthesilea**, a virgin, was the Queen of the Amazons and succeeded the queens Orithyia and Antiope. <note>Orithyia and Antiope were legendary early leaders of the Amazons, a tribe of warrior women in Greek mythology.</note> However, I have not read how she was related to these predecessors. They say that this woman, stirred by a rejection of traditional beauty and having overcome the perceived softness of the female body, set out to take up the arms of her ancestors. She covered her golden hair with a helmet, protected her side with a quiver, and following military custom, she mounted chariots and horses. She dared to show herself more remarkable than other queens through her strength and discipline. 

It is clear that she did not lack a powerful intellect, as it is recorded that she invented the use of the battle-axe <note>original: "securis vsum"</note>, which had been unknown until her time. Furthermore, as some authors like to say, having heard of the virtue of the Trojan Hector, she fell ardently in love with him without ever having seen him <note>The idea of loving someone through reputation alone—"love from afar"—was a popular theme in medieval literature.</note>. Desiring to bear a noble child by him to succeed her in the kingdom, she seized a great opportunity and brought her vast forces to his aid against the Greeks...

<summary>This page introduces Penthesilea, the legendary Amazon Queen who joined the Trojan War. It highlights her rejection of traditional feminine roles in favor of military prowess and her supposed invention of the battle-axe.</summary>
<keywords>Penthesilea, Amazons, Trojan War, Hector, women warriors, battle-axe, ancient history, Orithyia, Antiope</keywords>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Penthesilea, the Amazon Queen, and transitions into the story of Polyxena, daughter of Priam. The text reflects medieval perceptions of gender roles and the Trojan War.</meta>

She would at times lay low those standing in her way with a spear, open a path with her sword, and very often drive back routed troops with her bow. She performed so many and such great deeds with manly strength that she sometimes moved Hector <note>The greatest Trojan prince and warrior</note> himself, as he watched her, to admiration of her. Finally, while this warrior-woman <note>Latin: *virago* — a woman of masculine strength or spirit</note> fought alone against the bravest enemies, proving herself more than usually worthy of such a great suitor <note>Penthesilea was said to have come to Troy out of admiration for Hector's prowess</note>, and with many of her own followers already slain, she received a fatal wound. To be pitied, she fell in the midst of the Greeks she herself had struck down. Others, indeed, claim that she arrived at Troy only after Hector was already dead, and was slain there in a fierce battle, as the records state. 

There might be those who wonder how women, however well-armed, ever dared to charge against men, if such wonder were not removed by the fact that practice turns into a second nature. By this habit, she and others of her kind became far more like men in arms than those whom nature made male by sex, but whom idleness and pleasure turned into women—or rather, "helmeted hares." <note>Latin: *lepores galeatos* — a derogatory term for cowardly men who wear the armor of a soldier but have the heart of a rabbit</note>

## Of Polyxena, Daughter of King Priam. Chapter 31.

[Woodcut illustration of a man about to execute a kneeling woman with a sword over a tomb. Labels: Neoptolemus, Polyxena, Achilles]

**P**olyxena was a maiden, the daughter of Priam, King of the Trojans, and Hecuba. She was a young woman of such blooming beauty that she was able to cast the flames of desire into the stern breast of Achilles, the son of Peleus <note>Achilles was the greatest Greek hero of the Trojan War; Polyxena's beauty allegedly distracted him from his duty</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the story of the Amazon Queen Penthesilea, praising her courage over cowardly men, and begins the account of the Trojan princess Polyxena, whose beauty captivated Achilles.</summary>
<keywords>Penthesilea, Amazons, Hector, Trojan War, gender roles, Polyxena, Achilles, Neoptolemus, Priam</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Polyxena, the daughter of King Priam, who was used as a lure to kill the Greek hero Achilles. It continues from the previous account of her tragic role in the Trojan War and begins the biography of her mother, Queen Hecuba.</meta>

.34.

...him <note>Achilles</note>, whom through her mother Hecuba’s deception, she led alone at night into the temple of Thymbrean Apollo <note>A temple near Troy where Achilles was lured under the pretense of a marriage negotiation</note> to his death. Because of this—though she deserved it less than the cause—once the Trojan forces had collapsed and the battle was lost, she was led by Neoptolemus <note>The son of Achilles, also known as Pyrrhus, who sought revenge for his father's death</note> to his father’s tomb to serve as an offering to appease his ghost. There (if any great trust can be placed in literature), seeing the fierce youth drawing his sword in joy while everyone else standing around was weeping, the innocent girl offered her throat with such a steady heart and fearless expression that the minds of the beholders were moved no less by admiration for her bravery than by the duty owed to the parent <note>referring to Neoptolemus's perceived duty to avenge his father</note>. It is indeed a great and noteworthy thing that neither her tender age, nor her female sex, nor her royal upbringing, nor her changed fortune could crush the maiden's great spirit—and most especially under the sword of a victorious enemy, beneath which the courageous hearts of even distinguished men sometimes waver and very often fail. I would easily believe this was the act of a noble-born woman, done to show by her disregard for death what a great woman she might have become, had the fortune of the enemy not snatched her away so soon.
