--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographies of famous women, transitioning to the tragic figure of Hecuba, the Queen of Troy, following the traditional accounts of her life and the fall of her city.</meta>

[Large decorative initial **H**]**ecuba** was the most renowned queen of the Trojans, a shining example of fleeting splendor and the surest testament to human misery. According to some, she was the daughter of Dymas the Phrygian <note>original: "dimantis aonis"</note>. Others, however, maintain she was the child of Cisseus, King of Thrace, which I myself believe to be true since it is the opinion of many. As a maiden, she married Priam, the illustrious king of the Trojans, and by him she conceived and bore nineteen children of both sexes. Among them was that exceptional ray of Phrygian prowess, **Hector**; so great was the brilliance of his military service that he not only made himself radiant with eternal fame, but also ennobled his parents and his country with everlasting glory.

But she did not become so famous throughout the whole world because of the beauty of her happy kingdom or the serenity of her numerous offspring as she did when she encountered adverse fortune. She most mournfully wept for Hector, who was most dear to her, and the young **Troilus**—who already dared deeds beyond his strength—both slain by the hand of **Achilles**; by that slaughter, the solid foundation of the kingdom was nearly overturned. Likewise, she saw **Paris** slaughtered by Pyrrhus <note>Also known as Neoptolemus, the son of Achilles</note>; then **Deiphobus** mutilated, his ears and nose cut off, and foully killed; she saw Troy <note>original: "pylione", likely referring to Ilion/Troy</note> burned by the fire of the Greeks <note>original: "danao"</note>; and **Polites** stabbed in the very lap of his father, Priam. 

She pitiably witnessed the aged **Priam** himself disemboweled beside the domestic altars; her daughter **Cassandra**, her daughter-in-law **Andromache**, and herself dragged away as captives by the enemy; **Polyxena** slaughtered before the tomb of Achilles; and her grandson **Astyanax** snatched from his hiding place and dashed against a rock. And finally, she looked upon and wept for the young **Polydorus**, buried on the Thracian shore after being killed through the treachery of Polymestor <note>The King of Thrace, who murdered the boy for his gold</note>. 

Overwhelmed by so many and such immense sorrows, some say she turned to madness and howled through the Thracian fields like a dog; having died thus, she was buried in a tomb on the shore of the Hellespont, which was named *Cynossema* <note>original: "cynoſenia", meaning "The Dog's Tomb"</note> after her. Others say she was dragged into slavery with the rest, and so that no degree of misery should be lacking to her, she saw her daughter Cassandra finally slaughtered by order of **Clytemnestra** after **Agamemnon** had been killed.

## Concerning Cassandra, daughter of Priam, king of the Trojans. Chapter XXXIV.

->[Illustration: A woodcut depicting the violent fall of the city labeled **Troy**. In the center, a soldier drags the princess **Cassandra** by her hair while she kneels in prayer or supplication. A fluttering scroll above her reads: "Proud Ilium <note>Ilium is another name for the city of Troy.</note> falls."]<-

**C**assandra was the daughter of Priam, the king of the Trojans. To her indeed (as the ancient writers assert) belonged a mind for prophecy <note>original: *vaticinij mens*</note>. Whether this was acquired through her own studies, or given as a gift from a god, or rather achieved through some diabolical trickery, is not entirely certain. Nevertheless, this much is affirmed by many: that long before the abduction of Helen, the audacity of Paris, the arrival of the daughter of Tyndareus <note>referring to Helen of Troy, daughter of Tyndareus</note>, the long siege of the city, and finally the destruction of Priam and Troy itself, she had predicted these events often and with a clear voice. Because of this, and because no one would place any faith in her words, they say she was punished with lashings by her father and brothers. Furthermore, a fable was concocted from this <note>The "fable" Boccaccio refers to is the myth that Apollo gave Cassandra the gift of prophecy to win her love, but when she rejected him, he cursed her so that her true warnings would never be believed.</note>...

<summary>This page introduces Cassandra of Troy, describing her prophetic abilities and the skepticism she faced from her family despite her accurate warnings about the city's destruction.</summary>
<keywords>Cassandra, Troy, King Priam, prophecy, Trojan War, Helen of Troy, Boccaccio, Famous Women</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Cassandra and begins the entry for Clytemnestra (Chapter 34), detailing the aftermath of the Trojan War and the beginning of the conspiracy in Mycenae.</meta>

She <note>referring to Cassandra from the previous page</note>, moreover, had been betrothed to a certain noble youth named Coroebus, but she lost him in war before she could be received by him into the marriage chamber. And finally, as all things went to ruin, she fell as a captive to Agamemnon as his share of the spoils. While she was being led by him toward Mycenae, she prophesied to him the traps and the death prepared for him by Clytemnestra. Although no faith was placed in her words, after a thousand dangers of the sea, she arrived at Mycenae with Agamemnon; there, once he had been slain by the deceit of Clytemnestra, she herself was also slaughtered by the command of that same Clytemnestra.

### Concerning Clytemnestra, Queen of the Mycenaeans. Chapter 34.

[Woodcut illustration depicting the murder of Agamemnon. Labels within the image: "Clytemnestra", "Agamemnon", "Orestes" <note>transcribed as 'Dorestes'</note>, and "Aegisthus" <note>transcribed as 'Egillus'</note> written on the leg of the figure on the left.]

**C**lytemnestra was the daughter of King Tyndareus of Oebalia <note>original: "cebalie"; a poetic name for Sparta</note> and of Leda. She was the sister of Castor, Pollux, and Helen, and as a young maiden she married Agamemnon, the king of the Mycenaeans. Although she was distinguished enough by her noble birth and her marriage, she became even more famous for a wicked deed. For while her husband Agamemnon was in command of the Greek forces at Troy—and though she had already borne several children by him—she fell into a lustful passion for Aegisthus <note>original: "egisti"</note>. Aegisthus was a lazy and idle young man, the son of Thyestes by his daughter Pelopia, who abstained from bearing arms on account of his priestly office <note>The author suggests Aegisthus avoided the Trojan War by claiming religious duties</note>. And (as it pleases some to believe), through the persuasions of the old man Nauplius—the father of the late Palamedes—she came into his embrace and his bed.

<summary>This page recounts the tragic end of the prophetess Cassandra and begins the history of Queen Clytemnestra, focusing on her infidelity with Aegisthus while Agamemnon was at war.</summary>
<keywords>Cassandra, Agamemnon, Clytemnestra, Aegisthus, Mycenae, Trojan War aftermath, infidelity, Nauplius</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the aftermath of the Trojan War, focusing on the domestic tragedy of the House of Atreus. The text details the assassination of King Agamemnon by his wife Clytemnestra and her lover Aegisthus, and the subsequent vengeance of Orestes.</meta>

.36.

From which crime it followed—whether out of fear for the deed she had committed upon the return of Agamemnon, or by the persuasion of her lover and a lust for the kingdom, or from the indignation she felt because of Cassandra <note>The daughter of King Priam of Troy, whom Agamemnon took as a war prize and concubine.</note> whom Agamemnon was bringing to Mycenae—that this bold-spirited woman, with a mind armed for deceit and with rash daring, rose up against her husband. As he returned as the victor of Troy <note>original: "plij," a common transcription error for "Ilii" (Ilium/Troy).</note> and weary from the storms of the sea, she received him into the palace with a feigned joy upon her face. And (as it pleases some to say) while he was dining and perhaps already soaked with wine, she ordered him to be struck down by her lover, who emerged from an ambush. Others, however, say that while he lay resting, wrapped in garments sought as the spoils of victory—as if a feast for the Greeks were to be more illustrious—his treacherous wife calmly persuaded him to put on his former robes. She, acting boldly, handed him those very robes which she had prepared beforehand for this purpose, which lacked an opening for the head. And when the man had already thrust his arms into the sleeves and, entangled, was searching for where he might put out his head while thus half-bound, he was surrendered to the adulterous assassin <note>The Latin text here reads "sacerdote" (priest), which is likely a scribal error for "satellite" (accomplice) or "sicario" (assassin) common in other versions of this story.</note> at her urging; and thus, while no one was looking, he was struck down. Once this was done, she occupied the entire kingdom and ruled for seven years with her lover Aegisthus. Indeed, when in the meantime Orestes, the son of Agamemnon born of her—whom friends had secretly saved from the fury of his mother—had grown up, he took it into his heart to avenge the death of his father. Having chosen his time, he killed her along with the adulterer. I know not which to blame more: the crime or the audacity? First, the illustrious man did not deserve such a great evil. Second, the less it befitted a treacherous woman, the more abominable the act was. I have, however, something to praise: namely the <term>virtue</term> <note>In this context, "virtue" refers to the Roman concept of *virtus*—manly courage and the fulfillment of duty—rather than modern moral purity.</note> of Orestes. For a long time, he did not allow his sense of duty to be restrained by "piety" toward an unholy mother, but rather he boldly rushed forward to avenge the undeserved death of his father. As a son, he did to his ill-deserving mother what his less-deserving father had suffered at the hands of the adulterous priest, at the command of that shameful woman. And by their impious work, the father's blood had been spilled so that the crime might turn back upon its authors, and be atoned for by the spilling of blood.

<summary>This page recounts the assassination of Agamemnon by his wife Clytemnestra using a trap involving a headless robe, and the eventual revenge taken by their son Orestes.</summary>

# **Of Helen, wife of King Menelaus. Chapter 35.**

[Woodcut illustration showing a boat with four figures sailing from a city on the left to a city on the right. Labels above the elements: Sparta <note>original: "Lacedemonia"</note>, Helen, Paris, Troy.]

**H**elen—as much for her wantonness (as it has seemed to many) as for the long war that followed because of her—is a woman most well-known to the whole world. She was the daughter of Tyndareus, king of Oebalia <note>An ancient name for the region of Laconia or Sparta.</note>, and of Leda, a most beautiful woman; she was also the wife of Menelaus, king of the Spartans <note>original: "lacedemonū"</note>. Concerning her, as all the ancient Greeks (and the Latins after them) say, her beauty was so celebrated that Nature herself easily surpassed all her other works in this one woman. Indeed, it exhausted (to say nothing of others) that man of divine genius, Homer, before he could sufficiently and appropriately describe her in his poetry according to the rules of his craft. 

Furthermore, many excellent painters and sculptors all undertook the same labor, so that they might leave at least an image of such extraordinary beauty (if they were able) for posterity. Among these, Zeuxis of Heraclea <note>A famous Greek painter of the 5th century BCE, known for his realism.</note>, a most renowned painter preferred above all others, was hired by the people of Croton <note>A Greek colony in Southern Italy.</note> for a vast sum of money. He applied his entire genius and all the powers of his art to portraying her with his brush. Because he had no other model besides Homer's poem and her great fame everywhere, he frequently used his mind to conceive of her face and the rest of her physical stature based on those two sources. He decided to [gather] from many other beautiful forms a divine... <note>The text cuts off here; historically, Zeuxis is said to have selected the five most beautiful virgins of Croton to use as composite models for his painting of Helen.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Helen of Troy, focusing on her legendary beauty which challenged the descriptive powers of Homer and the artistic skills of the painter Zeuxis.</summary>
<keywords>Helen of Troy, Menelaus, Homer, Zeuxis, Sparta, Trojan War, Greek Mythology, beauty in art</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion of the famous Greek painter Zeuxis and his attempt to create a perfect representation of Helen of Troy by combining the best features of several models.</meta>

->.37.<-

...that he could perceive that image of Helen, and display it as requested by others; and having displayed it, he left it for posterity. First, he chose from among the most beautiful boys, and then from among their sisters, selecting those who were most remarkable for their exceptional grace. Having gathered a single form from the collective beauty of them all, and having exerted all the strength of his famous intellect, it is hardly fully believed that he was able to accomplish through art what he had desired. 

<note>This refers to the legendary method of the painter Zeuxis, who selected five of the most beautiful maidens of Croton to serve as models, combining their best features to depict the ideal beauty of Helen of Troy.</note>

Nor do I wonder at this; for who, with the brush or pigment of a painting or statue, could ever depict the joy of the eyes, the gentle kindness of the whole face, a heavenly smile, or the various and graceful movements of the countenance that match the quality of one's words and deeds—since this is the task of Nature <note>original: "nate," likely a contraction for "naturae"</note> alone? 

He did, therefore, what he was able to do, and he left what he had painted to posterity as a likeness of heavenly beauty. From this, sharper minds <note>The author suggests that more imaginative or critical thinkers expanded upon the historical account.</note> fashioned a story, and [claimed] they watched her with an eye <unclear>...</unclear>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Paris (also known as Alexander), the prince of Troy. It details his return to the royal family, his journey to Greece, the abduction of Helen, and the subsequent arrival of the Greek fleet at the shores of Troy.</meta>

...had been exposed on Mount Ida <note>original: "p̄dam," likely a scribal error for "Idam." In Greek myth, Paris was left on the mountain to die as an infant because of a prophecy.</note> and in a wrestling match had surpassed his brother Hector, though he was not recognized; and having shown certain tokens <term>crepundia (recognition tokens) — small objects or jewelry left with an exposed infant to identify them later</term>, he was recognized by his mother and thus escaped death. Mindful of the promise of the most beautiful woman offered to him by Venus as a result of his judgment on Mount Ida, or (as others would have it) intending to demand the return of Hesione <note>original: "helionā." Hesione was Priam's sister, who had been captured by the Greeks in an earlier conflict.</note>, he built ships from the timber of Ida. Accompanied by a royal retinue, he sailed across to Greece and was received with hospitality by Menelaus. 

When he beheld Helen, conspicuous for her celestial beauty and reveling in royal luxury—and she herself desiring to gaze upon him—he was immediately captivated. Taking hope from her behavior, and seizing the moment with eyes sparkling with fervor, he secretly instilled the fire of his love into her immodest breast. Fortune favored his undertaking, for as the opportunity arose, Menelaus left him behind and set out for Crete. 

For this reason, it pleases some to say that with their horses burning with flames from opposite sides <note>This likely refers to the "horses of the sun" or a metaphorical description of the passion driving the events.</note>, it happened that Paris carried into his fatherland the fire that Hecuba had seen in her dream during her rest, thus fulfilling the prophecies. With the greatest part of Menelaus's treasures, he snatched the wakeful Helen by night from the Laconian shore—or as others prefer, from the nearby island of Cythera—while she was in a certain temple according to an ancient rite for performing a sacrifice. He placed her upon his prepared fleet and, after many dangers, arrived with her at Troy <note>original: "tropam," a variant for Troia.</note>. There, she was received with the highest honor by Priam, who judged that he had wiped away the stain of injury regarding Hesione (who was held by Telamon) rather than believing he had brought the final desolation of his kingdom into his fatherland.

By the enticement of this woman, all of Greece was moved. When the Greek princes weighed the injury caused by Paris more heavily than Helen’s own wantonness—and after she had been fruitlessly demanded back many times—they unanimously swore to her internal destruction. Having gathered their forces from the cities with a thousand or more ships laden with armed men, they occupied the shore between the Sigean and Rhoetean promontories of Phrygia <note>Phrygia is the region in Asia Minor where Troy was located.</note> and besieged Troy, though the Phrygians resisted them. Helen, indeed, was able to see from the walls of the besieged city just how much her beauty was worth, as she looked out upon the entire shore filled with enemies, and...

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the Trojan War and the fate of Helen of Troy, following the narrative of her abduction by Paris. It concludes her biography and introduces the next figure, Circe.</meta>

.xxxvii.

Everything was laid waste by fire and sword; nations entered into certain struggles, and through mutual wounds, men went to their deaths. All things were defiled by both Trojan and Greek blood. She <note>Helen of Troy</note> was sought after and held back with such a stubborn purpose that, since she was not returned, a bloody siege persisted for ten years, resulting in the slaughter of many nobles. While this siege was ongoing—Hector and Achilles being already dead, and Paris having been slaughtered by the most fierce youth Pyrrhus <note>Original: "apirro"; Pyrrhus, also known as Neoptolemus, was the son of Achilles.</note>—as if it seemed to her a small thing to have sinned once, Helen entered into a second marriage and wed the younger Deiphobus <note>A son of Priam and brother to Hector and Paris.</note>.

Finally, since it appeared that what could not be obtained by arms should be attempted through betrayal, she who had provided the cause for the siege did not fail to assist in the destruction. To earn the favor of her first husband <note>Menelaus, King of Sparta.</note>, she willingly and knowingly joined the plot. When the Greeks had feigned their departure through trickery, the Trojans—terrified, exhausted by their labors, and overcome by new joy and festive feasts—were buried in sleep. Helen, pretending to lead a ritual dance <note>Original: "coreã"; likely a religious procession or choral dance used as a signal.</note>, recalled the waiting Greeks from the citadel by signaling with a lit torch at the opportune time.

Those who returned entered the half-sleeping city through the opened gates in silence. The city was burned, and Deiphobus was foully slain. Thus, twenty years after her abduction, Helen was restored to her husband Menelaus.

Others, however, assert that Helen was not snatched away by Paris of her own free will, and for that reason, she deserved to be received back by her husband. They say that while Menelaus was seeking to return to Greece with her, he was driven by a storm and contrary winds and forced to change his course toward Egypt, where he was received by King Polybus. After these storms were calmed, he finally returned to <term>Lacedaemon</term> <note>The ancient name for the region of Sparta.</note> with his recovered wife, nearly eight years after the destruction of Troy <note>Original: "plione," likely a transcription of "Ilione" or "Ilio," referring to Ilion/Troy.</note>. As for her, how long she lived after this, or what she did, or under what sky she died, I do not remember ever reading.

## Concerning Circe, daughter of the Sun, Chapter .xxxvi. <note>The manuscript numbering jumps from 37 back to 36 here; this may be an error in the original text or a specific organizational choice by the author.</note>

<summary>This page recounts the final fall of Troy through Helen's betrayal of the Trojans and her eventual return to Menelaus, noting conflicting accounts of her journey through Egypt.</summary>

<meta>This page begins a biographical or mythological entry on Circe, likely from a Renaissance or early modern compendium of famous women. It transitions from general history into the specific myths recorded by poets.</meta>

[Woodcut illustration: A woman in a long, flowing dress and an elaborate headdress, labeled **"Circe"** <note>original: "Circes"</note> on the left, gestures towards a group of men on the right. The foremost man has the head of a lion and is labeled **"Ulysses"** <note>original: "Ulixes"</note>. Behind him are men with the heads of a stag, a pig, and another animal. They are standing in a landscape with hills in the background.]

**C**irce, most famous even to this day for her incantations, as the songs of the poets testify, was the daughter of the Sun and the nymph Perse <note>original: "p̄se"</note>, a daughter of Oceanus, and the sister of Aeëtes, king of the Colchians. She was called the daughter of the Sun (as I believe) because she flourished with a singular beauty, or because she was highly skilled in the knowledge of herbs, or rather because she was most wise in her actions; all of these qualities the astrologers <term>mathematici (astrologers)</term> <note>In this period, "mathematici" usually referred to those who calculated horoscopes and studied the influence of stars.</note> believe the Sun grants to those being born under various aspects.

However, by what agreement she left Colchis and sought out Italy, I do not remember having read. For all histories testify that she inhabited the Aeaean mountain <note>Monte Circeo</note>, which we call Circeum after her name to this day. And since nothing but poetic fables are read concerning this so celebrated woman, after briefly reciting these poems, we shall examine—with whatever talent is available—the minds of those who believe them.

They [the poets] claim, therefore, above all else, that whatever sailors landed on the shores of the aforementioned mountain—which was once an island—whether by purpose or driven by the impulse of a storm, were converted into wild beasts of various species by the songs of her art or by cups tainted with poison. They claim that among these were the companions of the wandering Ulysses; Mercury intervening... <note>The text ends mid-sentence; "mēcum̄" is likely an OCR error for "mercurio," referring to the god Mercury providing the antidote to Circe's magic.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the mythological figure Circe, daughter of the Sun, explaining her fame as an enchantress and her geographical association with Monte Circeo in Italy.</summary>
<keywords>Circe, mythology, Sun, Ulysses, transformation, Monte Circeo, astrology, enchantments</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the myth of Circe and Ulysses, following Ulysses' success in resisting her enchantments through the aid of Mercury.</meta>

39

...by following [Mercury's] advice; who, when he threatened the sorceress with a drawn sword, is said to have restored his companions to their original form. Having shared her bed for a time, they say he fathered a son, Telegonus <note>The text reads "Theologonū," likely a scribal variant for Telegonus, the son of Ulysses and Circe in Greek mythology.</note>, by her, and departed from her full of wisdom. I believe these meanings lie hidden beneath the "bark" <note>original: "sub cortice"; a common medieval metaphor meaning the outer layer of a story that hides a deeper allegorical truth.</note> of these stories. 

There are those who say this woman was very powerful in strength and speech not far from Gaeta, a town in Campania. They say she cared for nothing as long as she obtained what she desired, yet she preserved her reputation for chastity from public scandal. Thus, many of those who arrived at her shore were drawn into her charms not only by flatteries and ornate speech, but she drove others into piratical plunder; some, setting aside all honesty, she incited to trade and commerce through deceits; and many others she raised up in pride because of her singular affection for them. 

And so, to those who saw human reason stripped away by the works of this ill-fated woman, it was rightly believed they had been turned by her into the beasts of her own making. From this, we can sufficiently understand, by looking at the morals of men and women, that there are many "Circes" everywhere, and far more men turned into beasts by their own lust and crime. Ulysses, however, thoroughly instructed by the advice of Mercury <note>Mercury often represents eloquence or reason in these allegories.</note>, clearly represents the prudent man whom the snares of flatterers cannot trap; by his spirit and teachings, he often releases those already ensnared from their bonds. The rest is clear enough as belonging to history, by which it is certain that Ulysses remained with Circe for some time.

It is further reported that this same woman was the wife of Picus, son of Saturn and King of the Latins, and that she taught him the science of <term>augury</term> <note>The practice of interpreting omens from the flight and song of birds to predict the future.</note>. Out of jealousy, because he loved the nymph Pomona, she transformed him into a bird of his own name. For he was the "picus" <note>original: "picus"; the Latin word for a woodpecker.</note>, a familiar bird from whose song and behavior he used to take omens regarding the future. And because he led his life according to the actions of the woodpecker, he was said to have been turned into one. When or by what kind of death, or where this Circe died, I have not discovered.

<summary>This page provides a rationalized and moral interpretation of the Circe myth, suggesting she was a powerful woman whose "magic" was actually persuasive speech and beauty that led men to abandon reason. It also connects her to the legend of King Picus, whom she allegedly turned into a woodpecker out of jealousy.</summary>

**[C]amilla** <note>The "C" is missing in the transcription, likely intended for a large decorative initial.</note> was a distinguished virgin and most worthy of being remembered; she was a queen of the Volscians. <note>The Volscians were an ancient Italic tribe often in conflict with early Rome.</note> She was the daughter of Metabus, a very ancient king of the Volscians, and his wife Casmilla. Her birth was the cause of her mother’s death, for as soon as she had given birth to the little girl, she died. In his own grief, her father Metabus named the daughter Camilla, deriving the name from the mother’s name by removing only a single letter, the "s." <note>original: "Casmilla" becomes "Camilla".</note> Indeed, this maiden’s fortune was harsh from her very birthday. For a little while after the mother’s funeral, Metabus was driven from his kingdom by a sudden rebellion of his leading citizens. Fleeing in haste, he was able to carry nothing into exile with him except this little daughter, whom he loved more than all other things. 

As he fled, wretched, alone, and on foot, carrying his daughter Camilla in his arms, he came to the Amaseno River, <note>A river in the Lazio region of Italy.</note> which was swollen by heavy rains. Because he was burdened by the weight of the infant, he was unable to swim across. However, he came upon a timely plan provided by God, <note>original: "deo". Boccaccio often uses the singular "God" even when discussing pagan myths, reflecting the medieval Christian perspective of the author.</note> who did not wish for this future famous maiden to be overtaken by an ignoble fate. Therefore, he wrapped her in the bark of a cork tree and bound her to a spear which he happened to be carrying; and from that side...

...rumor showed that the others had been dashed by a sea storm, driven onto foreign shores, swallowed by the waves, or that a few had been received back in their homeland; but for Ulysses alone, it was uncertain what course his ships had taken. In her pitiable belief of this, his mother Anticlea ended her life with a noose to alleviate her grief <note>Ancient traditions regarding the death of Anticlea vary; while the Odyssey suggests she died of a broken heart, other medieval and early modern accounts specifically mention suicide by hanging</note>.

Penelope, however, although she suffered greatly from her husband’s absence, bore the painful and ill-omened suspicion of his death for a long time. But after many tears, and having very often called for Ulysses in vain, she resolved with a firm mind to grow old in most chaste and perpetual widowhood, living between the aged Laertes <note>Ulysses's father</note> and the boy Telemachus <note>Ulysses's son</note>.

Truly, since her becoming beauty, her commendable character, and her excellent lineage had provoked the minds of many nobles from Thrace <note>original: "trachia"</note>, Cephalonia, and Aetolia to love and desire her, she was greatly vexed by their constant demands. For as the daily hope for Ulysses’s life or his return seemed to diminish continually, it came to this: that while his affairs were wasting away due to the arrogance of the suitors, the noblemen occupied Ulysses's very palace. They urged Penelope with prayers and persuasions with all their might—and very frequently—to join them in marriage.

But the woman, fearing lest she might violate the purpose of her sacred heart, and seeing that the path of simple refusal was being taken away from her, was enlightened by a divine light. She judged that these hateful men should be deceived, at least for a time, by cleverness. She requested of those pressuring her that she be allowed to wait for her husband only until she could finish the web <term>shroud: a burial cloth intended for her father-in-law, Laertes</term> which she had begun according to the custom of royal women. 

When the competitors had easily granted this, the excellent woman, by feminine craft, would secretly unweave at night by pulling back the threads whatever she was seen to weave by day with skillful zeal. By this art, she mocked them for some time while they consumed Ulysses's goods in the palace with constant feasting. ## Concerning Lavinia, Queen of Laurentum, Chapter 39

[Woodcut illustration: In a wooded setting, a man labeled **ASCANIUS** stands on the left, gesturing toward a woman labeled **LAVINIA** who is resting on the ground. A small child labeled **SILVIUS** lies near her. Trees fill the background.]

**Lavinia, queen of Laurentum**, was the only daughter of King Latinus and his wife Amata. She was a descendant of Saturn of Crete <note>In mythology, Saturn was a Titan who fled to Italy after being deposed by Jupiter</note>. Ultimately, she became the wife of **Aeneas**, the most valiant leader of the Trojans. She is far more famous for the war fought between Aeneas and Turnus of the Rutulians <note>A local tribe in ancient Italy</note> than for any specific deed of her own.

Indeed, because of her remarkable beauty and the fact that she was the heir to her father’s kingdom, she was most insistently sought in marriage by **Turnus**, king of the Rutulians, a most passionate young man. Her mother, Amata, had encouraged his hopes, for she strongly favored him out of a desire for a grandson. 

However, Latinus was skilled in **augury** <term>The ancient practice of interpreting the will of the gods by observing the flight and behavior of birds</term>. Having received an oracle <note>A divine prophecy</note> stating that his daughter must be given to a foreign leader, he was slow to agree to his wife’s wishes. On the contrary, when **Aeneas** arrived as a refugee from Troy, Latinus—both because of the nobility of Aeneas's lineage and the warning of the oracle—promised his daughter to him when he requested a peaceful alliance. For this reason, war was stirred up between Aeneas and Turnus. After many struggles, the Trojans prevailed through the wounds, blood, and deaths of many nobles; by Aeneas in Lavinia's <note>The text ends mid-sentence; "in lauinie" likely refers to Aeneas winning the right to marry her or naming a city after her.</note>

<summary>This page recounts the legend of Lavinia, daughter of King Latinus, whose hand in marriage was contested by the local King Turnus and the Trojan hero Aeneas, leading to a bloody war dictated by prophecy.</summary>
<keywords>Lavinia, Aeneas, Turnus, Latinus, Amata, Troy, Rutulians, Roman Mythology, Laurentum, Augury</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Lavinia, the wife of Aeneas, following the death of her mother Amata and her husband Aeneas. It transitions into the biography of Dido, the founder of Carthage.</meta>

...the wedding proceeded; however, her mother Amata, having already died by the noose out of indignation <note>Amata, the mother of Lavinia, committed suicide because she opposed her daughter's marriage to Aeneas, preferring the local prince Turnus.</note>. There are those, nonetheless, who claim a war arose after the wedding. However this may have happened, it is certain that Lavinia conceived a son by Aeneas, that most famous prince. After he was removed from human affairs <note>original: "rebis humanis subtracto"; a euphemism for death. Aeneas was said to have vanished or died during a battle at the river Numicus.</note> at the river Numicus before the day of the birth, Lavinia, fearing her reigning stepson Ascanius, withdrew into the woods. There she gave birth to a <term>Postumus</term> <note>In Roman custom, a "Postumus" is a child born after the death of the father.</note> and, as some would have it, named him Julius Silvius.

Indeed, since Ascanius was believed to be more gentle toward his stepmother than feared, and had founded for himself the city of Alba, he withdrew of his own accord and left the paternal kingdom to Lavinia. Lavinia, bearing an ancestral nobility in her heart and living honestly and modestly, held that kingdom with the greatest diligence. She preserved it for a long time, until she resigned it, in no way diminished, to her son Silvius when he reached puberty. Some claim, however, that she was recalled from the woods and married a certain Melampus, and that Silvius was raised by Ascanius with brotherly benevolence.

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Dido (also known as Elissa), the legendary founder of Carthage, following her flight from Tyre after the murder of her husband Sychaeus.</meta>

->44<-

...[she acted] so that no one would suspect she was about to do something great. She purchased from the inhabitants of the land on the shore only as much land for herself to establish a seat as could be covered by the hide of an ox. O, the cunning of the woman! By her command, the bull’s hide was cut and torn into narrow strips joined together; it enclosed far more space than the sellers could have imagined. Following the omen of a horse's head <note>The discovery of a horse's head while digging was interpreted as a sign that the city would be wealthy and powerful in war</note>, she founded a warlike city, which she named **Carthage**!

She called the citadel **Byrsa** <term>Byrsa: a Greek word meaning "hide" or "skin," referencing the trick used to claim the land</term> after the bull's hide. When she had revealed the treasures she had hidden through trickery and had encouraged her companions with the great hope of flight, walls, temples, a forum, and both public and private buildings immediately arose. After she had given laws and a standard of living to the people, the city suddenly emerged as excellent and remarkable. The renown of her beauty, her unseen and unheard-of virtue, and her chastity was carried throughout all of Africa. 

Because of this (since African men are most prone to lust), it happened that the king of the *Musicani* <note>original: "musicanorum rex"; likely referring to Iarbas, king of the Maxitani or a similar North African tribe</note> fell into a longing for her. He demanded her in marriage from certain leaders of the city, delivering a threat of war and the destruction of the rising city if she were not given to him. When the leaders heard this—knowing the widowed queen’s sacred and inflexible vow of chastity, and fearing for themselves the coming battle—they worried they would be swallowed up in a disastrous war by frustrating the suitor. They did not dare to explain to Dido what was being asked when she questioned them; instead, they thought to deceive the queen and lead her to their own desired conclusion. 

They told her that the king desired to reduce his wild barbarism to more humane manners through their teaching, and for this reason, under the threat of war, he demanded teachers from among them. However, they claimed to be uncertain which of them would be willing to assume such a burden: to leave their homeland and go to live with such a savage king. The queen did not perceive their trickery; on the contrary, turning to them, she said: "Excellent citizens, what sluggishness is this? What indolence? Do you not know that we are born for our parents and our country? No one can rightly be called a citizen who, for the sake of public safety, would refuse death if the situation required it, much less [discomfort]..."

<summary>Dido founds Carthage using the "bull's hide" trick to secure land and is later pressured into a marriage proposal by a local king, which her advisors try to trick her into accepting by appealing to her sense of civic duty.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a moral argument regarding widowhood and chastity, specifically using the historical/legendary figure of Dido as an example of a woman who chose death over breaking a vow of faithfulness to her deceased husband.</meta>

...she would enter into marriage, or whether she would allow her most venerable vow of observance to be violated. I think some women will say—since our women are most clever at making excuses—"It had to be done; I was left destitute by the death of my parents and brothers; <term>suitors</term> <note>original: "procatore," referring to those seeking marriage</note> were insistent with their flatteries; I could not resist; I am made of flesh, not iron." O ridiculous! Consider Dido: under whose protection did she trust, she who was an exile and whose only brother was her enemy? Did not Dido have many suitors? And was Dido herself made of stone or wood any more than women today? Indeed not. Therefore, being strong at least in spirit, she—who did not think she could avoid those allurements—avoided them by dying in the only way she could. <note>In the older tradition followed by authors like Boccaccio and Petrarch, Dido committed suicide to remain faithful to her first husband, Sychaeus, rather than being forced to marry King Iarbas—contrary to Virgil’s story of her and Aeneas.</note> But for us, who call ourselves so devout, if Christ is our refuge, is not the pious Redeemer himself always present for those who hope in Him? Or do you think He—who snatched the youths from the <term>fiery furnace</term> <note>A reference to Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego from the Book of Daniel</note>, who liberated Susanna from the false charge <note>The biblical story of Susanna, who was nearly executed after being falsely accused of adultery by two elders whose advances she rejected</note>—cannot take you from the hands of flatterers if you wish? Cast your eyes to the ground and plug your ears, and like a cliff, repel the oncoming waves; and remaining unmoved, let the winds blow—you will be saved. Perhaps another woman will rise up, saying: "I had fields stretching far and wide, a splendid house with royal furnishings, and an ample possession of riches; I desired to become a mother so that such great wealth would not pass to strangers." O insane desire! Did not Dido also have a kingdom without children? Were there not royal riches, yet she refused to become a mother? For she most wisely judged that nothing would be more foolish than to destroy yourself in order to build up another. Shall I therefore stain my <term>chastity</term> <note>original: "castimonia"</note> so that I may provide a possessor—or what happens most often, a destroyer—for my fields, my splendid house, and my furnishings? Even if you have vast riches—which should certainly be spent, not thrown away—are there not many of Christ’s poor? By giving to them, you build eternal palaces for yourself; by giving to them, you illuminate your chastity with a different kind of brilliance. Furthermore, there are friends, than whom there are no more suitable heirs, since you have chosen them for the qualities you yourself have proven. But as for sons, you will have them not as you wish, but as nature grants.

<summary>This page presents a moral defense of chaste widowhood, arguing against common excuses for remarriage such as loneliness or the need for heirs. It cites the legendary Dido as a pagan model of virtue and contrasts her with the spiritual protection offered by Christ to Christian women.</summary>

--- Page 99 ---
And a third woman will come forward, asserting that she had to act as she did because her parents ordered it, her blood relatives compelled her, and her in-laws persuaded her. As if I were unaware! It was nothing if not her own concupiscence <note>The Latin word *concupiscentia* refers to strong sexual desire or lust, often viewed as a spiritual trial in medieval theology.</note> that urged her; her own unbridled passion commanded it first. All those aforementioned excuses are in vain. With a single refusal, Dido chose to die rather than live unchastely; yet this woman, so that she might live "chastely," claimed she could not refuse marriage.

There will also be one woman—more clever than the rest in her own judgment—who says: "I was a fervent youth and (as you know) I could not restrain the impulses of my youth. I followed the counsel of the **Doctor of the Gentiles** <term>Doctor of the Gentiles: A traditional title for Saint Paul the Apostle, referring to his mission to non-Jewish people.</term> when he said: *it is better to marry than to burn.*" <note>original: "melius est nubere quam uri" — a famous quote from 1 Corinthians 7:9.</note>

Oh, how well said! As if I were only demanding chastity from those advanced in years! Or was Dido not a young girl when she steeled her mind for chastity? <note>The author is referring to the older version of the Dido myth, where she commits suicide to remain faithful to her deceased husband rather than be forced into a new marriage.</note> O, heavenly deed! That holy counsel from Paul was not given so that it might be basely alleged in defense of a crime. Can I gradually restore exhausted strength with food? And can I not likewise lessen superfluous strength through abstinence?

...if I have been carried away by passion somewhat beyond my intended purpose, I beg those who read to forgive me, and let us return to the point from which we turned aside. Therefore, the citizens, with public tears and sorrow, performed the funeral rites for the lifeless Dido not only with human but even with divine honors. They buried her magnificently according to their means, honoring her not merely as a public mother and queen, but as a renowned deity who favored them constantly so long as Carthage stood. They worshipped her with altars, temples, and specialized sacrifices.

## Of Nicaula, Queen of the Ethiopians. Chapter 41.

[Woodcut illustration showing Queen Nicaula meeting King Solomon. Labels: NICAULA, SALM]

The furthest reaches of the Ethiopian wilderness <note>original: "Ethiopum barbarie"</note> produced **Nicaula** <note>Nicaula is the name used by the historian Josephus for the biblical Queen of Sheba.</note>; she is indeed all the more worthy of memory because she shone more splendidly for having arisen among such uncultivated customs. There, she abounded with such a great wealth of riches that she is believed to have surpassed almost all other mortals in this life. Among these riches...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pamphile of Kos's invention of silk-weaving before transitioning to the life of Rhea Ilia, the mother of Romulus and Remus. It maintains the chronological lineage of the Alban kings following the Trojan War.</meta>

Whoever discovers something demonstrates no small measure of natural talent, and depending on the quality of the achievement, they should be granted a certain degree of glory. Thus, those authors who are held in high regard maintain that she <note>This refers to Pamphile of Kos, the subject of the previous chapter</note> was the first to collect the silk-moths <term>silk-moths: The Latin 'bombice' refers to the cocoons or the moths of the silkworm</term> fluttering from the bushes, and to clean them of excess material with a comb. Once they were cleaned, she placed them upon the distaff <note>A tool used in spinning to hold the unspun fibers</note> and taught how to draw thread from them and weave it. Thus, she introduced a use for silk that had been unknown until that time. The reasoning she devised for this shows how greatly **Pamphile** must have excelled in other endeavors.

->### Of Rhea Ilia, the Vestal Virgin Chapter 42<-

[Woodcut illustration: The image is divided into two parts. On the left, a woman labeled **RHEA ILIA** rests in a grand, canopied bed. On the right, the scene shifts to the origin story of Rome: a man casts two infants, labeled **REMUS** and **ROMULUS**, into a river. Below them, on the riverbank near a city labeled **ROMA**, a wolf is shown nursing the two children.]

**Rhea Ilia** shone among the famous women of old for the exceptional nobility of her blood. She traced her lineage through the Silvian kings of Alba Longa, who ruled in succession and were descended from **Aeneas**, the renowned leader of the Trojans. Her father was **Numitor**, the noble king of the Albans. While she was still a young maiden, it happened that **Amulius**, Numitor’s younger brother—driven by a lust for power and disregarding the common laws of nations—violently stripped Numitor of his kingdom. Yet, a sense of brotherly duty <note>original: "pietas"</note> restrained him from committing further cruelty against his brother; he was content to banish Numitor to the countryside to live a life of private retirement.

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Alban kings, specifically the usurpation of the throne by Amulius and his treatment of Numitor's lineage.</meta>

The youth Lausus, however, the son of Numitor, he <note>Amulius</note> dispatched with a cruel spirit, viewing him as a rival for the love of the kingdom. Having slain him, he spared Lausus's sister, Ilia <note>Also known as Rhea Silvia</note>, who was still a young girl. The King, in order to strip her of all hope of marriage and offspring, added her to the Vestal Virgins, forcing her to profess perpetual virginity. When she had reached a more mature age—driven by the stings of Venus <note>The goddess of love; here meaning sexual desire</note>—it is not known by what means, but her swelling womb revealed that she had come into the embrace of a man. Having become pregnant, she gave birth to Romulus and Remus, the parents of the Roman city, in one and the same delivery. Because of this crime, even though she was of royal blood, the sons were exposed <note>The practice of abandoning infants to the elements</note> according to ancient custom and the King's command, and she herself was buried alive. Truly, although her body was overwhelmed by the earth, the extraordinary work of her sons raised her name to the highest peak.

### Concerning Sappho, the Girl of Lesbos and a Poet. Chapter 45.

->[Woodcut illustration titled "SAPHOS" depicting a woman seated and playing a lute-like instrument. She is surrounded by books on a table and various musical instruments on the floor, including a harp and a horn. In the background, a doorway shows two figures embracing.]<-

**S**appho of Lesbos was a girl from the city of Mytilene; no further record of her ancestry has been left to future generations. Yet truly, if we examine her dedicated studies, we will see partially restored what the passage of many years has taken away: specifically, that she was born to honorable and distinguished parents. For a lowly or base soul could never have desired such things, nor could a common person have attained them. 

This woman—though we do not know the exact era in which she flourished—possessed such a noble mind that, while in the bloom of her youth and beauty, she was not satisfied merely with knowing how to join letters together <note>A common way of describing the basic act of writing or literacy.</note>. Driven by a great fervor of spirit and a lively intellect, she applied herself to rigorous and wakeful study. She climbed the steep and high summit of Mount Parnassus <note>A mountain in Greece traditionally considered the home of the Muses and the center of poetry.</note> and, with bold success and the Muses <note>The nine goddesses who presided over the arts and sciences.</note> welcoming her, she joined their company. Wandering through the laurel groves, she made her way into the very cave of Apollo <note>The god of music, poetry, and prophecy.</note>. Having bathed in the waters of the Castalian Spring <note>A spring on Parnassus where poets drank to receive divine inspiration.</note>, she took up the plectrum <note><term>plectrum</term>: a small flat tool used to pluck the strings of a lyre or lute</note> of Phoebus <note>original: "phebi"; another name for Apollo</note> among the sacred nymphs...

<summary>This page concludes a chapter on domestic virtue and introduces the biography of Sappho, emphasizing her noble spirit and her intellectual journey to master the art of poetry.</summary>
<keywords>Sappho, Lesbos, Mytilene, Parnassus, Muses, Apollo, poetry, education of women, Castalian Spring</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Sappho, the Greek lyric poet, focusing on her musical innovation and her legendary unrequited love, before transitioning to the story of Lucretia.</meta>

.48.

While others were leading the dance, this young woman did not hesitate to strike the strings of the lyre with its ringing sound and express melodies—tasks which, indeed, seemed very difficult even to the most studious men. To be brief: through her own dedication, she reached such a height that even to this day her most famous poetry shines through the testimony of the ancients. A bronze statue was erected in her honor, dedicated in her name, and she herself was numbered among the celebrated poets. This splendor is certainly no less bright than the crowns of kings, the headbands <term>headbands</term> <note>original: "infule", sacrificial or ceremonial head-dress worn by priests</note> of priests, or even the laurels of those celebrating triumphs.

However, if we are to believe the accounts, just as she studied with great success, she was also seized by an unhappy love. Whether because of his wit, his beauty, or some other grace, she was overcome by an unbearable passion for a certain young man. When he did not accommodate her desire, she is said to have sung mournful verses while groaning at his stubborn hardness. I would have thought these were <term>elegies</term> <note>poems of lamentation often written in alternating lines of different lengths</note>, as elegiac verse is usually assigned to such subject matter, had I not read that she—scorning the forms of previous poems as if they were outdated—invented a new kind of meter. This meter proceeds with feet different from the others and is still called <term>Sapphic</term> <note>a specific four-line poetic form that Sappho famously used</note> after her name.

But why should the <term>Pierides</term> <note>another name for the Muses, the goddesses of music and inspiration</note> seem at fault? If the lyre-playing of <term>Amphion</term> <note>a mythological musician said to have moved stones into place to build the walls of Thebes through song</note> could move the very Ogygian <note>an ancient name for Theban</note> stones, why were they unwilling to soften the heart of a young man when Sappho sang?

LUCRETIA • SEXTUS • COLLATINUS • BRUTUS

<meta>This page begins Chapter 46 of the manuscript, following the previous accounts of famous women. It introduces Lucretia, a central figure in Roman legendary history whose story marks the transition from the Roman Kingdom to the Republic.</meta>

# On Lucretia, the wife of Collatinus. Chapter 46.

**L**ucretia was an outstanding leader of Roman chastity and the most holy ornament of ancient frugality <note>original: "parsimonie," referring here to the Roman ideal of a simple, modest lifestyle free from excess</note>. She was the daughter of Spurius Lucretius Tricipitinus, a most illustrious man among the Romans, and the wife of Tarquinius Collatinus, the son of Egerius (who was the brother of Tarquinius Priscus). It is uncertain whether she appeared more beautiful among Roman matrons for her physical beauty or for the integrity of her character. 

While Tarquin the Proud <note>Tarquinius Superbus, the seventh and final King of Rome, known for his tyrannical rule</note> was besieging the city of Ardea, Lucretia had retired to her husband’s house in the town of Collatia, not far from the city. It happened that in the camp, as the siege dragged on, the royal youths were dining—among whom was Collatinus—and as they happened to be warmed by too much wine, a conversation arose about the virtue of their wives. And since each man (as is the custom) praised his own wife above the others, they reached this plan: to mount swift horses and see for themselves what duties their wives, unaware of their coming, were engaged in at night while the men were waging war; thus they would determine the most worthy by the evidence of their own eyes. 

Indeed, when the Romans found the royal daughters-in-law <note>The wives of the sons of King Tarquin</note> amusing themselves among their peers, they turned their horses and came to Collatia. There they found Lucretia busy with wool-working <term>lanificio: the traditional Roman art of spinning and weaving wool, which symbolized a wife's diligence, domesticity, and virtue</term> among her maidservants, unadorned by any finery; for this reason, she was judged the most praiseworthy of all in the judgment of everyone present. 

Collatinus kindly welcomed the other young men into his home. While they were being entertained there, Sextus, the son of Tarquin the Proud, cast lewd eyes upon the integrity and beauty of this chaste woman. Inflamed by a wicked fire, he resolved in a secret plan to take possession of her beauty by force, if it were not otherwise granted to him. Not many days later, driven by his madness, he secretly left the camp and came to Collatia at night. There (because he was a kinsman of her husband) he was courteously received and honored by Lucretia. After he felt the whole house was silent and believed everyone was asleep, he entered Lucretia's bedroom with a drawn sword and revealed who he was, threatening her with death if she let out a sound.

<summary>This page introduces Lucretia, the Roman paragon of virtue, and describes how a drunken boast among soldiers led to a contest of wives. Her superior modesty attracts the unwanted and predatory attention of Prince Sextus Tarquinius, who later returns to assault her.</summary>
<keywords>Lucretia, Tarquinius Collatinus, Sextus Tarquinius, Roman chastity, Ardea, Collatia, wool-working, Roman Republic, Tarquin the Proud</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page concludes the narrative of Lucretia, a Roman noblewoman whose story was a central legend in the founding of the Roman Republic. It details her response to her violation by Sextus Tarquinius and her subsequent suicide, which sparked the revolution against the Roman kings. It then begins a new chapter on Queen Tomyris.</meta>

->. 50.<-

or if she would not yield to his will. When he saw her resisting his desire and unafraid of death, he turned to a damning trick, saying that he would kill her alongside a servant of his, and would tell everyone he had slain her after catching her in the act of adultery with the slave. Upon hearing this, the woman stood trembling, terrified by such a filthy scandal. She feared that if she died in that manner, no one would remain to clear her name <note>The Latin 'purgatorem sue innocentie' literally means 'a cleanser/purifier of her innocence'—someone to testify that she was framed.</note>, and for this reason, though her soul loathed it, she surrendered her body to the adulterer.

When he had satisfied his seductive lust and departed, victorious in his own eyes, Lucretia—sickened by so wicked a crime <note>original: "celesti facinoris," likely a medieval spelling of "scelesti," meaning wicked or heinous.</note>—at the breaking of dawn, ordered her father Tricipitinus <note>Spurius Lucretius Tricipitinus, a high-ranking Roman official.</note> and Brutus, the kinsman of [her husband] Collatinus—who until that day had been considered a fool <note>Brutus famously feigned idiocy to survive the reign of the tyrant Tarquin the Proud; his name 'Brutus' literally means 'Dullard.'</note>—and her other relatives and her husband to be summoned immediately. When they arrived, she recounted in order and with tears what Sextus had done to her in the dead of night. And while her relatives tried to comfort her as she wept miserably, she drew a knife she had hidden under her garment and said: "I, though I absolve myself of the sin, do not free myself from the punishment; nor shall any unchaste woman live hereafter by Lucretia's example."

With these words, she plunged the blade into her innocent breast, and falling upon the wound in the sight of her husband and father, she collapsed as she died; and before long, she poured out her soul with her blood. Her beauty was indeed unfortunate; but her chastity, never sufficiently praised, must be extolled with worthy proclamations, all the more because it was so sharply expiated <note>To 'expiate' here means to make amends or purify the shame through her sacrifice.</note> after the shame of the assault. Since by this act not only was her honor restored—which that foolish youth <note>Referring to Sextus Tarquinius, the King's son.</note> had tried to ruin with his foul crime—but Roman liberty followed.

<meta>This page begins the biography of Tomyris, Queen of the Scythians, following the common medieval tradition of detailing famous women of antiquity.</meta>

[Illustration: A woodcut depicting the story of Tomyris and Cyrus the Great. A woman, labeled **TOMYRIS**, is shown placing a severed head into a large vessel or bag. To the right stands a man in full plate armor, labeled **CYRUS**. In the background, soldiers are visible, and in the foreground, several dead bodies lie on the ground.]

[Labels in illustration:]
**TOMYRIS**
**CYRUS**

[Main text:]

**T**omyris was the illustrious queen of the Scythians. Because these Scythians live in a barren land and under a cold region of the sky, connected to the Riphean and Hyperborean mountains <note>The Riphean and Hyperborean mountains were legendary ranges in ancient geography, believed by the Greeks and Romans to be located in the far north of Eurasia.</note>, they were known almost only to themselves. For this reason, it is unknown from which parents Tomyris was born, or to whom she was joined in marriage. Therefore, she is conspicuous only insofar as she is noted for her distinguished nobility, having ruled over fierce and untamed peoples, and for her stand against the one who then held the kingdoms of Asia <note>original: "pro iam asye regna tenente," referring to Cyrus the Great, founder of the Achaemenid Empire.</note>. 

This man—perhaps so that Tomyris might become more widely known—was seized by a desire for the Scythian kingdom, seeking more to extol his own glory than to increase his empire. He had heard, indeed, that the Scythians were a poor and rustic people, but that they had been undefeated by the greatest kings up to that time. Driven by this greed, he led an army against Queen Tomyris, who was then a widow. 

When Tomyris learned of his arrival—even though he was formidable to all of Asia and nearly the whole world because of the scale of his deeds—she did not, however, seek a hiding place like a terrified woman, nor did she send a herald <term>caduceatore: a peace-making messenger who carried a "caduceus" or staff as a symbol of diplomatic immunity</term> to ask for terms of peace. On the contrary, having gathered her forces and having become a leader in the war, she realized she could resist with her full strength; she let him [enter] with all...

<summary>This page introduces Tomyris, the widowed queen of the Scythians, and describes her initial defiance of Cyrus the Great during his attempted conquest of her northern kingdom.</summary>
<keywords>Tomyris, Cyrus the Great, Scythia, Riphean Mountains, ancient warfare, women rulers, Persian Empire</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical account of the conflict between Cyrus the Great, King of Persia, and Tomyris, Queen of the Massagetae (a Scythian people).</meta>

->.51.<-

She allowed the army to cross the <term>Araxes<note>The Aras River, which formed a boundary between the Persian Empire and the Scythian lands.</note></term> and permitted them to enter her borders. This wise woman judged that Cyrus’s <note>original: "rabiem" (madness or rage)</note> fury could be far better defeated within the boundaries of her own land than outside of them. When she was informed that he had penetrated into the interior of the kingdom, she ordered her only son, a young man, to go out and face Cyrus in battle, granting him a third of her total forces.

Cyrus, however, had considered the nature of the terrain and the customs of these people, both through what he had heard and what he observed. Sensing that the youth was approaching with an army, he decided to conquer by trickery rather than by force of arms. He prepared a camp stocked with wine—which the <term>Scythians<note>A nomadic people of Central Asia; the text notes they were unfamiliar with wine, likely being more accustomed to fermented mare's milk.</note></term> did not yet know—and filled it with feasts and other delicacies. Then, leaving everything behind, he feigned a retreat.

When the young prince entered the empty camp, he acted as if he were already the victor and the enemy had been put to flight. Feeling joyful, he and his Scythians—as if invited to a banquet rather than a war—began to gorge themselves greedily on the food and the unfamiliar wine. As a result, their military discipline dissolved and they fell into a deep sleep. While they were buried in sleep, Cyrus returned and put the youth and the rest of his men to death. Then, as if certain of total victory, he proceeded further into the country.

But when <term>Tomyris<note>The Latin text spells her name "Tamiris." She was the legendary warrior queen of the Massagetae.</note></term> heard of the slaughter of her people, the widow was moved with great grief, especially because of the death of her only son...

<summary>This page describes a pivotal moment in the war between Cyrus the Great and Queen Tomyris, where Cyrus uses a deceptive "wine trap" to intoxicate and slaughter the Queen's son and his army.</summary>
<keywords>Cyrus the Great, Queen Tomyris, Scythians, Massagetae, Araxes River, military strategy, wine, deception, ancient warfare</keywords>

# Concerning Leena the Prostitute. Chapter 47.

->[Woodcut illustration: A woman labeled **LEENA** is tied to a wooden post. To her left, an executioner or torturer operates a mechanical winch or rack. To her right, two men dressed in long academic or judicial robes and hats stand and observe the scene.]<-

I believe Leena was a Greek woman. Even if she was less than chaste, I would still wish to describe her among famous women—with the permission of good, honorable matrons and illustrious queens. For, as I have said in previous sections, I promised to include women who became famous for any notable deed <note>original: "facinus" - can mean a great deed or a crime; the author uses it here to mean a significant action regardless of moral standing.</note>, not just those who were chaste. 

Furthermore, we are so indebted to virtue that we should not only elevate it when we see it placed in a distinguished position, but we ought to try to bring it into the light of merit even when it is buried by the stain of a crime. For virtue is precious everywhere, and it is no more stained by the contagion of vice than a sunbeam is polluted when it passes through filth <note>original: "ceno" - mud or filth. The author uses this powerful image to argue that a person's low social status or "sinful" profession does not diminish the purity of a brave or virtuous act.</note>. 

If, therefore, we ever see virtue fixed in a heart dedicated to a detestable trade <note>The author is referring to her life as a <term>meretrix</term>, a Latin term for a prostitute.</note>, we ought to loathe the trade in such a way that its praises do not diminish the virtue; indeed, virtue is all the more wonderful and worthy in such a person, as it was thought to be so much further removed from them. 

Therefore, the memory of prostitutes is not always to be despised. Rather, when they have made themselves worthy of remembrance through some merit of virtue, they are to be extolled with wider and more joyful praise, because in them, this desired virtue is clearly at work!

<summary>In this introductory passage, Boccaccio justifies the inclusion of Leena, a prostitute, in his book of famous women, arguing that true virtue is noble even when found in someone of low social or moral standing.</summary>
<keywords>Leena, virtue, prostitution, morality, Greek history, Boccaccio, Famous Women</keywords>

## Concerning Athalia, Queen of Jerusalem. Chapter 49.

[Woodcut illustration depicting a woman labeled **ATHALIA** on the left holding a scepter, a man in the center with a sword, a kneeling man, and a man on the right labeled **JOASH** holding a child and pointing away. Several bodies lie on the ground.]

**A**thalia’s savage mind made her more notorious to posterity <note>original: "posteris"; the OCR likely misread this as "spiritibus"</note> than was fitting for the lineage of David. Although her house was defiled on all sides by the blood of servants and manifold slaughter, she added no less ill-omened fame to her name than the brightness of royal crowns. She, then, was the daughter of Ahab, King of the Israelites, and Jezebel <note>Jezebel was a Phoenician princess in the Bible who encouraged the worship of Baal and became a symbol of wickedness.</note>, that most wicked woman; she was married to Joram, the son of Jehoshaphat, King of Jerusalem. 

And finally, after Jehoshaphat and Ahaziah <note>original: "ozia"</note>, the eldest son to whom the succession of the kingdom had passed upon his father's death, were removed from the scene, Joram her husband was unexpectedly crowned King of Jerusalem. He also desired his wife to be queen, for whom the splendor of Ahab...

<meta>This page continues the historical account of Athaliah, the daughter of Ahab and Jezebel, who became Queen of Judah. The previous narrative likely detailed her marriage into the House of David and the death of her husband, King Jehoram.</meta>

### .54.

Upon the death of her father, her brother Joram, who succeeded his father, did not bestow a small amount of glory <note>original: "lucis", literally 'light'</note> upon her. However, as time passed, having been previously tossed about by many misfortunes, after her husband was now deceased, she saw her son Ahaziah sitting on his father’s throne, so that the woman shone on all sides with royal honors. 

Indeed, when Ahaziah also went to his death after being struck by an arrow, the fierce woman was set on fire with a desire for the kingdom. When she had devised a memorable crime and gathered enough courage for its execution, she cast aside womanly piety. She not only failed to shed heart-felt tears for her son, but on the contrary—had she a womanly heart, she would have proceeded to even greater weeping—while her son's blood was still wet, she drew swords against all the descendants of the line of David. She raged against them for so long that she left no male among them who was not driven to his death by wounds. 

Only one child was withdrawn from her cruelty, though she did not notice: Joash, the little son of King Ahaziah. For Jehosheba <note>original: "iosabe"</note>, the daughter of the king and sister of the late Ahaziah, had secretly snatched the little boy away and carried him to the house of the High Priest <term>High Priest (pontificis)</term> Jehoiada, her husband, to be kept and raised. And so, through the blood of so many impiously slain, this bold woman—as if entering a possession left vacant by her own hand—dared to ascend the royal throne and arrange all royal affairs. 

Why should we wonder <note>original: "mũabim", likely "mirabimur"</note> at Atreus? Why at Dionysius? Why at Jugurtha? <note>These are infamous male tyrants of antiquity: Atreus of Greek myth, Dionysius of Syracuse, and Jugurtha of Numidia, all known for political killings.</note> We might hear that men of sharp intellect, drawn by a desire for power, reached the summit through the wounding of some of their kin; but here we see a woman who, to reach that point, stabbed all the royal offspring and did not even spare her own. 

Thus Athaliah shone with the royal diadem, though she was truly more notable for being splattered with purple blood than for the royal insignia. Indeed, just as she had fiercely raged with the sword against the innocent souls of David’s lineage, she was able to feel the same toward her own embittered relatives. Wishing to see a certain Joram, the King of Israel, her brother, lying in the field of Naboth and offering his blood to the dogs through a thousand wounds... <note>This refers to the biblical prophecy regarding the downfall of the House of Ahab, where his descendants' blood would be licked by dogs in the vineyard of Naboth.</note>

<summary>This page recounts the bloody rise of Athaliah to the throne of Judah, describing how she murdered the entire royal line of David, sparing only the infant Joash who was hidden by his aunt.</summary>

...could have easily happened. Thus also he saw **Jezebel**, the mother <note>Latin: *matrem*; Jezebel was the Queen Mother to Joram of Israel and a symbol of tyranny in the biblical narrative</note>, adorned in royal finery, cast down from a high tower and trampled by the feet of those running about, and even ground down by hooves and the tracks of wheels, so that, turned into mire, no trace of her ill-fated body remained. 

Thus also his **seventy brothers** were struck down at Samaria in a single hour by the victor's command <note>A reference to Jehu’s purge of the house of Ahab as recorded in 2 Kings 10</note>, and their heads, fixed on poles around the city of Jezreel, offered proof of the remarkable deed. Thus also all other kinsmen; not one escaped who was not pierced by the sword of that same executioner. 

And finally, lest that wicked woman of blood should pass unpunished, after she had reigned for seven years, through the work of **Jehoiada the high priest** <note>Latin: *Ioada pontificis*; the High Priest who led the revolt against Queen Athaliah</note>, she saw her grandson **Joash**—whom she herself believed had been killed with the rest—raised up as king and herself dragged from the royal throne. --- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues a moralizing discourse on the nature of power, specifically focusing on the heavy price and inherent instability of a throne seized through violence and injustice. It follows a previous section likely discussing the futility of worldly ambition.</meta>

.54.

...you cannot. What it finally means to obtain a throne must now be asked: now let the ears be turned away <note>original: "aūtas" / aversas</note> from tears, and the eyes from crimes and slaughters: let the heart be hard as stone: let credulity be armed, let piety be excluded: let reason be neglected: let injustice be cultivated: let power be taken away from sacred laws: let it serve lust: let malice be summoned: let simplicity be mocked: let rapacity, luxury, and gluttony be commended as the excellences of a famous king: let neither divine nor human things be spared; let the sacred and profane be mixed together: and with mercy cast down, let one wade through blood via the greatest wickedness: let pious men be struck down, let the impious be exalted: let virgins be raped: let free-born boys be dragged into abuse; let virtue be condemned, and let vice be forgiven: and with peace driven out everywhere, let discord hold its triumphs. 

O, what a "spectacular" accession for a king! But why go to a throne through blood and shameful deeds? O, would that, however it was sought, one might live there blamelessly! Instead, immediately, as suspicion presses, the leading men are driven into exile: the rich are reduced to poverty: old friends, brothers, sons, grandsons, and parents are banished; they are imprisoned and killed as if they were rivals. No faith, no holiness: let no justice live. He watches anxiously: he sleeps with difficulty: nor is food tasted without fear. And with the faithful driven away, his whole life is surrendered to the wicked. 

O beautiful, O desirable, O praiseworthy possession of authority—sought in such a way! <note>The author uses heavy irony here to describe the miserable life of a tyrant.</note> Indeed, it would have been better to have entered the tiny hut of a poor man, full of peace, full of security, strong and free from worry. For high positions are held with as much fear as the blood with which they were sought; and those faithful ones we abandoned because we suspected them, while we abandon ourselves to the unfaithful through the mediation of crime. It often happens that by the help of those unfaithful ones, just as our entries were, such—or even more unstable—are our exits; and in a single hour, along with our destruction, that which had been wickedly advanced through many unhappy days is snatched away. This, Athalia <note>Athaliah: A biblical queen of Judah (c. 841–835 BCE) who seized the throne by murdering the royal family, but was eventually overthrown and executed in a counter-coup.</note> was able to learn too late.

<summary>This page presents a biting rhetorical critique of tyranny, detailing the moral decay and constant state of fear experienced by those who seize power through violence. It concludes with the biblical example of Queen Athaliah to illustrate the inevitable and sudden downfall of such rulers.</summary>
<keywords>tyranny, kingship, justice, Athaliah, political philosophy, vice and virtue, instability of power</keywords>
