# Concerning Cloelia, a Roman Maiden. Chapter 50.

[Illustration: A group of women are shown crossing a river on horseback, while figures watch them from both banks. Above the central figure on horseback is the name: CLOELIA]

**Cloelia**, a distinguished Roman maiden, had parents whose origins were either not recorded by the ancients for posterity, or have been lost to the passage of time. However, it can be judged with certainty that she was born of noble stock, as the nobility of her spirit testifies to it—and because she was given as a hostage of peace, among other noble Roman women, to Porsenna <note>Lars Porsenna, an Etruscan king known for his war against the early Roman Republic</note>, the King of the Etruscans, during the war against Tarquin the Proud <note>Tarquinius Superbus, the legendary final king of Rome whose tyrannical rule led to the establishment of the Republic</note>.

In order to explain her praiseworthy boldness in greater detail, it must be noted that after King Tarquin the Proud had been expelled because of the enormous crime perpetrated by his son Sextus against Lucretia <note>The rape of the noblewoman Lucretia by the king's son was the catalyst for the Roman revolution</note>, open war broke out to prevent the King's deceptive attempts to return to power.

King Porsenna of Clusium <note>An ancient city in Etruria, modern-day Chiusi</note> joined this war, having been summoned by the pleas of Tarquin. After the Etruscans were prevented from crossing the river by the bravery of Horatius Cocles <note>A Roman hero famous for defending the Sublician Bridge alone against the entire Etruscan army</note> as he defended the Sublician bridge <note>The oldest bridge in Rome, built entirely of wood</note>, and after Porsenna had been terrified by the audacity and the clever plot of Mucius Scaevola <note>Gaius Mucius Scaevola, who famously thrust his right hand into a fire to prove his resolve after failing to assassinate Porsenna</note>, the King entered into a peace treaty with the Romans. To ensure this peace was maintained, he received several hostages, and so it happened that **Cloelia** was sent along with many other young women. To her, when perhaps—

<summary>This page introduces the Roman heroine Cloelia, explaining her noble character and the historical context of the war between the early Roman Republic and the Etruscan King Porsenna.</summary>

<keywords>Cloelia, Roman Republic, Lars Porsenna, Tarquin the Proud, Horatius Cocles, Mucius Scaevola, hostages, Roman history, female heroism</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the Roman maiden Cloelia, who was given as a hostage to the Etruscan king Lars Porsenna during his siege of Rome in the 6th century BCE.</meta>

.55.

She <note>Referring to Cloelia, a legendary Roman heroine</note> saw that it was beneath the dignity of the Republic for so many young women to be held by a foreign king; she armed her maidenly heart with a man’s courage. Having deceived the guards, she mounted a horse—which perhaps she had never ridden before—that was grazing along the Tiber. At night, after leading many of the other girls to the bank, she mounted the horse; not terrified by the depth of the river or the swirling waters, she led them all safely to the opposite side and restored them to their people. When this was discovered by Porsenna <note>Lars Porsenna, the Etruscan King</note> in the morning, as was the custom, it was ordered by a full Senate that the leader of the fugitives should be returned to the king upon his demand, with the condition added that she should be returned by the king to her people unharmed in due time. The King, however, admiring the maiden’s courage and delighted by her audacity, not only allowed her to return to her people but gave her the power to choose and lead away whichever of the remaining hostages she wished. From all of them, she chose only the young boys <note>original: "in pubes"; she chose those who had not yet reached puberty</note>, which seemed a commendable choice for a maiden’s honor; this was most welcomed because she had primarily liberated those of an age that seemed most vulnerable to injury <note>In this context, "injury" refers to the risk of physical or sexual abuse while in captivity</note>. For this reason, she was honored by the grateful citizens with an unusual distinction: an equestrian statue <term>equestrian statue: a monument depicting a person riding a horse, usually reserved for great military leaders</term> was granted to her, which was placed at the top of the Sacred Way <note>original: "via sacre"; the main street of ancient Rome, which served as the setting for triumphs and religious processions</note> and remained there untouched for a long time.

### Concerning Hippo, a Greek woman. Chapter 51.

[A woodcut illustration depicting a woman, **Hippo**, jumping from a boat into the water. Several men are in the boat, some looking on in surprise or trying to stop her. The side of the boat is inscribed with the word **"HIPPO"**.]

<summary>This page concludes the story of the Roman heroine Cloelia, who escaped Etruscan captivity and was honored with a rare equestrian statue, and begins the story of Hippo, a Greek woman.</summary>
<keywords>Cloelia, Lars Porsenna, Tiber River, equestrian statue, Sacred Way, hostages, Hippo, Greek history, feminine virtue</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of biographies from Giovanni Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women), following the lives of women from antiquity whose virtues or vices were deemed noteworthy.</meta>

[Decorative Initial H] Hippo was a Greek woman, as is clearly enough understood from ancient manuscripts. I hardly believe she was the only one of her kind, or that she reached the peak of her merit all at once; for we ascend to higher things by degrees, because no one becomes great <note>original: "nemo repente fiat"—an adaptation of the proverb "nemo repente fuit turpissimus," meaning no one becomes extremely good or bad suddenly</note> suddenly. But since, through the malice of antiquity, her lineage, her homeland, and her other deeds have been lost, it is my intention to bring forward what has reached us, lest her story perish or her deserved honor be withdrawn. 

We have learned, therefore, that this Hippo was captured by chance by enemies at sea. Since she was possessed of great beauty and felt that the pirates intended to violate her chastity, she valued the honor of her purity so highly that—perceiving it could not be saved except through death—she threw herself headlong into the waves without waiting for the violence to occur. By these waves, her life was taken, but her chastity was preserved. 

Who is so old <note>The author implies that even those hardened by age or long experience would be moved by her resolve.</note> that they would not praise the woman's decision? Indeed, she redeemed her chastity with those few years in which her life might have been prolonged, and in an early death, she sought eternal glory for herself. This distinguished work of virtue the sea could not cover, nor could the deserted shore take it away; rather, history <note>original: "litera"—literally "letters" or "written records"</note> preserves it in the light with perpetual monuments and her honor. 

Moreover, after her body had been tossed about for some time by the waves in the manner of a plaything, it was driven by them onto the Erythraean <note>referring to the coast of Erythrae in Ionia, modern-day Turkey</note> shore, and was buried by the coastal people according to the custom for shipwreck victims. Finally, when the report arose from the enemies as to who she was and the cause of her death, the place of her grave on the shore was adorned by the Erythraeans with the highest veneration—a huge mound destined to remain for a long time—as a testimony to her preserved honor. Thus we may know that no light of virtue can be darkened by the shadows of adverse fortune.

### Concerning Megullia Dotata. Chapter 52.

[Decorative Initial E inside a circle with two figures] Megullia, whom the ancient Romans surnamed **Dotata** <term>Dotata: Latin for "the well-dowered." In early Rome, a dowry of 50,000 copper coins was so exceptionally large it became her identifying name.</term>, was a Roman woman and noble, I believe, in that unrefined and (as I might say) austere age, before the Roman citizens <note>original: "quirites"—the formal name for Roman citizens in their civil capacity</note> had yet passed from the arms of poverty, their nurse, into Asiatic splendors and the treasures of great kings.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient Roman virtues, specifically focusing on the shift from historical simplicity and poverty to contemporary extravagance in dowries. It follows a section likely discussing the modesty of ancient women.</meta>

.57.

...[they] had attained an illustrious status, though those ancient ways were neglected. For a certain woman obtained the surname "the Endowed" <note>original: *dotatae*</note>—as I believe—more through the extravagance of her ancestors than through any merit of her own deeds. 

For, five hundred thousand pieces of copper <term>copper pieces: original *aeris*, referring to the *as*, the standard bronze or copper coin of early Rome</term> having been given to her husband as a dowry, it seemed so monstrous <note>The author uses "monstrous" to mean something unnaturally large or omen-like for the time period.</note> in that era that the giver was granted the surname "the Endowed." This name persisted through many ages; meanwhile, so that they might add something beyond the usual custom of the citizens to a maiden's dowry, she in particular would be called Megullia the Endowed. 

O, blessed simplicity! O, praiseworthy poverty! That which you rightly caused to be judged as monstrous would be seen as a joke by the modern excesses <note>original: *lascivie*, implying luxury, wantonness, or lack of restraint</note> of today. For in the meantime, we have exceeded the measure of things in every way, so that you will hardly find a smith, a woodworker, a common laborer, a camp-follower, or a farm steward who would be willing to lead a wife into his home for such a small pittance. Nor is it surprising that even among the common people...

<meta>This page begins a biographical account of Veturia and her son Gnaeus Marcius Coriolanus, likely following a section on other famous Roman families or virtues.</meta>

[Decorative V] Veturia <note>The source text reads 'Eturia', which is almost certainly a scribe's error for Veturia, the famous mother of the Roman general Coriolanus</note>, a noble Roman woman now advanced in years,
through her praiseworthy works extended her years into a perpetual,
flourishing vigor. She had a son, the young Gnaeus Mar-
cius, a man of vigorous courage, ready in both counsel and
action. When he saw that the town of Corioli, belonging to the
Volscians, was captured by the besieging Romans due to his
renowned bravery, he earned the surname Coriolanus.
He enjoyed such immense favor from the nobility that he dared
all things in word and deed. For this reason, while the city
was suffering from a grain shortage <term>grain shortage (annonae penuria): a recurring crisis in early Rome where food supplies failed, often leading to civil unrest</term> and a large supply had been
brought from Sicily through the efforts of the Senate <note>original: 'patrum', literally 'the Fathers', referring to the Patrician class or Senators</note>,
he argued in a severe speech that it should not be distributed
to the common people until they surrendered the honors <note>political offices or privileges</note>
which they had taken from the nobility shortly before, when
they withdrew to the Sacred Mount <term>Secession to the Sacred Mount: a protest in 494 BCE where the commoners left Rome to demand political rights, resulting in the creation of the Tribunes</term> in exchange for their return.
The hungry commoners would surely have laid hands on him
in their rage, had not a tribune of the people timely set a day
for him to plead his case. Because he, being indignant, did
not appear, he was condemned to exile and withdrew to the
Volscians, who had been enemies of the Romans shortly before.
By them, he was received both kindly and honorably.

<summary>This page introduces Veturia and her son Coriolanus, detailing his military success at Corioli and the political conflict regarding grain distribution that led to his exile from Rome.</summary>
<keywords>Veturia, Coriolanus, Gnaeus Marcius, Volscians, Roman Senate, grain shortage, Sacred Mount, exile</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>The previous page likely detailed the Roman Senate's failed attempts to negotiate with Gaius Marcius Coriolanus and the subsequent decision to send a delegation of Roman matrons, led by his mother Veturia and wife Volumnia, to plead for peace.</meta>

Nor was there lacking among them a huge crowd of following women. When Coriolanus recognized her arrival, even though his spirit was swollen <note>original: "turgidus"; implying he was puffed up with pride or suppressed rage</note> with pride, he was nonetheless struck with dismay at his mother’s coming. Rising from his chair, he left the tent and went to meet her to receive her.

Indeed, Veturia—holding the wife of Coriolanus on one side and his children on the other—did not look upon her son before she put aside her maternal devotion and kindled her own anger. Though she had left the city as a humble suppliant, upon entering the enemy camp she was transformed into a fierce rebuker. Stirring up the strength in her worn-out body, she said:

"Halt your step, hostile youth! I wish to know, before I accept your embrace, whether you come to receive a mother or a captive enemy? I suspect an enemy. Wretched me! Has the long life I so desired only brought me to this: to see you first condemned to exile, and then an enemy of the Republic? I ask you, do you recognize on what soil you stand as an armed foe? You do indeed recognize it; and if you claim not to know, this is the land in which you were conceived, in which you were born, and in which you were raised by my own labor. With what spirit, then—with what mind or what impulse—could you bring hostile arms against it?

As you entered, did the honor due to your land, the love of your sweet wife, the devotion of your sons, and the reverence for your native fatherland not stand in your way? Did they not move your sharp breast? Could they not break your angers, however justly they were undertaken? While you first gazed upon those walls, did it not come to your memory: 'There is my home and the <term>Penates</term> <note>The Penates were ancient Roman household gods believed to protect the domestic hearth and the state.</note> of my fathers; there are my wife and children; there, in her misfortune and by my own doing, is my unhappy mother'? The Senators came; the priests came; and yet they could not move your stony heart to do by request what you ought to have done of your own accord.

I admit it is enough to make me wretched: that my own fertility has been a curse to my fatherland and to me. Where I once believed I had given birth to a son and a citizen, I now see that I have brought forth an enemy, most hostile and inflexible. Indeed, it would have been better had I never conceived; had I been sterile, Rome might stand without being besieged, and I, a poor old woman, might die in a free fatherland. But I can now suffer nothing..."

<summary>This page depicts the dramatic confrontation between the Roman exile Coriolanus and his mother, Veturia, who rebukes his treason and appeals to his sense of duty to family and fatherland.</summary>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of the Roman legendary figure Coriolanus and his mother, Veturia. On the previous page, Coriolanus had led an army against Rome, and his mother went to his camp to plead for peace.</meta>

...nothing could be more miserable for me, nor more shameful for you. Nor have I been miserable for long <note>original: "nec vt sim miserrima diu futa sum" — Veturia implies that she is near the end of her life, so her suffering will be short, but her son's shame will last</note>. As for these children of yours, consider well: for them, either an early death or a long life of slavery awaits. Tears followed these words, and then came the prayers of his wife and children, along with mutual embraces and the cries of the weeping and praying matrons <note>noble Roman women</note>. By these words, groans, and prayers, it was achieved that what the majesty of the ambassadors and the reverence of the priests could not accomplish, was instead broken by the veneration of a mother: the anger of that most bitter leader was shattered, and his purpose was turned. After embracing and dismissing his kin, the enemy's camp was moved back away from the city. From this, it followed—lest the glory of the woman be diminished by ingratitude—that by a decree of the Senate, a temple and an altar to Women’s Fortune <term>Fortuna Muliebris: a Roman deity representing the fate and protection of women</term> were constructed of polished stone in the very place where Veturia softened her son's anger, as a perpetual memory of this event. Even though this temple is very ancient, it has endured into our own age almost entirely undiminished.

...Roman liberty would have remained standing. They would have been content with a smaller reward, for the temple dedicated to Women's Fortune <term>Fortuna Muliebris: a Roman goddess representing the fate of women, particularly matrons</term> was seen as a very great tribute. 

But what of it? The world is womanish, and thus men are made womanish themselves. Whatever was turned against men, time—which has consumed so many useful things—could not consume, nor could it diminish the rights of women who preserve their status with tenacious perseverance. Therefore, let women applaud Veturia; let them honor her name and her merit every time they adorn themselves with white pearls, purple cloth, and golden brooches, and whenever men rise from their seats as they walk by, or when the estates of the dying are diminished to support those who live in leisure <note>A reference to Roman inheritance laws that allowed women to inherit significant wealth, which the author views with a touch of medieval skepticism.</note>.

### On Thamyris the Painter. Chapter 57.

->[Illustration: The Scythian Queen Tomyris (labeled TAMIRIS) stands over the defeated Persian King Cyrus (labeled CIRVS), placing his severed head into a vessel of blood. Though the text describes Thamyris the artist, the illustrator has depicted the much more famous—and similarly named—Queen Tomyris.]<-

**T**hamyris <note>Also known in other sources as Timarete, a Greek painter from the 5th century BCE.</note> was a woman who, in her own time, was an excellent painter. Although the passage of ages has perhaps taken away much of her excellence, it has not yet been able to take away her distinguished name or the memory of her skill. Some wish to place her in the ninetieth... <note>original: "nongesima," likely referring to the 90th Olympiad, a common way of dating ancient Greek figures.</note>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographies of famous women, specifically concluding the account of the Greek painter Olympias and beginning the entry for Queen Artemisia II of Caria.</meta>

Olympias <note>original: "olimppade"</note> was the daughter of the painter Micon. However, although we read that there were two Micons—both of them painters who flourished in Athens at the same time—the records do not distinguish between them, except for these few words stating that she was the daughter of the Micon to whom the surname "the Younger" <note>original: "minoris cognomen"</note> was reportedly given. Truly, whoever her father was, she possessed such marvelous talent that, looking down upon traditional womanly duties, she imitated her father's art <note>original: "paternam artem"</note>. During the reign of Archelaus <note>Archelaus I (reigned c. 413–399 BCE) was a king of Macedon known for his patronage of poets and artists.</note> over the Macedonians, she achieved such singular glory in painting that at Ephesus—where Diana <note>The Roman name for the Greek goddess Artemis; her temple at Ephesus was one of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World.</note> was worshipped with special honor—they preserved for a long time a famous image of that same Diana, painted by her hand on a wooden panel. Since she lived to a very advanced age, she provided such a great testimony of her skill that it remains memorable even to this day. Indeed, her dedication is most praiseworthy if we consider the spindles, baskets, and other womanly tools <note>original: "fusos & calathos"; these were the standard symbols of domestic labor for women in the ancient world, which Olympias rejected in favor of the paintbrush.</note> she set aside.

### On Artemisia, Queen of Caria. Ch. 55.

[A woodcut illustration showing two women. The woman on the left is labeled "ARTEMISIA" and is pouring a liquid from a cup into a bowl on a table. The woman on the right is seated. To the far right, a figure lies in a fire, labeled "MAUSOLUS" <note>Mausolus was the ruler of Caria; his wife Artemisia was so devoted to him that she famously mixed his ashes into her daily drink to become a living tomb for him.</note>.]

**Artemisia**, Queen of Caria, was a woman of great spirit and an example of the most sacred and rarest love, as well as of perfect widowhood, enduring forever for posterity. Although the identity of the parents from whom she descended or the country she was born in has not sufficiently reached our days...

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of noble women from antiquity, focusing on Artemisia II of Caria and her devotion to her husband, Mausolus.</meta>

59

It is to the praise of her nobility to know that she was the wife of Mausolus, the most powerful king of Caria; <note>Caria was an ancient region in western Asia Minor, in what is now Turkey.</note> she loved him so much in life that, surviving him, she could not forget him in death. Great monuments to this devotion stood for a long time; for if we are to trust famous writers, as soon as her most beloved husband ended his days, she carried forth his body with the highest honors. Nor would she allow his ashes, which were carefully collected after the funeral fires, to be stored in a golden urn; she believed that any other vessel was unsuitable for so beloved a spouse except for that breast in which the flame of her old love burned far more than usual now that he was deceased. Therefore, so that what remained of him on earth <note>original: "quod terreum superat"</note> might reside where the perpetual memory of his past life dwelt, she gradually consumed all the collected ashes by mixing them into her drinks until they were finished. The remainder of her life was dedicated to perpetual tears, and thus, with her bodily moisture consumed by weeping, and believing she was about to join him, she came joyfully to her death.

Yet, as a widow, she performed great deeds. It was an ancient custom for outstanding men to have noble tombs erected; so that this work might appear worthy of her love, Artemisia—setting aside all greed—devised a project exceedingly marvelous and sumptuous. Not content with just one or a common craftsman, she ordered Scopas, Bryaxis, Timotheus, and Leochares <note>These were the four greatest sculptors of the 4th century BCE Greek world.</note> to be summoned, whom Greece proclaimed the most distinguished in the whole world in that age. She caused a magnificent "Mausoleum" <term>Mausoleum: a grand tomb, named after King Mausolus; this structure became so famous that his name became the generic word for all large funerary monuments.</term> to be designed for Mausolus according to their judgment and constructed with fine marbles. Because of that marvelous work, even if she did nothing else, she would make her beloved husband's name immortal. It will not be absurd to make special mention of it here, because both in its artistry and its expense, it excelled almost all buildings in the world and has long been remembered as one of the Seven Wonders of the World; for the fame of the craftsmanship will flourish, and the magnificence of this renowned woman will become even more celebrated. Therefore, the architects at Halicarnassus <note>The capital city of Caria.</note>, the principal city of Caria, by the queen's command, established a tomb square in form; and those sides which look toward the south and the north...

We read that this woman indeed—and perhaps more often—at least after her husband’s death, having set aside her tears for a time, took up arms twice: first, to protect the safety of her homeland; and second, to preserve the loyalty of her allies as required. For upon the death of Mausolus <note>Mausolus was the ruler of Caria; his monumental tomb at Halicarnassus became one of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World, giving us the word "mausoleum."</note>, the Rhodians, being indignant that a woman should rule the kingdom of Caria <note>original: "regno carie pesse" (to hold power over the kingdom of Caria)</note> so close to Halicarnassus, arrived with an armed fleet in great numbers to occupy it, as if with a certain hope of seizing control. 

Truly, the city of Halicarnassus sits in a naturally fortified location overlooking the sea from a steep height. It possesses twin harbors, of which one—the smaller—lies within the city with a narrow entrance, almost hidden. It is situated so that from the royal palace overlooking it, all necessary supplies could be prepared and brought down without any of the citizens, let alone foreigners, being able to see those who guarded the palace. 

The other harbor, which is larger, is adjacent to the open sea alongside the city walls. When Artemisia <note>Artemisia II of Caria, who succeeded her husband-brother Mausolus in 353 BCE.</note> learned that the Rhodians were about to arrive there, she ordered her men to be under arms and, having gathered her naval companions...

### On the maiden Virginia, Chapter 56.

->[Woodcut illustration with labels: **VIRGINIUS**, **CLAUDIUS**, **VIRGINIA**]<-

**V**irginia, a Roman maiden by name and in deed, is to be remembered with pious memory. She was conspicuous for her remarkable beauty and was the daughter of Virginius, a man of the plebeian <note>The plebeians were the general body of free Roman citizens, distinct from the privileged patrician class.</note> class but honorable. Although she was of excellent character, she is not famous so much for her own steadfastness as for the crime of her ill-fated lover and the deed of her excessively stern father, and for the Roman liberty that followed from her story. 

This young woman, indeed, in the second year of the rule of the Decemvirs <term>Decemvirs: A ten-man commission established to govern Rome and codify its laws.</term> over the city, was betrothed by her father to Lucius Icilius, a man of tribunician rank <note>Icilius had previously served as a Tribune of the Plebs, an office dedicated to protecting the rights of commoners.</note> and a spirited youth. It happened that her marriage was delayed by a Roman military expedition undertaken against the Aequi <note>An ancient people of central Italy who were frequent enemies of early Rome.</note> at Mount Algidus, because her father, Virginius, was serving in that same campaign. 

While things stood thus, it happened through Virginia's misfortune that her beauty so pleased Appius Claudius—the Decemvir who, along with Spurius Oppius <note>original: "spurio appio" — the Latin text likely contains a scribal error, as Spurius Oppius was the colleague who remained in Rome with Appius Claudius while the others went to war.</note>, had remained behind from the other colleagues to protect the city—that he...

<summary>This page introduces the story of Virginia, a beautiful Roman plebeian whose tragic fate at the hands of the corrupt Decemvir Appius Claudius led to a revolution in Rome.</summary>
<keywords>Virginia, Virginius, Appius Claudius, Decemvirs, Roman Republic, Lucius Icilius, plebeians, Roman history, virtue</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Appius Claudius and his obsession with Virginia, detailing his transition from public pursuit to a legalistic fraud to claim her as a slave.</meta>

...his mind, but he judged it not safe enough to use force publicly. Instead, he turned his cleverness toward fraud. He arranged it so that Marcus Claudius, his own freedman—a man of ready boldness—should seize the maiden as she was passing by the Forum <note>The central public square of Rome, where legal and social business took place.</note> as soon as an opportunity arose, claiming her as his own runaway slave <term>mancipium</term><note>A legal term for property or a person held in absolute ownership; a chattel slave.</note> and taking her into his own house. If anything should stand in the way of this attempt, the freedman was to immediately drag the matter before Appius himself in a legal case.

A few days later, the freedman, with rash daring, seized the maiden as she was passing and claimed she was his. As the girl cried out and resisted the impure man with all her strength, aided by the matrons with whom she was walking, a crowd of people suddenly gathered. Among them arrived Icilius <note>original: "Procilius," likely a scribal variant for Icilius, Virginia's fiancé.</note>. After many arguments were made on both sides, the matter reached the point where she was led to the tribunal before the judge who was also her lover. Scarcely could a postponement of the judgment until the following day be obtained from the burning Appius.

In this interval, although Claudius’s fraud was failing—for word had been sent to the military camp to prevent the girl's father from coming to Rome if he were summoned—the father, having been called, appeared and was present nonetheless. Dressed in mourning clothes <term>sordidatus</term><note>A Roman custom where those under legal accusation or in deep grief wore soiled, dark clothing to win public sympathy.</note>, he came into the court with his daughter, his other friends, and Icilius. 

On the contrary, when Marcus [Claudius] sought to claim the girl as property, the lustful presiding judge refused to hear Virginius <note>The father's name.</note>. Virginia was adjudged to be a runaway slave. When Marcus attempted to seize her and Virginius had spoken many words against Appius in vain, the father finally obtained permission from the judge—who was now gnashing his teeth in anger—to speak at least a little while with the girl and her nurse. He hoped that perhaps, by discovering the truth of some old mistake, he might give up the "slave" with less pain to himself.

When he had withdrawn with them near the shops of Cloacina <note>The *Tabernae Novae* near the Shrine of Venus Cloacina in the Forum.</note>, yet still within sight of the court, he took up a butcher’s knife and said: "In the only way I can, my dear daughter, I claim your freedom." He then plunged the blade into the maiden's breast, to the great grief of those watching. At this, the unhappy girl collapsed, pouring out her blood and her soul as all those standing by looked on. Thus, the most shameful hope of the lustful Appius was brought to nothing by the slaughter of an innocent. Through the efforts of Virginius and others, a second secession of the plebeians <note>A massive strike where the common people withdrew from the city to force political change.</note> was brought about, which resulted in the downfall of the Decemvirs <note>The "Ten Men," a body of magistrates who had been ruling Rome with increasing tyranny.</note>.

<summary>This page recounts the tragic climax of the Virginia story, where her father kills her to protect her from the lust of the tyrant Appius Claudius, sparking a political revolution.</summary>
<keywords>Virginia, Appius Claudius, Virginius, Icilius, Roman Forum, slavery, Decemviri, plebeian secession, Roman Law</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
62

...they were forced to abdicate their power and leave to the Roman people the liberty they had occupied <note>The "they" refers to the Decemviri, a ten-man commission that had seized autocratic power in Rome.</note>. Not long after, with Virginius acting as a tribune of the plebs <term>tribune of the plebs: an official elected by the common people to protect their rights from patrician overreach</term>, a trial date was set for Appius Claudius. When he was about to come forward to plead his case, he was dragged into prison by the order of Virginius and bound in chains; so that he might flee the deserved disgrace and appease the innocent ghosts <note>original: "manes"</note> of Virginia, he ended his life there by noose, sword, or poison. However, the reckless client <term>client: a person of lower social status protected by a wealthy "patron" in exchange for services and political support</term> Marcus Claudius did not answer for his crime in the way he should have; for having protected himself by flight, he mourned in exile, with the goods of both his patron and himself confiscated to the state treasury. 

Nothing is more ruinous than an unjust judge. Whenever such a man follows the command of a wicked mind, it is inevitable that the entire order of law is perverted: the power of the law is dissolved, the work of virtue is weakened, the reins of crime are loosened, and in short, the entire public good is dragged into ruin. If this is not enough, other accounts of Appius’s nefarious undertaking and what followed thereafter bring the matter to light. For while this powerful man set no bridle on his evil lust, he proceeded to claim a free woman as his own, turning a virgin into an adulteress and a betrothed woman into a concubine, largely through a freedman steeped in fraud. 

By his detestable decree, it came to pass that a father was armed against his daughter <note>Refers to the famous story where Virginius killed his daughter, Virginia, to save her from being enslaved and raped by Appius Claudius</note>, piety was turned into cruelty, and—lest the incestuous man enjoy the fruit of his fraudulent vow—an innocent girl was killed. A cry went up in the city, a riot broke out in the military camps, a separation of the commoners from the fathers <note>original: "sepacio plebis a paribus" - This refers to the Secession of the Plebs, where the commoners left the city in protest</note> arose, and almost the entire Roman state was put at risk. 

O illustrious governor and outstanding legislator! That which he should have punished in others with dire execution, he himself did not fear to commit. Alas for me, how often we mortals are endangered by this plague, how often we, the undeserving, are dragged to destruction and are pressed under a shameful yoke, driven, despoiled, and killed by pressing wickedness. What evil is this, that officials do not fear the very laws that were invented to restrain lusts? Terrified by no fear of God, they turn their office into a license for crime. And whereas it is necessary for a governor to have eyes and a mind that are equally modest, mild speech, a grave and holy character, and hands entirely immune from bribes: they are lewd not only with their eyes but with an insane mind, nor do they [respect] the laws...

<meta>This page concludes a critique of corrupt legal officials—likely from the previous chapter regarding the goddess Flora or a similar figure—and begins Chapter 57, which discusses the ancient Greek painter Irene. ...but they follow the judgments of pimps; they are haughty and do not soften unless a little harlot <term>meretricula; a diminutive for a prostitute, implying someone of low status wielding high influence</term> requests it, or gold soothes their anger. Nor do they only receive gifts; they demand them. They trade and steal, and even resort to violence if they cannot achieve what they desire; inflamed with fury, they break out. And thus the best interpreters of the law behave: on one side luxury, on the other money. In vain is justice sought for our cause unless influence is bought from these men or from the women who sway them.

<note original: "KAROLVS MAGNVS">The label for Charlemagne is likely a printer's error or a reused woodcut from the chapter on Empress Irene (who was a contemporary of Charlemagne), as the text here clearly discusses the ancient Greek painter Irene.</note>

**I**rene—whether she was a Greek woman or in what age she flourished is not sufficiently certain, yet she is believed to be Greek. It is established that she was the daughter and pupil of a certain painter named Cratinus. Yet I consider her more praiseworthy insofar as she seems to have surpassed her master in art and fame, since her name still thrives in many places, while the father (except because of her) is almost nameless <note original: "inominato"></note>—unless he was the one of whom we read, who described the leaves and roots of all herbs in their proper form to provide knowledge of them; although some call him Cratinax, not Cratinus. This Irene’s talent was celebrated and her craftsmanship memorable, of which mastery there were long-lasting proofs: [a painting of] a certain girl which [was kept] at the city of Eleusis...

### Of Leontium. Chapter 58.

**L**eontium, if I judge correctly, was a Greek woman and was perhaps prominent during the era of Alexander the Great, the Macedonian king. If she had only preserved the <term>modesty</term> <note>original: "matronalem pudicitiam"; the social and moral conduct expected of a respectable woman of her status.</note> of a wife, her name’s glory would have been far more brilliant, given that the powers of her intellect were so great. For by the testimony of the ancients, she was so proficient in literary studies that—whether stirred by envy or driven by a womanly boldness—she dared to write a <term>scathing critique</term> <note>original: "in vehendo"; literally "carrying against," referring to a polemic or written attack.</note> against **Theophrastus** <note>A famous Greek philosopher and the successor to Aristotle.</note>, who was the most celebrated man of that time. I have not seen this work myself. Truly, since her reputation has endured through so many centuries even to our own age, it could not have been a small thing, nor a work of meager skill...

# Concerning Olympias of Macedonia, Chapter 58

->**OLYMPIAS — PAUSANIAS — CLEOPATRA**<-
<note>This illustration depicts the violent ends of those associated with the Macedonian court: Olympias herself, the assassin Pausanias, and Philip’s wife Cleopatra.</note>

**O**lympias, Queen of the Macedonians, was illustrious for her many titles. First, indeed—insofar as family lineages can bring any renown to mortals—she drew her origin from the blood of the Aeacidae <term>Aeacidae: a legendary dynasty of Epirus claiming descent from Aeacus, the grandfather of the hero Achilles</term>, which was then considered more splendid than any other in all of Greece or the known world. She was the daughter of Neoptolemus, King of the Molossians. Although her name from infancy was Myrtale, she was first called Olympias (as some prefer to believe). Furthermore, she had Alexander, King of Epirus, as her brother; and upon the death of Philip of Macedon, her son was Alexander <note>Alexander the Great</note>, whose deeds were so immense that no one who might surpass him in glory—vain as it may be—had been heard of before, nor would be born in the future. This added no small amount of luster to Olympias, if indeed it is a glory for mothers to have given birth to outstanding sons. But this radiance could not entirely escape being frustrated by certain stains cast upon her reputation; let us see how Olympias emerged more notorious from those events.

<summary>This page introduces Olympias of Macedonia, highlighting her prestigious lineage from the Aeacidae and her status as the mother of Alexander the Great.</summary>
<keywords>Olympias, Alexander the Great, Macedonia, Epirus, Aeacidae, Neoptolemus, Myrtale, Queen, lineage</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Macedonian royal house, specifically detailing the marital breakdown between King Philip II and Queen Olympias and the subsequent assassination of the King.</meta>

# Page 67

For, in the bloom of her youth, Olympias was led astray <note>original: "labefactata est," suggesting she was morally undermined or corrupted</note> by the allure of adultery, and almost nothing more shameful could have happened to a queen. What was even more disgraceful was the suspicion that Alexander <note>Alexander the Great</note> was born of adultery. This suspicion so disturbed Philip that he not only openly declared at times that Alexander was not begotten of him, but he also marked Olympias with the disgrace of divorce <note>original: "repudii"</note> and took Cleopatra <note>This refers to Cleopatra Eurydice, a Macedonian noblewoman, whose marriage to Philip caused a major rift in the family.</note>, the daughter of Alexander of Epirus, as his wife. 

Olympias could not hide how bitterly she took this. Indeed, until that day—with the exception of this stain—she had been famous only for royal brilliance; however, she now made herself notorious for various outrages. It is believed by some that she herself incited and urged on Pausanias <note>Pausanias of Orestis, a member of the King's personal guard</note>, a young man born of noble and honorable blood, to the murder of her husband Philip, with Alexander also being aware of the plot. 

For the morning after Pausanias had been hung on a cross <note>Historical accounts suggest the assassin was killed during his escape; here, the text refers to the public display of his corpse.</note> for the killing of Philip, his head was found adorned with a golden crown by the work of Olympias, on the very day after he had been fixed to the cross.

...of Cassander, he would not omit any deceit; for after her friends had been killed by his treachery, her death was demanded following her surrender. When Cassander sent the ministers of crime into the place where she was being held, she—already realizing she was to die by the hands of those approaching—rose up undaunted, leaning upon two handmaidens. She arranged her clothing and hair so that no unseemly sight might be seen as she fell. She did not allow herself to beg, nor were any cries or wails heard to come from her; on the contrary, she met her strikers and of her own accord offered her exposed body to the wounds, as if belittling that which even the strongest of men are usually most terrified of. By that death, she proved that she died as the mother of such an outstanding commander. <note>original: "ĩpatoris tã egregij gẽitricem" — referring to her son, Alexander the Great.</note>

### Concerning Claudia the Vestal Virgin Chapter 60

**C**laudia the Vestal Virgin I would believe to be worthily born of Roman blood when I look upon her remarkable **piety** <note>In this context, "piety" (pietas) refers to the Roman virtue of duty and devotion to one's family and country, rather than just religious faith.</note> toward her father. Indeed, when her father was celebrating a magnificent triumph by decree of the Senate, with the Roman people watching the procession, one of the tribunes of the plebs—due to a private grudge—broke through toward him as if he were a man of no merit. Stepping into the middle of the path, and with the insolent and bold manner of a tribune, he laid violent hands on the man celebrating his triumph, attempting to drag him out of his chariot. 

When the maiden Claudia saw this among the spectators, she was immediately moved by an urgent sense of duty. Distressed, and forgetting both her gender and the dignity of the sacred headbands <term>vitta: a ceremonial wool headband worn by Vestal Virgins and priests to signify their sacrosanct status</term> with which she was covered, she could not endure it. Instead, she suddenly burst through the middle of the crowds with great force and, compelling the crowd to give way by her bold appearance, she placed herself between the tribune’s arrogance and her father’s glory with tireless strength. No matter what risks were dared, once the tribune was pushed aside, she allowed her father a free ascent to the Capitol. 

Oh, sweet love! Oh, unbroken devotion! What should we believe provided strength to the weak body of a maiden, or what caused her to forget her religious status, except the sight of him being oppressed by injury—the man she remembered as the guardian of her infancy and the one who cherished her with pious prayers and affection?

<summary>This page concludes the story of the dignified execution of Olympias and begins the account of Claudia, a Vestal Virgin who used her sacred status to protect her father from a political assault during his triumphal parade.</summary>

## Concerning Virginia, Wife of Lucius Volumnius. Chapter 61.

[Woodcut illustration showing a group of figures in a formal setting, possibly a courtroom or public assembly. Two female figures are labeled "VIRGINEA" below them. One woman on the left stands before a group of men, while another on the right is kneeling.]

Virginia was indeed a famous matron among the Romans, though she was a different woman from the one mentioned previously. <note>This distinguishes her from the Virginia mentioned in Chapter 58, who was killed by her father to save her from the lust of the decemvir Appius Claudius.</note> Nevertheless, let it be noted she was born of a certain Aulus, but a <term>patrician</term> <note>The hereditary noble class of ancient Rome.</note> man. Beyond the symbols of her nobility, she was to be preferred above other Roman women in her own time for the merits of her chastity. It will be enough to recount a single act of hers—one most worthy of praise—to recognize the whole of her life and to grant her the fame she deserves. As is well established, there was once in the city of Rome...

<summary>This page concludes the praise of Claudia Quinta's miraculous proof of virtue and introduces Virginia the Patrician, emphasizing her nobility and superior chastity.</summary>
<keywords>Claudia Quinta, Virginia (wife of Volumnius), Roman virtue, chastity, patrician, tribunician power, Roman triumphs</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical accounts of Roman moral virtues, shifting to a specific anecdote regarding the goddess Pudicitia (Chastity) and the social divisions between the patrician and plebeian classes in early Rome.</meta>

In the Cattle Market <note>original: "foro boario"; the primary commercial center for livestock in ancient Rome</note>, near the round temple of Hercules, there was a famous shrine of Patrician Chastity <note>original: "patricie pudicitie"</note>, which had long ago been dedicated by noble women. In the year when Quintus Fabius and Quintus Publius Decius Mus were consuls for the fourth time—while by order of the Senate, as in other temples, supplications <note>religious ceremonies of prayer or thanksgiving to the gods</note> were being made to atone for omens, and while patrician women alone were performing sacred rites there in the most chaste manner according to ancient custom—it happened that Verginia approached with the others to participate in the sacred rites. 

When she was haughtily excluded by the presiding patrician matrons because she had married Lucius Volumnius, a man of the plebeian <note>the commoner class, as opposed to the aristocratic patricians</note> class—even though he had served as consul the previous year—a brief altercation broke out among the women at the sacred temple. This eventually flared up from womanly indignation into a great fire of spirit. For Verginia declared that she was chaste and a patrician, and should by no means be barred from the temple of Patrician Chastity, especially since she had married as a virgin to a plebeian man whose deeds she praised with wonderful acclaim. Leaving the patrician women, she returned home in indignation, and to her words she added an outstanding deed. 

For since she had a large house in the Long Street <note>original: "vico longonm" (Vicus Longus), a street on the Quirinal Hill</note> where she was then living with her husband, she decided that a portion of it would be sufficient for a modest shrine. After separating this area from the rest, she established an altar there. Having summoned the plebeian matrons, she explained the arrogance of the patrician women, and having complained of the injury she had received from them, she added: 

"I therefore pray and urge you that, just as you see the men of this city engaged in a continuous contest of virtue <term>Virtus: The Roman concept of masculine excellence, bravery, and moral character</term>, so you too should take up a contest among yourselves for the sole honor of matronly chastity <term>Pudicitia: The Roman virtue of feminine modesty and sexual fidelity</term>. Strive to ensure that this altar, which I dedicate to Plebeian Chastity <note>original: "plebeie pudicitie"</note> in your presence, may be believed to be honored more holily and by more chaste women than that other one. Let it be clear to the nations that heavenly souls are not infused only into the hearts of patrician women."

O worthy and most holy words of a matron! O indignation worthy of praise!

<summary>This page recounts the origin of the shrine of Plebeian Chastity, founded by the noblewoman Verginia after she was excluded from the patrician shrine for marrying a commoner.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Virginia, a Roman noblewoman who became a symbol of chastity (Pudicitia), and begins the biography of Flora, whose legacy shifted from a wealthy courtesan to a Roman goddess.</meta>

and found it to be something to be extolled to the stars with joyful applause—not for the material possessions of men, nor for the sake of acquiring wanton ornaments. It was sworn by her maidenhood; first, she went against the wanton and insolent eyes and desires of young men, and toward the deserving of her own glory of chastity <note>original: *castionie*, likely referring to *castimonia* or the cult of *Pudicitia*</note> with the best principles and holy strength. Indeed, it was then begun, and long after observed, that since no one—unless they were of proven modesty and had been married to only one husband <note>The Roman ideal of the *univira*, a woman who married only once, which was a requirement for certain priestesses and religious rites</note>—was allowed the right of sacrificing in that temple, she adorned the patrician altars with sanctity, breaking the lustful hope of those watching with unchaste <note>original: *incestuosis*, meaning impure or morally stained</note> eyes. Nor would I doubt that many, out of a desire for glory and to escape the ignominy of being barred from the sacrifice, were inspired to the cause and pursuit of preserving chastity.

### About Flora the prostitute, goddess of flowers and wife of Zephyrus. Chapter 62

**Flora** was a Roman woman, as antiquity seems to testify. As much as her shameful trade <note>original: *ignominiosus questus*, referring to her work as a prostitute</note> took away from her honor, her favoring fortune increased her fame. As everyone asserts, she was a very wealthy woman, but they disagree on how she acquired her riches. For some say she spent the entire flower of her youth and bodily beauty among the arches <note>original: *fornices*, the vaulted cellars or archways where Roman prostitutes often worked; the origin of the word "fornication"</note> and wicked pimps and young men as a public prostitute. Draining the resources of one fool after another with wanton flatteries (as is the custom of such women), and scraping and picking from everywhere, she arrived at such vast wealth. 

Others, however, considering it more respectable <note>The author likely means this ironically, as the following story is absurd</note>, tell a charming and ridiculous story from history. They claim that a temple attendant <note>original: *aedituum*, a sacristan or guardian of a temple</note> of Hercules at Rome, being idle, began a game of dice playing with his hands against one another. Having designated the right hand for Hercules and the left for himself, they say he made a wager: if he himself won, he would provide a temple supper and a mistress at the expense of the temple; but if Hercules emerged the victor, then he said he would provide the same from his own money.

<summary>This page concludes a tribute to Virginia's role in establishing exclusive rites for chaste women before transitioning to the controversial origins of the goddess Flora, debated as either a wealthy prostitute or the result of a divine wager at a dice game.</summary>
<keywords>Virginia, Chastity, Pudicitia, Univira, Flora, Goddess of Flowers, Hercules, Temple Attendant, Prostitution in Rome, Roman Morality</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
(vj)

But it is confirmed that when Hercules had conquered the monsters he was accustomed to defeat, a dinner and a famous prostitute named Flora <note>original: "meretricem floram"</note> were prepared for him. They say that while she was sleeping in the temple, a vision appeared to her showing that she had lain with Hercules, and he told her that she would receive a reward for their union from the first man she encountered when leaving the temple at daybreak. 

As she was leaving the temple, she met a very wealthy young man named Fanitius <note>original: "fanitio"; likely a scribal variant of Tarutius, a wealthy Etruscan in Roman myth who left his fortune to Flora or Acca Larentia</note>. She was loved and taken home by him, and after she had been with him for a long time, she was left as his heir when he died, and in this way she became wealthy. 

However, there are some who say this woman was not Flora, but Acca Larentia <note>original: "acciam laurentiam"; a legendary figure in Roman history often identified as the foster mother of the twins Romulus and Remus</note>, who had either nursed Romulus and Remus then or did so later. Truly, I do not care about this disagreement, as long as it is established that Flora was a prostitute and that she was wealthy. 

Nor [will I linger] as I move toward my intended goal: as the end of her mortal life approached, since she had no son and desired—as I believe—to perpetuate her name through feminine cunning, for the glory of herself and her name...

<summary>This page discusses the legendary origins of the wealth of Flora (or Acca Larentia), claiming she inherited her fortune from a wealthy youth after a divine encounter with Hercules.</summary>
<keywords>Flora, Hercules, Fanitius, Acca Larentia, Romulus and Remus, Roman mythology, inheritance, prostitutes in history</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
# LXVII

Recognizing that her shameful past could not be easily erased to hide her disgrace, he added a ridiculous and detestable falsehood to her baseness. Indeed, he invented a story to bring glory to the "flower" of this famous benefactor <term>testatrix</term><note>A woman who leaves a will; here referring to the harlot Flora, who left her fortune to the Roman people.</note> and recited it to the now-ignorant public. 

He asserted that she had long ago been a native nymph of wondrous beauty named Chloris <note>original: "cloram"; Boccaccio connects the name to the Greek 'chloros', meaning pale green or fresh.</note> and was loved most passionately by the wind Zephyrus—whom we call Favonius <note>The Roman name for the gentle West Wind.</note> in Latin. He claimed she was finally taken as his wife, and that he—whom the people in their foolishness numbered among the gods—granted her divinity as a wedding gift or dowry. This divinity came with a specific office: in early spring, she was to decorate the trees, hills, and meadows with flowers and preside over them. Thus, from Chloris her name became Flora. 

Furthermore, because fruit follows from flowers, the people believed that by placating her divinity with games celebrated with great license, she would lead the blossoms to a full harvest. Consequently, sacred rites, altars, and games were granted to this same goddess as if from ancient times.

Led astray by this deceit, the people came to believe that she—who in life had frequented brothels <note>original: "fornices coluerat"; the word 'fornix' (arch) refers to the vaulted underground rooms where Roman prostitutes worked.</note> and prostrated herself to anyone for even the smallest coin—had been carried to heaven on the wings of Zephyrus. They imagined her sitting beside Queen Juno and the other goddesses. 

And so, through her own cleverness and the favor of fortune, Flora was transformed from a harlot into a nymph by means of her ill-gotten wealth. She "gained" a marriage to the wind and, residing in temples as a divine power among mortals, she was celebrated with divine honors. In this way, she was not only changed from Chloris to Flora, but became famous <note>A pun on the Latin 'clara' (famous/bright) versus her original name 'clora'.</note> everywhere, rising from her status as the most notorious prostitute of her time.

<summary>This page describes the mythological "rebranding" of the historical Roman harlot Flora into a goddess of flowers to mask her scandalous origins. Boccaccio explains how her wealth bought her a place in the pantheon through a fabricated story involving the wind Zephyrus.</summary>
<keywords>Flora, Chloris, Zephyrus, Favonius, Roman Mythology, Goddess of Flowers, De Mulieribus Claris, Prostitution in Antiquity, Floralia</keywords>

<meta>This page begins Chapter 62 of Boccaccio’s "Concerning Famous Women" (De Mulieribus Claris). The text introduces a story of filial piety set in ancient Rome, often identified in history as the "Roman Charity."</meta>

# **On a Young Roman Woman. Chapter 62.**

[Woodcut illustration: A woman is seen through a barred window of a stone building, being visited by another woman. Outside the cell, a guard stands with a spear, and another man sits on a bench nearby.]

**T**here was a young Roman woman; unless I am mistaken, she did not draw her origin from the dregs of the common people. To her, the loss of her name through the malice of fortune, and the complete absence of any record of a husband, might seem to have somewhat stolen away the merit of her beauty. Lest this merit be seen as further diminished by me, even if I have not given her a specific place among the famous women <note>Boccaccio often groups unnamed women together when their names were lost to history but their deeds remained famous</note>, it is my intention to include her and relate the renowned piety of this unnamed woman. 

This young woman had a mother of honorable birth, but she was unfortunate. For in Rome, at the Praetor's tribunal <note>A legal court presided over by a Roman magistrate</note> (for what reason or desert I do not know), she was condemned to capital punishment and handed over by the Praetor to the *triumvir* <note>original: "triumviro"; a minor Roman official, specifically the *triumviri capitales*, who were responsible for prison management and executions</note> so that the sentence already pronounced might be carried out. By the *triumvir*, she was then presented to the keeper of the public prison for this same purpose. 

But because she was noble, it was ordered that she be put to death at night. The guard, however, moved by a certain humanity while showing respect for the woman's noble character, did not wish to attack her with his own hands; instead, he left her shut in alive so that she might be consumed by starvation <term>edia: a state of starvation or fasting</term>. 

Her daughter came to visit her, and having first been thoroughly searched <note>original: "excussa"; literally "shaken out" to ensure she was not smuggling food under her clothes</note> so that she might bring nothing inside with her, she obtained permission from the guard to enter the prison. To her already starving mother, and with milk... <note>The text cuts off here, but refers to the daughter nursing her mother from her own breast to sustain her life.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the story of a noble Roman woman condemned to death by starvation and her daughter's devoted efforts to visit her in prison. It sets the stage for the famous act of "Roman Charity," where the daughter saves her mother from hunger.</summary>
<keywords>Roman Charity, filial piety, Boccaccio, Roman law, imprisonment, starvation, mother and daughter</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues a discourse on moral virtues, specifically focusing on the concept of 'Pietas'—the dutiful devotion between parents and children. It recounts the famous Roman legend often called "Roman Charity."</meta>

lxviij

Since she had recently given birth, she had an abundance of milk and was able to help. Finally, after several days had passed, the guard began to wonder how the condemned woman had managed to stay alive for so long without food. Secretly watching what the daughter was doing with her mother, he noticed that she had drawn out her breasts and offered them to her mother’s mouth to be sucked. Marvelling at this devotion and this unusual way of nourishing a parent, he reported the daughter’s secret deed to the magistrate <note>original: "triumviro"; one of a board of three officials in charge of prisons and executions</note>. The magistrate then told the praetor, and the praetor announced it to the public council. Because of this, by common consensus, it was decreed that the mother's deserved punishment should be remitted as a gift to the daughter's devotion. 

Just as in ancient times an oaken crown <note>The *corona civica*, one of the highest military honors, awarded to a soldier who saved the life of a fellow citizen in battle</note> was bestowed upon a citizen who saved another in battle by force of arms, so shall we honor this daughter who saved her mother in prison with her milk. Indeed, you will not find a garland among the leaves worthy enough for such a pious deed. This devotion was not only holy but admirable; it is not merely to be considered equal to, but rather preferred over, the natural gift by which we are taught to bring newborn children to a stronger age with milk, and to pull parents back from the brink of death. 

Marvelous, therefore, are the powers of devotion! For it penetrates not only female hearts—which are easily drawn to compassion and tears—but sometimes even breasts hardened by unrestrained and diamond-like obstinacy. Once it has taken its seat near the heart, it first softens every hardness with flexible humanity. As a seeker of opportunities and a perfect companion in suffering, it causes people to mix tears with weeping, to face illnesses and dangers out of longing, and sometimes, if no other remedy exists, to undergo death in another’s place. These effects are so great that we should wonder less if we children act with devotion toward our parents; for in doing so, it seems we are rather returning a favor and paying back what we once received through a rightful restitution.

<summary>This page recounts the legend of "Roman Charity," where a daughter saves her imprisoned mother from starvation by breastfeeding her. The author uses this story to illustrate the transformative power of filial devotion (piety), which can soften even the hardest hearts.</summary>
<keywords>Roman Charity, Pietas, filial devotion, mercy, breastfeeding, oaken crown, parental care, restitution</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page begins Chapter 64 of Giovanni Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women), following the previous biographies of notable women from antiquity. This entry focuses on Marcia, a Roman artist.</meta>

**Concerning Marcia, Daughter of Varro. Chapter 64.**

->[Woodcut illustration showing a woman, Marcia, painting a portrait on the left and sculpting a figure on the right. Above the scenes are the labels: **MARCIA** and **VARRONIS**.]<-

**M**arcia, daughter of Varro <note>original: "Marcia varronis"</note>, a perpetual virgin, was recorded in Rome long ago. I do not remember finding which Varro she belonged to, nor even in what era she lived. I believe this observed virginity of hers should be extolled with all the more outstanding praise because, as a woman of her own legal standing <term>sui iuris: a legal status meaning she was not under the authority of a father or husband</term>, she preserved her integrity by her own will and not by the compulsion of a superior. For I find that she was not bound by the garments of the priesthood, nor subject to a religious vow, nor entangled in any higher profession—circumstances by which many women are either restrained or held back. Instead, by the integrity of her mind alone, she overcame the "sting of the flesh" <note>A common medieval reference to carnal desire</note>, to which even the most excellent men sometimes succumb. She preserved her body unstained by contact with men until her death. 

But although Marcia is primarily to be praised for this commendable constancy, she is no less to be commended for the strength of her intellect and the skill of her hands. Whether she learned under a master or had nature as her guide is uncertain to us; however, this seems most certain: that having turned away from traditional women's duties <note>original: "muliebribus ministeriis," likely referring to domestic tasks like spinning wool</note> so that she would not waste away in idleness, she devoted herself to the study of painting and sculpture.

<summary>This page introduces Marcia, a Roman woman praised by Boccaccio for her lifelong virginity and her dedication to the arts of painting and sculpture. He highlights that her independence and artistic skill were choices made of her own free will rather than religious obligation.</summary>

<keywords>Marcia, Varro, Roman artists, women painters, virginity, independence, painting, sculpture, Boccaccio</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
.lxviii.

...all! And finally, she painted with a brush and carved images from ivory so skillfully and so elegantly that she surpassed Sopolis and Dionysius <note>original: "Sopolini & dioniſiū"; these were famous Greek painters mentioned by Pliny the Elder as contemporaries of the artist.</note>, the most renowned painters of her age. A very well-known proof of this fact was that the panels painted by her were more valuable than all others. And what is far more wonderful, they claim that she not only painted with excellence—which occasionally happens to some people—but that she possessed a hand so swift at painting that no one else ever had the like.

Furthermore, the tokens of her art endured for a long time; but among other things, there was her own likeness, which she portrayed on a panel by consulting a mirror <note>This refers to Iaia of Cyzicus (often called Marcia in this period), who is credited with one of the first recorded self-portraits in history.</note>, with the lines and colors so perfectly preserved and the character of her face so captured that no contemporary who saw it could doubt who it was.

And among other things—to come to her unique character—they assert it was her particular habit, whether painting with a brush or carving with a chisel <term>chisel: a "celte" or tool used for carving stone or ivory</term>, to most often create images of women, while she rarely or never depicted men. I believe a modest blush <note>The author Boccaccio often attributes the choices of ancient women to "pudicus rubor" or modest shame, reflecting 14th-century values more than ancient ones.</note> gave cause for this habit; for since the ancients for the most part portrayed images naked or half-naked, it seemed to her that she must either depict men as imperfect, or if she made them perfect <note>In this context, "perfect" implies anatomically complete or realistically nude.</note>, she might seem to have forgotten her virginal modesty. Lest she fall into either fault, she judged it better to abstain from both.

# Of Sulpicia, wife of Fulvius. Chapter 65.

**S**ulpicia <note>The OCR reads 'Vlpicia', but the decorative initial 'S' confirms the name as Sulpicia, a famous figure of Roman virtue.</note> was once a most venerable woman who attained no less a reputation for praise among Roman matrons for her preserved chastity than **Lucretia** <note>A legendary figure in Roman history who became a symbol of female virtue after she took her own life to preserve her honor.</note> did by destroying herself with a knife. For she was the daughter of Servius Paterculus and the wife of Fulvius Flaccus, both of whom were noble men. 

And when, by order of the Senate, the <term>Decemvirs</term> <note>A council of ten men in ancient Rome appointed for special legal or religious tasks.</note> decreed according to ancient custom and the <term>Sibylline Books</term> <note>A collection of prophecies and ritual instructions consulted by the Roman state during times of crisis.</note> that a statue of **Venus Verticordia** <note>original: "veneris verticordie"; This title means "Venus the Changer of Hearts," a deity invoked to turn the hearts of women from lust toward purity and marriage.</note> should be consecrated in the city, it was intended that virgins and other women might not only abstain from lust, but also more easily turn toward praiseworthy modesty. 

A search was made according to the mandate of the ten men, which proclaimed that the most chaste of the Roman matrons should be the one to dedicate the statue. From the immense multitude of chaste women with which Rome then abounded, a judgment was held. First, one hundred women were selected from the entire group who were considered the most renowned for their purity. Among these, Sulpicia was chosen. Finally, by order of the Senate and by the judgment of those same women...

<summary>This page recounts how Sulpicia was singled out from all the women of Rome for her exceptional chastity, earning the honor of dedicating a new statue to Venus the Changer of Hearts.</summary>
<keywords>Sulpicia, Venus Verticordia, Roman virtue, chastity, Lucretia, Sibylline Books, Decemvirs, matrons</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of virtuous Roman women, specifically focusing on the requirements of a modest matron and the exceptional character of Sulpicia, who was chosen as the most chaste woman in Rome.</meta>

. 70 .

...they do so unwillingly. Indeed, it is necessary for matrons, so that they may truly be called modest in every sense, to restrain their greedy and wandering eyes before all else, and to keep them tucked within the borders of their own garments. They should speak words that are not only honorable, but also few and appropriate for the time. They must flee idleness as if it were the most certain and ruinous enemy of modesty. They must abstain from banquets, for **without grain and wine, lust grows cold** <note>original: "sine cerere et libero friget venus." This is a classical proverb meaning that lust is fueled by excessive food (Ceres) and wine (Liber/Bacchus).</note>. They should avoid songs and dances as if they were the arrows of excess, and instead devote themselves to thrift and sobriety. They must take care of domestic matters and keep their ears closed to obscene conversations. They should abstain from wandering about and cast away makeup <note>original: "pigmenta," referring to cosmetic paints or dyes</note> and unnecessary perfumes. They must reject superfluous ornaments and trample harmful thoughts and appetites with all their strength. They should persist in sacred meditations and keep watch. To avoid running through every single point: in the chastity of complete modesty, a woman should cherish her husband alone with the highest love, while neglecting all other men (unless she loves them with the affection of a brother). Even when she comes to the embraces of her husband for the purpose of conceiving children, she should do so not without a blush upon her face and in her soul. Because all these virtues together were perhaps not found fully realized in other women, but were united in Sulpicia alone, she was rightly preferred above all the rest.
