-># Concerning Harmonia of Sicily, daughter of Gelon. Chapter 66.<-

**H**armonia the Sicilian was the daughter of the young Gelon, who was the brother <note>Historical records usually identify Gelon II as the son of King Hiero II of Syracuse, rather than his brother; Boccaccio often follows specific medieval manuscript variations of these lineages.</note> of Hiero, the king of Syracuse. Although she was distinguished by her royal lineage, she was made much more worthy of remembrance by her devotion. Some maintain that she died a virgin; others, however, claim she was the wife of a certain Themistius. But this latter opinion is more widely accepted, though it subtracts nothing from her courageous devotion regardless of the differing views. 

Therefore, when the people of Syracuse rose in a sudden gathering and rebellion, venting their rage against the entire royal family, and after the young king Hieronymus <note>The grandson of Hiero II; his assassination in 214 BCE led to the downfall of the Syracusan monarchy.</note> had already been brutally killed, as well as Andronodorus <margin>An orange mark in the margin draws attention to the list of the doomed royal family.</margin> and Themistius—the sons-in-law of the king—the tumultuous mob made an armed rush with drawn swords against Damarata and Heraclea (the daughters of Hiero) and against Harmonia (the daughter of Gelon). 

It was arranged through the wisdom of Harmonia’s nurse that a certain girl of the same age as Harmonia, dressed in royal finery, should present herself to the killers in Harmonia’s place. This girl in no way failed the hope of the one who planned it; indeed, when she saw the crowd of assassins rushing toward her with the points of their hostile blades, she neither fled in terror nor confessed her true status to those who were striking her. Nor did she betray the hiding Harmonia, in whose place she was being killed; instead, silent and unmoving, she received the lethal blows and died. 

Happy in the devotion shown to her and yet unhappy was Harmonia; happy in such loyalty, yet unhappy in the betrayal of one so faithful. However, when Harmonia watched from her hiding place the perseverance of the innocent girl and her brave spirit in the face of death, and saw the maidenly blood flowing from her wounds, she was stunned. Although the girl had been slain and the assassins were already departing, allowing Harmonia the chance to escape, she began to admire such loyalty with her whole heart. Seized by this noble devotion <term>pietas</term> <note>original: "pietate". A Roman virtue denoting duty, religious behavior, and loyalty to family.</note> and with tears pouring forth, she could not bear to look upon that innocent blood shed without consequence, nor to prolong a life preserved at such a cost and by another’s loyalty into the future. She considered it better to descend to the underworld <note>original: "inferos". The ancient Greco-Roman realm of the dead.</note> in an untimely death with so faithful a companion than to wait to grow old among unfaithful citizens. O devotion! O ancient loyalty, which had escaped...

<summary>This page tells the story of Harmonia of Syracuse, whose life was briefly saved by a loyal servant girl who took her place during a massacre. Overwhelmed by the girl's sacrifice, Harmonia chose to reveal herself and die rather than live with the guilt of the servant's death.</summary>
<keywords>Harmonia of Sicily, Syracuse, Gelon, loyalty, sacrifice, royal family, revolution, ancient history</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous account of a loyal servant's sacrifice and begins the biography of Busa of Canosa (Chapter 67 of Boccaccio's "Famous Women").</meta>

. 71 .

...stepping forward into the midst, turning the points of the swords toward herself, she confessed her deception to the messengers and her loyalty to the woman who had been killed. She died voluntarily, offering her own blood as a funeral sacrifice <term>inferias: an offering or sacrifice to the spirits of the deceased</term> for the woman who had been slain. Stricken by many wounds, she collapsed as near as she could to the body of her mistress. It was most worthy that literature restore what the piety of that age took away. Truly, it is difficult to judge whose glory was greater: whether the loyalty of the one who died first, or the piety of the one who survived. The former makes the virtue of the first eternal, while the latter does the same for the name of the second.

# Concerning Busa, a woman of Canosa in Apulia. LXVII

[Woodcut illustration of a woman standing on the left, labeled "BUSA", and a group of men seated at a round table, eating and drinking.]

Busa—whom some call Paulina, as if "Busa" were a family name—was an Apulian woman originally from Canosa <note>original: "camisina," referring to the ancient city of Canusium</note>. I believe she was born of noble blood and was distinguished by many other merits, primarily because of that magnificent deed which antiquity alone has left to posterity regarding her. They say that while Hannibal the Carthaginian <note>original: "hannibale poeno"</note> was waging a bitter war against the Romans and was devastating all Italy with fire and sword, staining it with much blood, he had not only overcome his enemies in a great struggle at Cannae (an Apulian village) but had almost shattered all the Roman forces. It happened that in that conflict and immense slaughter...

<summary>This page concludes a narrative about a servant's ultimate sacrifice for her mistress and introduces Busa of Canosa, a noblewoman noted for her generosity during the Second Punic War.</summary>
<keywords>Busa of Canosa, Hannibal, Battle of Cannae, Roman History, loyalty, sacrifice, Apulia, Punic Wars</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the aftermath of the Roman defeat at Cannae (216 BCE), focusing on the hospitality of the noblewoman Busa toward the fleeing survivors.</meta>

by night through pathless ways, out of many scattered wanderers, about ten thousand arrived at Canusium; which city at that time remained faithful to its alliance with Rome. All of these—bloodless, weary, destitute, unarmed, naked, and suffering from wounds—not outside the camp or by the power of a victor, but in her own home, Busa received with hospitality. First of all, she bade them be of good heart, and having summoned physicians, she saw to it that the wounded were cared for with motherly <note>original: "materna"</note> affection. To the naked, she first granted clothing to all with wonderful liberality; and for those who [needed] arms, she provided daily expenses for everyone from her own wealth. Having revived the wretched men with kind piety and restored them to hope, she voluntarily granted travel expenses <term>viaticum: funds or supplies provided for a journey</term> to all who wished to depart; nor did she in any part withdraw her hand from the needs of those who were continuously flocking to her. Indeed, it is wonderful to tell, and in a woman far more praiseworthy than if it had happened to a man. For the ancients were accustomed <note>The Latin "cons" likely refers to "consueverunt," meaning "were accustomed to."</note> to extol Alexander, King of the Macedonians and invader of the whole world, for his magnificent generosity as chief among his other honors...

<summary>This passage recounts the legendary generosity of Busa of Canusium, a wealthy woman who provided medical care, clothing, and funds to 10,000 Roman soldiers fleeing the Battle of Cannae. The author argues her virtue is even more remarkable than that of Alexander the Great because of her gender and status.</summary>
<keywords>Busa of Canusium, Battle of Cannae, Roman History, hospitality, maternal care, Alexander the Great, liberality, Second Punic War</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Busa of Canusium, a wealthy woman who provided for Roman soldiers after their defeat at Cannae, and begins the account of Sophonisba.</meta>

. 72 .

...he might gain through his munificence; she did it so she might bestow help upon the needy. Why say more? If we consider the mind, the sex, and the character of both individuals, I would not doubt that under an impartial judge, Busa attained far more glory from her liberality than Alexander <note>original: "alexander"; referring to Alexander the Great, often cited as the pinnacle of royal generosity.</note> did from his munificence. But to whichever you wish the purer praise to go, in my opinion, Busa made the best use of her wealth. For Mother Nature did not bring gold out into the light from the deep recesses of the earth so that it might be carried from its mother's womb <note>The "mother's womb" is a poetic metaphor for the earth or the mine where gold is found.</note> into a tomb—which is what misers do when they bury it in chests and brood over it with excessive guarding, as if it were to be born again. 

But above all, wealth was produced to be offered for the common good; next, for our own honorable splendor and the social life of our friends; and finally, if anything remains, we should provide aid with a liberal spirit to those prostrated by the injury of fortune, those worn out by the "wrath of heaven" <note>A common classical idiom for natural disasters or sudden misfortune.</note>, those weighed down by undeserved poverty, those shut in prison for another’s crime, and whoever is worn down by anxious want. Indeed, we should give not so that we may be seen, but so that we may be helpful; not so that we may profit, but so that we may be generous. These benefits must be bestowed with the guidance of reason's moderation, lest while we help others we bring poverty upon ourselves, by which we might be forced—I will not even say to lay violent hands on the goods of others—but even to look upon them with greedy eyes.

<meta>This page begins the biography of Sophonisba, a Carthaginian noblewoman during the Second Punic War. It follows the common medieval and Renaissance tradition of focusing on her tragic end and her role in the political maneuvers between Carthage, Rome, and the Numidian kings.</meta>

[Decorative initial **S** containing a dragon or serpent] **Sophonisba** <note>The OCR reads 'Ophonisba,' but the decorative initial provides the 'S.' Sophonisba (c. 245–203 BCE) was a famous Carthaginian noblewoman whose beauty and tragic death became a popular subject in art and literature.</note> indeed, while she may have walked as the shining queen of the **Numidians**, became far more illustrious through the severity of a death she undauntedly took upon herself. For she was the daughter of **Hasdrubal**, the son of Gisco <note>original: "asdrubalis gisgonis filij." Hasdrubal Gisco was a major Carthaginian general, distinct from Hannibal's brother of the same name.</note> and a great prince of the **Carthaginians** at the time **Hannibal** was harassing **Italy**. When she was in the flower of her youth and of quite extraordinary beauty, she was joined as a virgin bride by her father to **Syphax**, the most powerful king of **Numidia**.

Not indeed solely from a desire for a royal alliance, but that wise man <note>referring to her father, Hasdrubal Gisco</note> hoped, with the **Roman** war pressing in, not only to draw the <term>barbarian</term> <note>In the Latin context, 'barbarus' referred to those outside the Roman or Greek cultural sphere; here it refers to the North African Numidians.</note> king away from the Romans, but by the aid of his daughter's charms to turn him to the side of the Carthaginians against the Romans. And he was not deceived in his calculated trick; for when Syphax had celebrated the nuptials, favored by the beauty of the well-instructed young girl, he was drawn into such a fire of love for her that Syphax thought nothing dear or delightful to himself except her.

And so, while disaster was brewing and it appeared that **Cornelius Scipio** <note>Scipio Africanus, the Roman general who eventually defeated Hannibal.</note> was about to cross from **Sicily** into **Africa** with his armies, Sophonisba, at Hasdrubal's prompting, so drew Syphax's mind to her own desires with flatteries and prayers that he not only abandoned the Romans, to whom he had pledged the faith of friendship, and joined the Carthaginians, but even voluntarily took up the leadership of someone else's war.

Wherefore, treading faith underfoot with treachery—the faith he had previously promised to Scipio, his guest—he forbade him via letters (before he had yet crossed to **Africa**) from entering. However, Scipio, a youth of great spirit, having condemned the wickedness of the barbarian king, and having stationed his forces not far from **Carthage**, attacked him before all others through **King Masinissa** <note>A rival Numidian king who allied with Rome.</note>, his ally, and **Laelius**, his <term>legate</term> <note>A high-ranking Roman military officer or deputy.</note>. 

By them, the army was routed, and Syphax was captured, bound, and led before the royal city of Numidia. Nor was he displayed, weighed down by chains, to the citizens before the surrender of the city was made to Masinissa; who, since Laelius had not yet arrived, [encountered] the queen, while he was still armed, amid the sudden movement of events and the general uproar...

<summary>This page introduces Sophonisba, daughter of the Carthaginian general Hasdrubal, who was married to King Syphax of Numidia to secure a military alliance against Rome. It describes how her charms persuaded Syphax to betray his Roman allies, leading to his eventual defeat and capture by Scipio Africanus and Masinissa.</summary>

<keywords>Sophonisba, Syphax, Hasdrubal Gisco, Scipio Africanus, Masinissa, Carthage, Numidia, Second Punic War, Roman History</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Roman conquest of Numidia during the Second Punic War (c. 203 BCE). Following the defeat of King Syphax, the Numidian prince Masinissa (a Roman ally) enters the palace at Cirta.</meta>

.83.

Upon entering, he encountered **Sophonisba**. She, well aware of her misfortune, when she saw him entering the vestibule—distinguished above the others by his armor—having recognized him as the king, fell at his knees (which were like the knees of the gods). Retaining the spirit of her former station, she said:

"Thus, illustrious king, it has seemed right to God and to your good fortune that you should have all power over us—who but a short time ago were sovereigns. But if it is permitted for a captive to utter words of supplication in the presence of a victor and the master of her life and death, and to touch his knees and his victorious right hand, I pray and beseech you by your majesty (a state in which I also lived until recently), by the royal lineage and the entire <term>Numidian name</term> <note>This refers to the collective identity and honor of the Numidian people</note>—and may you be received with a better omen than **Syphax** <note>Sophonisba’s husband, the former king of Numidia who had just been defeated and captured</note> who departed from here—concerning me, whom adverse fortune has made subject to your law, do whatever seems pious and good in your eyes. My only wish is that I am not handed over alive to the insolent and arrogant judgment of the **Romans** <note>Sophonisba feared being paraded through Rome in a 'triumph,' a humiliating victory procession for captured royals</note>, especially as a Carthaginian."

"For you can easily understand what I—as a Roman enemy, a Carthaginian, and the daughter of **Hasdrubal** <note>Hasdrubal Gisco, a high-ranking Carthaginian general</note> (and thus the wife of Syphax)—might have to fear. If all other ways are barred, I pray and beseech you: let me die by your hand rather than fall alive into the power of the enemy!"

Masinissa, who was himself a Numidian and (as they all are) prone to passion, gazed at the beauty of her face as she pleaded. Indeed, her misfortune had added a certain solemn and unusual grace to her appearance. Moved by the captive's plea and drawn by desire, and since **Laelius** <note>Gaius Laelius, the Roman commander and second-in-command to Scipio</note> had not yet arrived, he gave her his right hand while still in his armor. Amidst the wailing of the women and the noise of soldiers running to and fro, he raised the supplicant and immediately joined her to himself as a wife, celebrating a wedding in the midst of the chaos, away from the Romans. I believe he judged that by this path he had found a way to satisfy both his own desire and Sophonisba’s prayers.

Finally, on the following day, he received the arriving Laelius. At his command, they returned to the camp with all the royal regalia, the rest of the plunder, and his new wife. At first, they were [praised] by **Scipio** <note>Scipio Africanus, the supreme Roman commander in Africa</note> for the business having been well...

<summary>This page depicts the dramatic meeting between the Numidian king Masinissa and the Carthaginian noblewoman Sophonisba, who successfully pleads to be saved from Roman captivity by marrying him on the spot. The narrative highlights the tension between Masinissa's impulsive romantic gesture and the impending Roman judgment.</summary>

<keywords>Sophonisba, Masinissa, Syphax, Numidia, Punic Wars, Scipio Africanus, Laelius, marriage, Carthaginian history, Roman victory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Second Punic War, specifically focusing on the tragic fate of Sophonisba following the Roman victory in Africa.</meta>

They were received courteously. Then, after Masinissa had been kindly rebuked by him <note>Scipio Africanus, the Roman general</note> for the marriage he had celebrated with a captive of the Roman people <note>original: "captiua romani populi," referring to Sophonisba, who as a Carthaginian was considered a prisoner of war</note>, he withdrew into his tent. There, with all witnesses removed, he was so overcome with sighs and tears that his groaning was heard by those standing outside. As the fate of Sophonisba pressed upon him, he ordered the most faithful of his servants to be summoned. To this servant, he had previously entrusted the keeping of a poison for the uncertain turns of fortune. He commanded him to carry the poison, diluted in a cup, to Sophonisba and to tell her that he would have gladly kept the pledge <term>fidem</term> <note>A Roman concept of a formal, binding promise or duty</note> which he had freely given her, if only he were able. 

However, since those who had the power to do so <note>The Roman authorities</note> were taking away his right to decide, he was now fulfilling the only promise he could—though not without great sorrow—namely, that if she chose to use it, she would not fall alive into the power of the Romans. He urged that she, mindful of her father <note>Hasdrubal Gisco</note>, her country, and the two kings <note>Syphax and Masinissa</note> to whom she had been married shortly before, should take whatever counsel seemed best to her. When she heard this, she said with a steady expression: "I accept this wedding gift; and if my husband has nothing else to offer me..."

<meta>This page continues the biographies of famous women from Giovanni Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris." This entry, chapter 74, focuses on Theosena of Thessaly, whose story takes place during the tumultuous reign of Philip V of Macedon.</meta>

. 74 .

[Woodcut illustration: A small, single-masted sailing boat tosses on the sea near a jagged, rocky coastline. Several figures are huddled in the boat. One person is leaping or being cast into the water, where a large, dolphin-like sea monster waits with an open mouth.]

-> **THEOSENA** <-

**[T]**heosena, a woman of noble Thessalian origin, left behind a lasting testimony of her character—one marked by both tender devotion and grim, severe resolution. She was the daughter of Herodicus, the prince of the Thessalians, during the time when Philip <note>Philip V of Macedon, reigned 221–179 BCE</note>, son of Demetrius, ruled over the Macedonians. She had a sister named Archo, born of the same parents. Their father was first murdered through the wickedness of King Philip; later, as that same treachery persisted over time, their husbands were also brought to a cruel death by the king's hand. Each sister was left a widow with a single surviving son.

Once widowed, Archo eventually married Poris, a leading man among their people, and she bore him several children. Theosena, however, possessed a more steadfast mind; though many noblemen sought her in marriage, she turned them all away and remained a widow for a long time. However, when Archo was taken by death, Theosena was moved by compassion for her young nephews. She feared they might fall into the hands of a new stepmother or otherwise be raised with less care by their father. To ensure she could educate them as if they were her own, she joined herself in marriage to the same Poris—a union that no law of that time prohibited. She began to nourish them with such pious diligence, as if she herself had given birth to them, that it became quite clear her marriage was intended for their service rather than... <note>The text continues on the next page, detailing how she eventually chooses a tragic path to save these children from King Philip's capture.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Theosena of Thessaly, a noblewoman who marries her deceased sister's husband, Poris, specifically to protect and raise her orphaned nephews after King Philip V of Macedon murdered their biological father and grandfathers.</summary>
<keywords>Theosena, Archo, Philip V of Macedon, Herodicus, Thessaly, Macedonia, Poris, motherhood, widowhood, noblewomen</keywords>

That you had married Poris for your own advantage; <note>The text likely refers to Theoxena, a noblewoman who married a man named Poris to help protect her deceased sister's children.</note> things being in this state, it happened that Philip, King of the Macedonians, was of a restless disposition. Likewise, he was planning in his mind a war against the Romans, who were then flourishing throughout the world in famous prosperity. For this reason, having cleared out the old inhabitants with a massive upheaval of his kingdom, he ordered almost all the maritime cities of Thessaly and their people to migrate across to the inland region which was later called Emathia <note>A region of the ancient kingdom of Macedon.</note>. He then granted the vacated towns to Thracians <note>Tribes from the Balkan Peninsula seen as more loyal or rugged soldiers by Philip.</note> as being more suitable and faithful for the coming war. 

When he heard the curses of those who were being forced to leave directed against him, he reckoned that nothing would be safe for him unless he also seized the children of those men whom he had cruelly killed long ago. He ordered them to be captured and held in custody, intending not to kill them all with a single blow, but to destroy them one by one as time passed. It then happened that Theoxena heard the edict of the wicked king. Mindful of the murder of her sister’s husband, she believed her own son and her nephews were destined to perish.

**B**erenice of Pontus, who was also named Laodice <note>original: "leodices"</note>, might perhaps seem to have earned her place among famous women by the luster of her royal blood. However, she is judged far more worthy not for the fervent love she bore her son (a trait most mothers share), but for the merit of her remarkable audacity in taking vengeance on his behalf. Lest this claim seem like a slip of the pen, the matter must be explained in a few words. 

She was the daughter of Mithridates, King of Pontus <note>Mithridates V Euergetes</note>—the same king who had waged war alongside the Romans against Aristonicus <note>A claimant to the throne of Pergamum</note> shortly before, and who was eventually snatched away by a sudden death. She was also the sister of the later Mithridates <note>Mithridates VI Eupator, famously known as Mithridates the Great, a long-term enemy of Rome</note>, the son of the aforementioned Mithridates and a persistent foe in the Roman wars. 

Berenice married Ariarathes, the King of Cappadocia <note>Ariarathes VI</note>. After her husband was killed through the treachery of her own brother Mithridates—carried out by a certain man named Gordius—two sons remained alive. However, at that time, Nicomedes, the King of Bithynia, occupied Cappadocia as if the land were vacant following the king's murder, for he was greedy to possess the kingdom...

**Mithridates** feigned a sense of family duty <note>original: "pietatem," a Roman virtue encompassing duty to family, country, and gods</note>, claiming that he would recover the kingdom for his nephews; under this pretext, he took up arms against **Nicomedes** <note>Nicomedes III of Bithynia</note>. Indeed, when he had learned that **Laodice**, now a widow, had married Nicomedes, his feigned duty turned into a real campaign. Once Nicomedes had been driven out of **Cappadocia** by force of arms, Mithridates restored the ancestral kingdom to **Ariarathes** <note>Ariarathes VII</note>, the elder of the brothers. 

However, Mithridates later regretted this deed and had him killed through treachery. When the other younger brother, who was also named **Ariarathes** <note>Ariarathes VIII</note>, was seen to take the throne after being recalled from Asia by his friends—as it pleases some historians to record—he was also slaughtered through the ambushes of that same Mithridates. 

The unhappy mother <note>Laodice, who was the sister of Mithridates and mother of the murdered princes</note>, deprived of both her children, took this so bitterly that, overcome by grief and forgetting her gender, she seized weapons in a fury. Having harnessed a team of horses, she mounted a chariot; nor did she cease to pursue at headlong speed the fleeing **Caeneus**, the royal henchman and executor of the wicked crime...

**C**hiomara was the wife of Ortiagon <note>Ortiagon was a chieftain of the Tolistobogii, one of the three ancient tribes of Galatia</note>, a petty king of the Galatians <note original: "gallo grecorum">The Galatians were Celtic people who migrated to and settled in central Asia Minor (modern-day Turkey), becoming Hellenized over time</note>. The honor and the exceptional prize of her renown seemed as though it might have been stolen away if her name had remained unknown—which I believe the envy of a barbaric and unknown language buried among the mountain passes and caves of Asia, keeping it hidden from Latin ears. But let it not be said that such a misfortune could prevail, for she is granted the splendor she deserves under her husband’s title, as far as our modest writings are able to provide. 

Therefore, after Antiochus the Great, the King of Syria and Asia, had been defeated by the Romans under the leadership of Scipio Asiaticus, Gnaeus Manlius <note>The Latin text reads "torquatus," but the historical figure was Gnaeus Manlius Vulso, who led the Galatian War in 189 BC</note>, the consul who had been assigned the province of Asia, did not want it to seem that he had moved his troops in vain or kept his soldiers idle. Having cleared the remnants of the enemy from the coastal regions, he marched, by his own decision, into <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the narrative of the Roman invasion of Galatia on the following page</note>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Roman military campaigns, specifically focusing on the aftermath of the Roman victory over the Galatians (Gauls in Asia Minor) led by Gnaeus Manlius Vulso in 189 BC. It highlights the story of Chiomara, a noblewoman of the Galatians.</meta>

After leaving the towns, they retreated to the mountain peaks, which were naturally fortified, with their wives, children, and the rest of their possessions. They defended themselves with weapons and whatever strength they could against the surrounding enemies. However, they were overcome by the harsh strength of the Roman soldiers and driven down the slopes of the mountains. Having slaughtered those who remained, and after a surrender was made, they acknowledged the victory of Manlius <note>Gnaeus Manlius Vulso, the Roman consul who led the campaign against the Galatians in the Galatian War.</note>. There was a huge multitude of captives of both sexes and every age. The centurion in charge of their custody, when he saw the wife of Orgiagon, a Galatian chieftain <note>original: "reguli", meaning a petty king or tribal leader</note>—a woman in the prime of life and conspicuous for her physical beauty—fell into lust for her. Forgetful of Roman honor, he committed a rape upon her, despite her resistance with all her strength. This woman felt such great indignation that she desired vengeance no less than her freedom.

But, cautious for a time, she hid her vow in silence. However, when the agreed-upon money arrived to ransom the captives, the anger glowed anew within the woman's chaste breast. Having meditated on what she must do, and having been released from her bonds, she withdrew to one side with her people and ordered the gold to be weighed for the demanding centurion. While the centurion was focused with heart and eyes on this task, she gave a command in her own language—unknown to the Romans—ordering them to strike the centurion and immediately cut off his head now that he was dead; this head, hidden in the folds of her garment, she carried back unharmed to her people.

And when she came into the sight of her husband, and had recounted what had been done to her as a captive, she threw down what she had brought at his feet, as if it were the price of the disgrace <note>original: "dedecoris"</note> inflicted upon her and the feminine shame she had endured. Who would not call this woman not merely a Roman, but another Lucretia <note>Lucretia was a legendary Roman heroine whose suicide after being raped by the son of the king led to the founding of the Roman Republic; she was the ultimate symbol of female virtue.</note> from the battlefield, rather than a "barbarian" woman! Even while the prison and chains were still in sight, and the victorious weapons rattled around her, and the axe of a cruel punisher loomed over her, and freedom had not yet been fully restored to the woman, the indignation of her violated body drove her honorable heart to such great strength that, if necessary, even again...

To undergo chains, to be dragged into captivity, and to enter a foul prison and offer her neck to the axes—this spirited woman did not tremble, being the famous avenger of a wicked crime. Indeed, by her steady command, she drove the swords of her own men into the head of the wretched violator <note>original: "stupratoris." This refers to the Roman centurion who raped Chiomara, a Galatian noblewoman, while she was a prisoner of war.</note>. To what sharper man, more spirited leader, or more severe commander against those who deserve ill would you compare her? To what more shrewd and daring woman could you go, or to what greater champion and protector of matronly honor <term>matronal honesty: the social and moral dignity expected of a high-ranking married woman</term>? 

This woman saw with wonderful foresight of mind that it would be better to go to certain death than to return to her husband's house with certain disgrace. She realized that she could not testify that her mind remained pure within a violated body except through the greatest daring and danger. Thus, therefore, is feminine honor preserved; thus, once taken, it is recovered. Here, the testimony of a chaste heart is rendered. And therefore, let those in whose souls a care for famous modesty <term>pudicicia: the Roman virtue of chastity and sexual integrity</term> resides look forward; for it is not enough to testify to the sincerity of one’s heart by merely saying with tears and complaints that one has suffered violence, unless one can prove an undefeated spirit through an outstanding deed.

# .78.

## Concerning Tertia Aemilia, wife of the first Scipio Africanus. Chapter 71.

[Decorative initial T] **Tertia Aemilia**, though she was already illustrious through the fame of the Aemilian family from which she drew her splendid origin, as well as from her marriage to that most vigorous man, Scipio Africanus the First <note>Scipio Africanus (236–183 BCE) was the Roman general who defeated Hannibal.</note>, she shone with a far greater light due to her own noble deed. 

For while Scipio—who, in his younger days, restored a young virgin bride to the prince Luceius <note>original: "luceio"; this refers to Allucius, a Spanish chieftain whose fiancée Scipio famously protected and returned during the Punic Wars.</note> in the first flower of her youth and remarkable for her beautiful appearance, returning her with the treasures offered by her parents for her ransom—when he became an older man, he was unable to snatch himself away from the charms of a forbidden passion. Instead, he fell into a love and physical relationship with his little servant girl. 

Now, since it is very difficult to deceive the watchful eye of a devoted love, this was not hidden from Tertia; indeed, as time passed, she realized everything. And who could doubt that she bore this with great pain? For some women assert, putting aside all shame, that nothing more injurious or intolerable can happen to a married woman than for her husband to grant the right of his bed to a foreign woman. And by Hercules <note>original: "edepol", a common Latin oath to the god Pollux or Hercules, used here for emphasis.</note>, I would easily believe it! For whether it comes from the fragility of the sex, or from a poor opinion of themselves, a woman is a very suspicious creature. They think immediately that if anything is done by the husband for another woman, it works to the detriment of the love owed to them. 

But however difficult this appeared, the noble woman bore it with a constant heart. She suppressed the known crime of her husband with such silence that not only no one else, but even the man himself did not realize she had sensed what he was doing. For this discreet wife judged it to be far too unbecoming for it to be publicly known that he, who by his noble virtue had conquered kings and powerful nations, was himself a subject to the love of a mere servant girl. Nor did it seem enough to this most holy woman to have kept this secret while Scipio lived; rather, even after he had died, in order to remove from her husband’s memory <note>The text concludes here at the bottom of the page; the story likely continues with Tertia marrying the girl off to a freedman to protect Scipio's reputation posthumously.</note>

<summary>This page describes the magnanimity of Tertia Aemilia, who discovered her husband Scipio Africanus was having an affair with a servant but chose to keep it secret to preserve his heroic reputation.</summary>

<keywords>Tertia Aemilia, Scipio Africanus, Roman virtue, marriage, fidelity, Aemilian family, Allucius, female character</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the conclusion of a story regarding a virtuous widow’s extraordinary forgiveness toward her husband’s mistress, followed by the beginning of the biography of Dripetrua, daughter of Mithridates.</meta>

...the alluring mark, if somehow the crime could have emerged from elsewhere, and the reason being that she who had shared the bed of such a famous man should not be seen by anyone to fall into a state of hopeless servitude within the empire, or be forced into a less decent life of lust. Lest the reputation of so great a man seem to be undermined by his own desires, his wife first freed the girl <note>original: "manumiſit"; to formally release a slave from legal ownership.</note> with a generous spirit, and then gave her as a wife to a freed slave. 

O woman, to be extolled to the heavens with sacred praise! On one hand, she bore her injuries with a calm and silent mind; on the other, she paid the debts of her deceased husband by being generous to her rival, a mere handmaid. The rarer such an event is, the more splendid we must judge it to be. 

Another woman would have cried out and called together her kinsfolk, her neighbors, and every woman she knew. She would have filled their ears with long-winded talk and burdened them with countless complaints: that she was neglected, that she was abandoned, that she was devalued and held in no esteem by her husband. She would have complained that while he lived she was a widow, cast aside for a little handmaid—a prostitute of low station. She would have cast the girl out of the house immediately; first, she would have <term>sold her under the crown</term> <note>original: "sub corona vendidiſſet"; a Roman custom where prisoners of war or slaves were sold at auction wearing garlands or crowns to indicate they were being sold by the state or under legal authority.</note>, and would have even harassed her publicly with tears and grievances. Such a woman would not have cared that, while defending her own dignity with her gossip, she was staining the famous reputation of a most honorable man.

### Concerning Dripetrua, Queen of Laodicea. Chapter 74

**D**ripetrua was the Queen of Laodicea and the daughter of Mithridates the Great, as we read. Although that loyalty to which we are bound to our parents made her commendable—more than enough, in my judgment—Mother Nature made her even more memorable through a certain unheard-of trait. For (if faith is to be placed in the ancient books) she was born with a **double row of teeth**, providing a monstrous spectacle of herself to all the people of Asia in her time. And while she suffered no impediment in chewing from such an unusual quantity of teeth, she nevertheless did not lack a notable deformity, which she mitigated with praiseworthy loyalty, as has already been discussed. For...

# On Sempronia, Chapter 74

**S**empronia <note>The decorative initial S contains a dragon or serpent.</note> was the daughter of Tiberius Sempronius Gracchus, a most distinguished man of his time, born to Cornelia—the daughter of the elder Scipio Africanus. She was, moreover, the wife of the illustrious Scipio Aemilianus, who later earned his grandfather's surname <note>"Africanus"</note> for the destruction of Carthage. She was also the sister of Tiberius and Gaius Gracchus, and in the greatness and constancy of her spirit, she did not fall short of her ancestors. 

For it is said that after her brothers were killed because of their political uprisings <note>original: "seditiones"; referring to the Gracchi brothers' controversial land reforms and their violent deaths.</note>, she was dragged into judgment before the people by a tribune of the plebs <term>tribune of the plebs: a powerful Roman official elected to represent the interests of the common citizens</term>. This was certainly done with the intent of causing her great mental distress. In that moment, with the crowd favoring the man and the power of the tribunes pressing from all sides, they insisted that she should kiss Equitius—a man from Firmum in Picenum—as if he were her own nephew and the son of her brother Tiberius Gracchus. They were attempting to force her to accept him as one born into the Sempronian family.

Even though she stood in a place where even great princes were accustomed to tremble, and while she was harassed by the discordant shouts of the shameless multitude and threatened by the grim faces of the high-ranking tribunes, her womanly constancy was in no way shaken. For she remembered first that her brother Tiberius had only three sons: one of whom, as a young man, had died while serving in the military in Sardinia; another, a youth, had died in Rome shortly before his father's downfall; and the third, an infant born posthumously <note>born after the death of the father</note>, was being raised by a nurse. 

With a most steadfast heart and a sharp gaze, terrified in no way, she ignominiously rejected this stranger and reckless Equitius, who was attempting to defile the noble lineage of the Gracchi with a lying claim. Neither by any pleas nor threats could she be induced or turned to do what they commanded. This response, so spiritedly given to Equitius...

<summary>This page concludes the story of Hypsicratea's loyalty and begins the biography of Sempronia, focusing on her brave refusal to recognize a political pretender, Equitius, as her nephew despite intense public and official pressure.</summary>

<keywords>Sempronia, Mithridates, Pompey the Great, Scipio Aemilianus, Tiberius Gracchus, Gaius Gracchus, Cornelia, Equitius, Roman history, female constancy, tribunes of the plebs</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page concludes the biography of Sempronia, the sister of the Gracchi, and begins the account of Claudia Quinta. The text discusses Sempronia's famous refusal to acknowledge a false claimant to her family's legacy and the rumors surrounding her husband Scipio's death.</meta>

The rejection and the insolence of that madman <note>Referring to Equitius, who falsely claimed to be the son of Tiberius Gracchus</note> were frustrated. Once the tribunes had investigated the matter more thoroughly and understood it, they praised the perseverance of this noble-minded woman. 

Perhaps some will say that even if Sempronia deserved fame by the right of her ancestors, she should not be ranked among famous women for this constancy alone, on the grounds that women are, by some innate habit, stubborn in their opinions and inflexibly persistent in whatever they decide. I, however—while I do not deny this tendency—believe they are to be praised if they stand upon the truth. To this truth, Sempronia certainly held fast. 

There are others, moreover, who argue she was of such an untamable spirit <note>original Latin: "indomite cervicis," literally meaning "of an untamed neck," a common metaphor for stubbornness or pride</note> that she would never leave an act against her judgment unavenged if given the chance. For this reason, they believe she conspired in the death of her husband, Scipio <note>Scipio Aemilianus, a famous Roman general who was found dead in his bed; many suspected he was murdered due to his political opposition to the Gracchi</note>. This was because, after the destruction of Numantia, when he was asked for his opinion on whether he thought Tiberius Gracchus had been justly killed, he showed no regard for their family ties and praised the savage death of that rebellious man <note>Tiberius Gracchus was Sempronia's brother. Scipio's public approval of his murder was seen as a grave insult to her family</note>.

### Concerning Claudia Quinta, a Roman woman. Chapter 75.

[Woodcut illustration depicting a woman, identified by the label below as Claudia Quinta, kneeling on a riverbank and pulling a large ship with a rope. Three other women stand behind her on the left. On the ship, several men are visible, along with a statue of a goddess on a pedestal. The scene represents the miraculous arrival of the statue of the mother-goddess Cybele in Rome.]

->**CLAUDIA QUINTA**<-

<summary>This page concludes the story of Sempronia, defending her integrity while noting rumors that she helped kill her husband Scipio for insulting her brother. It then introduces the story of Claudia Quinta with a woodcut illustrating her miraculous towing of a ship.</summary>

<keywords>Sempronia, Scipio Aemilianus, Tiberius Gracchus, Claudia Quinta, Roman women, female constancy, Roman history, Cybele</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
.80.

**C**laudia Quinta was a Roman woman; though the parents from whom she was born are not sufficiently known, she nevertheless gained a special renown for herself through a certain remarkable boldness. Because she made constant use of varied and elaborate finery, and walked about with excessive attention to her appearance <note>original: "oris cultu nimio"; likely referring to makeup or hairstyles deemed scandalous for a modest woman</note>, she was judged by the more serious matrons to be not only lacking in honor but also quite immodest. 

Indeed, during the consulship of Marcus Cornelius and Publius Sempronius, specifically in the fifteenth year of the Second Punic War <note>204 BCE</note>, it happened that the Mother of the Gods <note>The goddess Cybele, also known as the Magna Mater, whose cult was being brought from Pessinus in Asia Minor to Rome</note> was brought by ship to the mouths of the Tiber. To receive the goddess from the ship, in accordance with the response of the oracle, Nasica <note>Publius Cornelius Scipio Nasica</note>—judged by the entire Senate to be the best man in the whole city—went with all the matrons to the place where the ship had approached. 

However, even though the sailors wanted to reach the shore, the ship carrying the cult image <term>simulacrum|A cult statue or material representation of a deity</term> became stuck in the shallows of the river. When it seemed that even a great multitude of young men pulling could not move it, Claudia, mixed among the other matrons and conscious of her own virtue, openly knelt and as a suppliant prayed to the goddess: that if the goddess judged her to be chaste, she should follow her belt. 

And immediately rising up confidently, hoping for what she had requested in prayer, she proceeded to tie her belt to the ship and ordered all the young men to move away. No sooner was this done than Claudia, by pulling, very easily hauled the ship from the shallows; while everyone marveled, she dragged it in the direction she desired. From this so wonderful a success, the opinion of everyone regarding her poorly-regarded modesty was immediately reversed into the greatest praise for Claudia. Thus, she who had proceeded to the shore stained with the mark of shameful lasciviousness, returned to her homeland decorated with the remarkable splendor of chastity. 

But even if things turned out according to Claudia's vow, far be it from me to think it the sign of a sound mind to dare such things, however innocently. For to wish to show oneself innocent by doing something that is beyond nature <note>The author is criticizing the use of "ordeals" or miracles to prove innocence, viewing it as a dangerous practice</note> is more a matter of tempting God than of purging the stain of a crime. We must act with holiness.

<summary>This page recounts the legend of Claudia Quinta, a Roman woman who proved her contested chastity by miraculously pulling the grounded ship of the goddess Cybele with only her belt. We must live sacredly; even if we are thought less of as good people, God permits this for our benefit. He truly wishes for our patience to be strengthened. Let pride be taken away; let virtue be exercised; and let us rejoice within ourselves even while he knows others are unworthy <note>The author suggests that inner peace comes from God's recognition of our virtue, even if other people do not see it.</note>. It is enough for us—it is indeed the greatest thing—if we live well with God as our witness. Therefore, if men think poorly of us even when we do well, let us not worry; but when we do poorly, we must strive with all our strength to reform ourselves. Let us rather allow others to think poorly of us than for us to act poorly.

### On Hypsicratea, Queen of Pontus. Chapter 77

**H**ypsicratea—though her origins may be obscure—was nonetheless the wife of Mithridates the Great <note>original: "magni Mithridatis"; Mithridates VI was a famous king of Pontus and a long-term rival of the Roman Republic.</note> and a grand queen of Pontus. She was of remarkable beauty and possessed an unconquerable love for her husband. She is so highly commended for this that she earned perpetual glory for her name. Indeed, while Mithridates was struggling through a long, costly, and perilous war against the Romans, she remained his most faithful and inseparable companion. Although, according to "barbarian" custom <note>The author uses "barbarian" in the classical sense to refer to non-Roman/non-Greek cultures, specifically noting the practice of polygamy.</note>, he had other wives and many concubines, she was inflamed by the torches of extraordinary love. Whether he was traveling through vast regions, entering into battles, or preparing for voyages across the sea, she was always by his side.

Truly, she could not endure his absence. She believed that no one but herself could properly attend to her husband’s needs, and feared that the service provided by his attendants would be unfaithful. To be present and helpful to the man she loved most, she chose a difficult path. Because womanly clothing seemed unsuitable for such a great task and improper for walking beside a most warlike king, she changed her appearance to mimic a man’s character. Above all, she cut her golden hair—in which women take the most pride—with shears. She allowed her face, her chief beauty, to be covered by a helmet along with her hair, and permitted it to be stained by dust, sweat, and the rust of armor. She set aside her golden bracelets, her jewels, and her purple robes that flowed down to her feet, [wearing instead garments that reached] only to the knee...

to cut <note>original: "resecare"; the context implies she cut her hair to look like a man</note> and cover her ivory breast with a breastplate, and to bind her shins with **greaves** <term>greaves: pieces of armor worn to protect the shins</term>; to cast away her rings and the most precious ornaments of her fingers, and in their place to carry a shield and spears made of **ash wood**, and to gird herself with Parthian bows and quivers instead of necklaces; and all this she did so aptly that you would believe a veteran soldier had been made out of a wayward queen. 

But perhaps these things were easy; though she was indeed accustomed to royal chambers, leisure, and luxury, and to seeing hardships only rarely, once these were set aside and she was endowed with a manly spirit, she learned to sit upon horses, remaining invincible even when weighed down by arms. Afterward, she was often seen running at a swift pace through the harshness of mountains and the slipperiness of valleys, overcoming cold and heat by day and night. 

In place of a royal bed, she sometimes lay undaunted on the bare ground or in the dens of wild beasts, her body hardened as sleep forced itself upon her. She was always a companion to her husband—whether he was victorious or a fugitive—a helper in his labors and a leader in his counsels, attentive to everything. 

In short! <note>original: "Quid multa!" a Latin idiom meaning "To put it briefly"</note> She taught her devoted eyes to gaze without horror upon wounds and bloody slaughters, which she herself sometimes caused by pouring out arrows while fighting; and she forced her ears, which were accustomed to songs, to hear the neighing of horses, the tumult of the military, and the **clarions** <term>clarions: high-pitched trumpets used for signals in battle</term> without her mind being stupefied. 

At last, when she had endured many things that would be heavy even for a robust soldier, she followed Mithridates—who had been overcome and exhausted by **Gnaeus Pompey** <note>Pompey the Great, a leading Roman general and rival of Julius Caesar</note>—through the Armenian glades, the hiding places of the Black Sea, and whatever savage nations lay beyond, accompanied by only a few friends. 

Now she revived him in his affliction with the hope of better things, now she soothed him with the comforts she knew he craved; she brought him such great solace that, into whatever solitudes he was driven, he felt himself to be refreshed as if he were in his own marriage chamber. 

O breast, sanctuary of marital sweetness! O inexhaustible virtue of friendship, with what holy strength you have fortified a woman's soul! Truly, no wife has ever endured things so similar to those of her husband, let alone more grievous things. Because of this merit, if the writers of antiquity occupied themselves with her perpetual praise, posterity can rightly wonder at it very little. Yet, from so many labors and such great...

<meta>This page continues a moralizing description of the downfall of women’s character, focusing on the historical figure Sempronia and the loss of traditional modesty.</meta>

Condemned are those characters who, turning licentiously (as often happens) toward lust, gradually lose their feminine <term>hesitation<note>original: "trepiditas"; referring to the modest reserve or bashfulness expected of women in this period.</note></term>; in its place, audacity immediately rises. And once they have gone down that path—such that their <term>virginal honor<note>original: "vginale decus"; "vginale" is a shorthand for "virginale."</note></term> is tainted and the blush of the brow <note>A classic metaphor for the ability to feel shame.</note> is cast aside—we spend our efforts in vain to pull back those who are slipping away. From this point, women do not merely meet the lusts of men, but actively provoke them.

Besides these things, Sempronia <note>Sempronia was a wealthy Roman woman involved in the Catilinarian conspiracy (63 BCE), famous for her intelligence, charm, and lack of restraint.</note> had a very great greed for gold. Just as she eagerly threw herself into shameful gain to acquire it, so too was she lavish in spending it on all manner of crimes—to such an extent that she knew no limit in either her greed or her extravagance. A desire for gold in a woman is a deadly evil and the clearest evidence of a corrupted heart.

So also is detestable prodigality <term>prodigality: reckless extravagance or wastefulness</term>; for whenever it enters a mind that has turned against its own nature—as is the case with a woman in whom stinginess <note>original: *tenacitas*; the author suggests that while women are naturally inclined to hold onto things, a "prodigal" woman is doubly dangerous because she acts against her own nature.</note> is normally fixed—no hope of salvation can henceforth be held for her, except for poverty. A woman’s primary duty <note>original: *p̄sp̄moia*, likely an abbreviation for *praecipua*, meaning "foremost" or "chief."</note> is to look toward those things within the threshold and to preserve with faithfulness that which is brought home by the men. ...at that time they preserved their spirits; nor did they allow any ignominy to defile the glory of their nation’s majesty. ### Concerning Julia, daughter of Gaius Caesar, chapter 79. ###

->**POMPEY** and **JULIA**<-

[A woodcut illustration depicting a scene of distress. On the left, a man identified as **Pompey** is kneeling. In the center, a man holds the reins of a horse. On the right, a woman, **Julia**, is collapsing into the arms of an attendant.]

**Julia** was perhaps the most famous woman in the whole world, both by her birth and her marriage, but she was far more famous for her most holy love and her sudden death. She was the only child born to Gaius Julius Caesar and his wife Cornelia, who was the daughter of Cinna (a man who served as <term>consul|One of the two primary leaders of the Roman Republic, elected annually</term> four times). This Julius Caesar traced his paternal origin back to Aeneas, the renowned leader of the Trojans, through a long line of kings and intermediaries. On his mother's side, he was descended from Ancus <note>Ancus Marcius, the legendary fourth King of Rome</note>, formerly a king of the Romans. Caesar himself was a man most distinguished by the glory of his wars, his triumphs, and his position as perpetual <term>dictator|A ruler with total power, originally a temporary emergency office in Rome</term>. Julia was married to Pompey the Great, who at that time was the most famous man among the Romans.

<meta>This page continues the biography of Julia, the daughter of Julius Caesar and wife of Pompey the Great, following a description of Pompey’s military triumphs.</meta>

...who, in conquering kings, settling their affairs and making them anew, subduing nations, and wiping out pirates, obtained favor by means of the common people and acquired the patronage of the kings of the whole world. He wearyed not only the lands but, for a long time, the very heavens themselves. This illustrious woman, though she was but a young girl and he a man advanced in years, loved him ardently; and because of this, she met a premature death. 

For when Pompey was performing a sacrifice during the elections for aediles <note>Aediles were Roman officials in charge of public buildings, markets, and games.</note>, he was splashed with a great deal of blood from the sacrificial victim he was holding, which was thrashing about from the wound it had received. Because of this, he took off those clothes and sent them home to put on others. It happened that the messenger carrying them met Julia, who was then pregnant, before meeting anyone else. When she saw her husband’s blood-stained clothes, before she could ask the reason, she suspected that perhaps a violent hand had been laid upon Pompey. Believing that she should not survive her most beloved husband if he were killed, she suddenly fell into a dark terror; with her eyes rolling into darkness and her hands clenched, she collapsed and died on the spot. This was a great misfortune not only for her husband and the Roman citizens, but for the entire world in that age <note>Historians note that Julia's death in 54 BCE broke the family bond between her father, Julius Caesar, and her husband, Pompey, removing a major obstacle to the civil war that followed.</note>.

<meta>This page begins chapter 85 of Giovanni Boccaccio’s "On Famous Women" (De Mulieribus Claris). It follows the life of Portia, the daughter of Cato the Younger, and her role in the events surrounding the assassination of Julius Caesar.</meta>

.85.

**P**ortia was the daughter of **Marcus Cato** <note>original: "marci Cathonis"; refers to Cato the Younger (95–46 BCE), a Roman statesman famous for his stoicism and opposition to Julius Caesar.</note>, who, after leading the remnants of Pompey’s army from Egypt through the burning deserts of Libya in Africa, impatient with Caesar’s victory, killed himself at **Utica** <note>original: "vticā"; a city in modern-day Tunisia where Cato committed suicide to avoid living under Caesar's rule.</note>. Indeed, this distinguished woman did not seem to have deviated in any way from her father's courage and perseverance.

Now she—to set aside her other noble virtues—married **Decimus Brutus** <note>original: "decio bruto"; historical accounts usually identify her husband as Marcus Junius Brutus, one of Caesar's assassins, though Boccaccio here uses the name Decimus.</note> while her father was still alive. She loved him so completely and purely that among her various womanly cares, this love was by far the first and most important. Nor could she, at the right time, hide the honorable flames of love within her chaste breast; since these acts have resulted in her perpetual praise, they offer themselves readily to increase her fame.

The pestilential tumult of the civil wars had already quieted down, with Pompey’s supporters everywhere crushed by Caesar. Yet, against him—now **perpetual dictator** <term>dictatorē ppetuū; a title granted to Caesar in early 44 BCE, giving him absolute power for life, which many Romans feared was a move toward kingship.</term> and, as was clearly perceived, aspiring to kingship—the saner part of the Senate conspired. Among them was Brutus himself.

Brutus, conscious of Portia’s integrity, had shared with her the secret of the wicked deed <note>the conspiracy to assassinate Caesar</note>. It happened that on the night before the day broke—the day on which Caesar was to be removed from human affairs by the aid of the conspirators—as Brutus was leaving the bedroom, Portia took up a barber's razor as if she intended to trim some excess from her nails. Pretending that it had fallen on her by accident, she intentionally wounded herself deeply. 

Indeed, when the standing handmaidens saw the blood flowing, they suspected something more serious and raised a cry. Brutus, who was leaving, was called back to the bedroom; he scolded Portia for having usurped the barber's duty. Portia, however, having sent the handmaidens away, said: 

"What you think was done is not at all reckless; I have tested with what spirit I could pierce myself with a sword and endure death, should your undertakings succeed as they must." <note>Portia wounded herself to prove to Brutus that she was strong enough to keep his secret even under torture.</note>

O love of inexhaustible vigor, and a husband happy with such a wife! But what more? The conspirators proceeded with the crime; Caesar was killed, and the killers escaped—though not with impunity—since everything from the Senate turned out contrary to what they had estimated...

The remaining condemned assassins <note>original: "patricide" (parricides); used here to denote those who killed Julius Caesar, often called the "Father of the Fatherland"</note> went their separate ways. **Brutus**, however, and **Cassius**, heading toward the East, gathered considerable forces against **Octavian Caesar** and **Antony**, the heirs of Caesar.

Against these men, when Octavian and Antony had led out their army, a battle was fought at **Philippi**. When it was seen that the factions of Cassius and Brutus were routed, and Brutus himself was also killed; when **Porcia** <note>The daughter of Cato the Younger and wife of Brutus</note> had heard this, she believed that with her husband taken away, no joy would remain for her. 

Judging that she would face death with the same courage with which she once endured the wound of a barber's knife <note>A reference to an earlier event where Porcia wounded her own thigh to prove she was brave enough to keep Brutus’s secrets</note>, she immediately turned to her purpose. Because no instrument for a voluntary death was readily available to her as quickly as her resolve demanded, she unhesitatingly took glowing coals—which she happened to have nearby—and, throwing them into her throat with her hands, she swallowed them. As these coals burned her vitals, the breath of life was forced to depart into death. Nor is there any doubt that...

Furthermore, [she claimed] that she herself desired to become a companion to the hardships of his flight and exile. She did many such things which the unhappy are accustomed to do, while the hiding places of the man <note>Referring to her husband, Lucretius, who was actually hidden nearby.</note> remained under the cover...

# On Hortensia, daughter of Quintus Hortensius. Chapter 82.

[Woodcut illustration showing a woman, Hortensia, addressing a group of seated men, the Triumvirs, with other women standing behind her. A label below her reads "HORTENSIA".]

Hortensia, daughter of the excellent orator **Quintus Hortensius** <note>A famous Roman lawyer and rival of Cicero, known for his "Asianic" style of oratory</note>, is to be extolled with worthy praises. She not only embraced her father Hortensius's eloquence with a lively heart, but also preserved that vigor of public speaking <note>original: "pronuntiandi vigorem"</note> which the occasion required, and which very often is accustomed to fail even in the most learned men. 

During the time of the **Triumvirs** <note>The Second Triumvirate, consisting of Octavian, Mark Antony, and Marcus Lepidus, who ruled Rome together from 43 to 33 BCE</note>, when a multitude of **matrons** <note>Respected married women of high social standing</note> appeared burdened by an almost intolerable tax <note>original: "onere pecuniae exsolvende," a burden of money to be paid</note> due to the pressing needs of the state, not a single man could be found who dared to provide legal defense for such an unusual matter. 

Hortensia alone dared, with a steady mind, to take up the women's cause before the Triumvirs. She pleaded the case so effectively and with such inexhaustible eloquence that, to the great amazement of those listening, it was as if her father's sex had returned in her... <note>The text cuts off here; historically, Hortensia successfully argued that women should not be taxed to pay for a civil war they had no part in starting, leading the Triumvirs to reduce the number of women taxed.</note>

<summary>This page recounts the story of Hortensia, a Roman woman who broke social norms by delivering a successful public oration before the Triumvirs to protest an unjust tax on women.</summary>
<keywords>Hortensia, Quintus Hortensius, Triumvirs, Roman law, oratory, women's rights, taxation, eloquence</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded the story of Hortensia’s speech against the Triumvirs; this page begins with a concluding reflection on her eloquence before transitioning to the story of Sulpicia.</meta>

.87.

I judge that just as silence is to be praised in public when under a matron’s robe, so too, when the opportunity demands, should eloquent speech be raised up, decorated with its own ornament. When this was finally done, not without the greatest brilliance of **Hortensia**, the small remaining part of the tax was easily extracted from the matrons <note>original: "a matronis facile exactum est"; this refers to the success of Hortensia's speech which led the Triumvirs to reduce the number of women taxed from 1,400 to 400</note>. What shall I say I have seen, that the spirit of ancient wisdom so breathed upon the woman **Hortensia**, unless she deservedly followed the name of **Hortensius**? <note>Hortensia was the daughter of Quintus Hortensius Hortalus, a famous Roman orator and rival of Cicero</note>

->### On Sulpicia, the wife of Cruscellio. Chapter 87.<-

**Sulpicia**, the wife of **Lentulus Cruscellio**, won for herself an everlasting fame through her devotion. For **Lentulus** had been <term>proscribed: officially declared an outlaw whose property could be seized and who could be killed without trial</term> by the Triumvirs <note>The Second Triumvirate of Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus</note> in that same political storm mentioned above. Being swift, he protected himself by fleeing to Sicily, and lived there as an impoverished exile. Once **Sulpicia** was informed of this, she decided she wished to suffer his hardships along with her husband. She judged it indecent to share in joyful honors and bright fortune with husbands, yet refuse to bear their calamities and flee when the time came to endure them.

But **Sulpicia** did not find it easy to depart; indeed, she was guarded with the greatest diligence by her mother, **Julia**, so that she would not follow her husband into exile. But what can true love not deceive? Having seized the right moment and put on a slave’s garment <note>She disguised herself as a servant to slip past her family</note>, she deceived her mother and the guards. Accompanied by only two female slaves and as many manservants, this noble woman left her native soil and her ancestral <term>penates: the household gods, representing the heart of the family home</term> and followed her exiled husband.

<summary>This page concludes the story of the orator Hortensia and begins the account of Sulpicia, who risked her life and status to join her outlawed husband in exile.</summary>
<keywords>Hortensia, Sulpicia, Lentulus Cruscellio, Triumvirate, Roman women, exile, proscription, female virtue</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues from a previous discussion regarding the virtues of a devoted wife, concluding the previous chapter before beginning the biography of Cornificia (Chapter 84).</meta>

...is the judgment. One should not always shine with gold and gems; one should not always indulge in finery; one should not always flee the summer sun or the winter rains. The marriage chambers are not always to be tended, nor should one always spare oneself. Rather, together with their husbands—as the unfolding of the fates demands—they must undergo labors, endure exiles, tolerate poverty, and bear dangers with a brave spirit. This is the expected "warfare" <note>The author uses a military metaphor (*milicia*) to describe the hardships of a dedicated wife.</note> of wives; these are the wars, these the victories, and these the conspicuous triumphs of their victories: to have surpassed softness, luxury, and domestic hardships with honor and a constant, modest mind. From this comes their phoenix-like fame and glory.

Therefore, let those women blush who not only follow the mere shadow of happiness with all their might, but even more those who fear "nausea" <note>original: "nausea"; likely referring to the literal sea-sickness of travel or the general discomfort of a difficult life.</note> for the sake of the common marital good. Such women are undone by slight labor; they dread foreign nations and grow pale perhaps even at the hearing of an ox's lowing—though when following after adulterers, they praise the act of flight. To those women, the seas might perhaps be pleasing, and they offer up their souls most swiftly to whatever opportunities arise.

->### Of Cornificia, Chapter 84.<-

Whether Cornificia was a Roman woman or rather a foreigner, I do not recall finding; yet by the testimony of the ancients, she was most worthy of memory. During the reign of Octavian Caesar <note>Augustus, the first Roman Emperor (reigned 27 BCE – 14 CE).</note>, she shone with such poetic skill <term>dogmate</term> <note>Here referring to her mastery of poetic theory and practice.</note> that she seemed to have been nourished not by Italian milk, but by the Castalian spring <note>A spring on Mount Parnassus sacred to the Muses; the author implies her talent was divine/literary rather than merely natural.</note>. She was equally illustrious in glory to her brother Cornificius, a distinguished poet of that same age.

Not content to have excelled in such a splendid faculty with words, and with the sacred Muses urging her to describe Heliconian <note>Refers to Mount Helicon, the home of the Muses.</note> song, she very often applied her learned hands to the pen, rejecting the distaff <note>A tool used in spinning wool, the traditional symbol of domestic womanhood.</note>. She wrote many distinguished and famous epigrams which, in the times of Jerome—that most clear and holy man—were still held in high esteem, as he himself testifies. Whether they have survived down to later centuries, I am not sufficiently certain. 

O, feminine glory! To have neglected womanly things and applied her genius to the studies of the greatest poets. Let the sluggish and those who miserably distrust themselves blush—those who, as if born only for idleness and the bedchamber, persuade themselves they are meant for nothing but the embraces of men and...

...useful for bearing and nurturing children, since they share in common everything that makes men glorious if they wish to apply themselves to studies. This woman was able, by nature—not by casting aside her strength, but through her intellect and watchfulness—to surpass the female sex, and to seek a perpetual name for herself through honest labor; not indeed a common name, but one that is very rare and excellent even among a few men.

## Concerning Mariamne, Queen of the Jews. Chapter 85.

->[Illustration: King Herod speaks to his wife Mariamne. The labels read HEROD and MARIAMNE. Speech bubbles emerge from them: "Go" and "in peace."]<-

**M**ariamne was a Hebrew woman, more fortunate in her lineage than in her husband. She was the daughter of Aristobulus <note>Aristobulus IV, a prince of the Hasmonean dynasty</note>, King of the Jews, and Queen Alexandra, who was herself the daughter of King Hyrcanus. She was born with such great and extraordinary beauty that she was believed not only to surpass all other women of that time in loveliness, but was considered a heavenly image rather than a mortal one. Nor was the testimony of Marc Antony the <term>triumvir</term> <note>One of the three men who shared rule over the Roman Republic; original: "triumviri"</note> lacking for this belief. 

Mariamne had a brother from the same parents named Aristobulus, who was equal to her in beauty and age. Because their father had died, their mother Alexandra greatly desired that the high priesthood be given to her son Aristobulus by Herod—who was Mariamne's husband and the king. At the persuasion of a friend named Gellius, the portraits of both siblings were painted with the skill of the finest artist on a panel and sent to Antony the triumvir...

<meta>This page continues the narrative regarding the Hasmonean princess Mariamne I and her husband, Herod the Great. It follows descriptions of her beauty and focuses here on Herod’s pathological jealousy and his secret orders to have her killed if he should die.</meta>

...of extreme luxury <note>original: "extreme luxurie hominem"; likely referring to Mark Antony</note> to incite his desire and consequently draw him to his own longing, they say [portraits] were sent as far as Egypt. When Antony saw them, he stood for a long time in admiration; then they claim he said that, as far as beauty was concerned, these were surely children of gods. Following this with an oath, he stated he had never and nowhere seen any more beautiful, nor even their equal. 

But returning to Mariamne alone: she was distinguished by a beauty that was, as some say, unheard of; yet she shone far more through the immense strength of her character. When she reached marriageable age, she was wed to an ill-omened man, Herod <note>Herod the Great, reigned 37–4 BCE</note> (son of Antipater), King of the Jews. To her greatest misfortune, she was deeply loved by him because of her loveliness. While he boasted that he alone in the whole world was the possessor of such divine beauty, he fell into such a great anxiety of mind—fearing that no one else should be his equal in this regard—that he began to fear that Mariamne might survive him. 

To avoid this, when he was first called to Egypt to go to Antony to answer for the death of Aristobulus <note>Mariamne's brother and High Priest, whom Herod had drowned</note> (Mariamne's brother, whom he had killed), and later, after Antony's death, when he was to approach Octavian Caesar <note>The future Emperor Augustus</note> to clear himself, if possible, of having provided aid to Antony's friends against him—he left instructions with Cypros <note>original: "cyprinne"; Herod's mother</note> and his friends that if anything happened to him tending toward death—whether caused by Antony, Caesar, or some other accident—they were to kill Mariamne immediately. 

Oh, the ridiculous madness of a king who was otherwise most wise! To be troubled and envious after death regarding another's potential pleasure! 

Mariamne found out what had been done so secretly over the course of time. Since she had already conceived an accursed hatred for Herod following the shameful slaughter of Aristobulus, and judging that she was loved by Herod for nothing but the use of her beauty, her anger overflowed. She took it very badly that her life had been twice condemned by him without merit. And although she had given birth to Alexander and Aristobulus—boys of conspicuous beauty—through him, she could in no way temper her feelings...

### Of Cleopatra, Queen of the Egyptians. Chapter 186

[Illustration: On the left, a man and woman (Antony and Cleopatra) sit at a banquet table with a servant. On the right, Cleopatra lies dead on the ground with asps biting her breasts.]

->**CLEOPATRA** · **ANTONY**<-

**C**leopatra was an Egyptian woman who became a legend throughout the entire world. Although she traced her lineage through many intervening kings back to Ptolemy—the King of Macedonia and son of Lagus <note>Ptolemy I Soter, the general of Alexander the Great who established the Greek dynasty in Egypt</note>—and though she attained the throne (albeit through wicked means <note>original: *per nephas*—an act against divine or moral law</note>) as the daughter of King Ptolemy Dionysus <note>Ptolemy XII Auletes, who styled himself after the god Dionysus</note> or, as others prefer, King Mineus, she possessed almost no mark of true nobility except for her ancestry and the beauty of her face. On the contrary, she became notorious throughout the world for her greed, cruelty, and extravagant excess <note>original: *luxuria*—in this context, it implies a lack of self-control and a devotion to expensive, sensory pleasures</note>.

For (as some authors prefer) to take our starting point from the beginning of her reign: Dionysus, a very great friend of the Roman people, died during the first consulship of Julius Caesar. In his signed will <note>original: *signatis tabulis*—referring to the wax tablets used for legal documents</note>, he left instructions that the eldest of his sons (whom some believe was named Lysanias <note>historically Ptolemy XIII</note>) should take the eldest of his daughters, Cleopatra, as his wife, and that they should reign together after his death. This arrangement was carried out because it was a very common custom among the Egyptians to exclude only mothers and daughters from marriage <note>a reference to the Ptolemaic practice of brother-sister marriage to keep the bloodline "pure"</note>. 

However, Cleopatra was so consumed by a burning lust for power that, as it seemed to many, she did away with that innocent youth—who was both her brother and her husband, then in his fifteenth year of age—using poison so that she might rule alone.

<summary>This page introduces Cleopatra VII, the last pharaoh of Egypt, focusing on her noble Greek ancestry and her initial rise to power. It portrays her as a beautiful but ruthless leader who poisoned her younger brother to secure sole control of the throne.</summary>
<keywords>Cleopatra, Mark Antony, Ptolemaic Dynasty, Ptolemy XII, Egypt, fratricide, royal marriage customs, Roman History</keywords>
