...she gained possession of the kingdom. From here they assert that when Pompey the Great <note>original: *pompeius magnus*</note> had occupied nearly all of Asia with his armies and was heading toward Egypt, he substituted the surviving boy for his dead brother <note>This refers to Ptolemy XIII, whom Pompey supported following the death of Ptolemy XII Auletes</note> and made him the King of Egypt. Indignant at this, Cleopatra took up arms against him. And while matters stood thus—Pompey having been routed by Caesar at Thessaly <note>The Battle of Pharsalus, 48 BCE</note> and the boy-king being with his own followers on the Egyptian coast—Caesar arrived afterward. He found them there waging war against each other. 

When he ordered them to be summoned before him to plead their cases—to say nothing of the young Ptolemy—the woman Cleopatra, armed with her own wiles and being very confident in herself, approached, marked with her royal insignia. She hoped to secure the kingdom for herself if she could draw the "master of the world" into her web of luxury <note>or "lasciviousness"</note>. Since she was most beautiful, and by the art of her sparkling eyes and the eloquence of her speech she could capture almost anyone she wished, she drew the lustful prince into her private quarters <term>contubernium: literally a shared tent or living quarters, implying an intimate domestic arrangement</term> with little effort. For many nights, amidst the tumult of the Alexandrians, she remained his companion; and she conceived (as is generally agreed) a son, whom she later named Caesarion after his father's name. 

Finally, when the boy Ptolemy—though released by Caesar—had turned to war against his own liberator at the urging of his followers, he met with an army at the Delta to face Mithridates of Pergamum, who was coming to Caesar's aid. Being defeated there by Caesar (who had arrived by another route), the boy attempted to escape in a small boat; but being weighed down by the many people boarding it, the boat sank. Thus, with matters settled, the city of the Alexandrians...

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Cleopatra VII, following the death of Julius Caesar and the rise of the Second Triumvirate.</meta>

Hence, with Caesar already killed and Brutus and Cassius defeated, Cleopatra went to meet Antony as he was traveling into Syria. She easily captured the impure man with her beauty and her seductive eyes, miserably ensnaring him in her love. She led him so far that she—who had already killed her own brother with poison—forced Antony to order the slaughter of her sister, Arsinoë, to remove any possible threat to her rule. This took place in the Temple of Diana at Ephesus, where the unhappy girl had fled, seeking safety. Cleopatra claimed this murder as the first "gift" from her new lover to celebrate their adulterous union. 

Once this wicked woman learned Antony's character, she did not fear to demand from him the kingdoms of Syria and Arabia. Indeed, although it seemed an excessive and entirely inappropriate request to him, he nevertheless gave her small portions of both to satisfy the desire of the woman he loved. He also added all the cities situated on the Syrian coast between the Eleutherus River and Egypt, though he kept Sidon and Tyre for himself. 

Having obtained these, she followed Antony as far as the Euphrates as he marched against the Armenians or (as others claim) the Parthians. While she was returning to Egypt through Syria, she was magnificently received by Herod <note>Herod the Great</note>, son of Antipater, then King of the Jews. She did not blush to suggest to him, through intermediaries, that he should sleep with her. Her plan was that if he agreed, she would take the kingdom of Judea from him as a "gift," even though he had received that kingdom not long before through Antony's help. 

But Herod, seeing through her, not only abstained out of respect for Antony, but even prepared to kill her with a sword to free his friend from the stain of such an incestuous woman—and he would have done so had his friends not talked him out of it. Cleopatra, however, being frustrated in her seduction, acted as if she had delayed her journey for business reasons; she leased back to Herod the revenues of Jericho, where the balsam <note>A highly prized resin used for perfume and medicine</note> grew. She later transferred these balsam plants to Egyptian Babylon <note>An ancient fortress city near modern-day Cairo; original: "babiloniam egiptiam"</note>, where they flourish to this day. Then, having received large gifts from Herod, she returned to Egypt. 

Later, she went to meet Antony, who had been summoned as he was retreating from his flight from the Parthians. This same Antony had, through trickery, captured Artavasdes <note>original: "arthabazanẽ"</note>, the King of Armenia and son of the former King Tigranes, along with his sons and governors...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Mark Antony’s infatuation with Cleopatra, focusing on the famous anecdote of the pearl and the dissolution of his political willpower.</meta>

...he had captured him and despoiled him of the greatest treasures; and as he drew him along bound in a silver chain, in order to entice the greedy woman into his embraces, the effeminate man <note>The author uses "effeminatus" to criticize Antony for being controlled by his passions rather than Roman "manly" virtue.</note> cast the captive king, along with all the royal regalia and plunder, into her lap as she approached. Rejoicing in this gift, the most covetous woman embraced the passionate man so enticingly that he repudiated Octavia—the sister of Octavian Caesar—and joined himself in marriage to this woman with his whole heart. And so that he might continually satisfy her gluttony with Arabian ointments, fragrant vapors, and extravagant debaucheries with magnificent feasts, he fell into a discussion; it was as if Cleopatra wished to boast of her own banquets, questioning what magnificence could possibly be added to their daily dinners.

To this, the wanton woman replied that, if she wished, she would consume ten million sesterces <note>original: "centies sestertium." A massive sum in Roman currency, often used in literature to denote impossible wealth.</note> in a single dinner. Antony judged that this could by no means be done, yet he was eager to see the attempt and taste the risk. Taking Lucius Plancus <note>The transcription reads "Plautus," but historical records identify this judge as Lucius Munatius Plancus.</note> as the judge, the meal took place the next day. When the feast had not exceeded the usual custom of eating, and Antony began to mock the wager, Cleopatra ordered the servants to bring the second course at once. Having been forewarned, they brought nothing but a single vessel of the sharpest vinegar. 

She then immediately took from one of her ears a pearl <term>unio: a large, high-quality pearl found alone, literally "unique"</term> of unthinkable price, which she wore as an ornament in the Eastern fashion. Immersing it in the vinegar, she dissolved it and swallowed the liquid. As she was reaching her hand toward the other pearl, which she wore in her other ear as its equal, intending to do the same thing, Lucius Plancus immediately declared that Antony had been defeated. Thus, the second pearl was saved by the victorious queen. 

That very pearl was later divided and taken to Rome to the Pantheon, where it was placed upon the ears of the statue of Venus. For a long time afterward, it bore witness to those who looked upon it of "half of Cleopatra’s dinner." However, as the insatiable woman’s greed for kingdoms grew daily—wishing to embrace everything as one—she demanded the Roman Empire from the drunken Antony, perhaps as he was rising from that very same extraordinary dinner. She acted as if it were within Antony's power to grant it, while he, hardly in control of himself...

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the downfall of Mark Antony and Cleopatra, following Antony's promise to grant her the Roman Empire and the subsequent military conflict with Octavian.</meta>

...less opportunely for his Romans than, having weighed his strength, he promised he would give the empire to her. O good God, how great was the audacity of the one asking! And no less was the folly of the one promising. O "generous" man <note>The author uses 'liberalis' (generous) sarcastically here to criticize Antony’s willingness to give away Rome.</note>—an empire barely acquired over so many centuries, with such great difficulty, with blood shed and death, by so many distinguished men, so many peoples, with such excellent resources, and so many wars—is it now, so foolishly and almost instantly, granted and conceded to a demanding woman, no differently than the lordship of a small cottage?

But what then? Now, because of the divorce of Octavia <note>Octavian’s sister, whom Antony abandoned for Cleopatra.</note>, the seeds of war <note>original: "belli seminarium"</note> were seen to be sown between Octavian and Antony. And so it was done: forces were gathered from both sides to go into battle. Truly, Antony and Cleopatra, adorned with purple sails and a golden fleet, proceeded into Epirus. There, having joined a land battle against the opposing enemies, they withdrew in defeat. Retreating to their fleet, Antony’s forces returned to Actium <note>The site of the decisive naval battle in 31 BCE.</note> to try the fortune of naval war. Against them, Octavian—together with his successful son-in-law Agrippa <note>Marcus Vipsanius Agrippa, Octavian's primary general and admiral.</note>—went to meet them with a huge fleet. He attacked them with marvelous audacity and was fiercely received; for a while, the god of war held the battle in suspense <note>original: "mars dubius pugnam in pendulo"</note>. 

At last, when Antony’s forces seemed to be yielding, Cleopatra—arrogant beyond all others—was the first to flee with the golden ship in which she was carried, along with sixty others. Antony immediately followed her, having cast down his commander's insignia <note>original: "praetoriana insignia"</note>. Returning to Egypt, they deployed their forces in vain <note>original: "in cassum"</note> for the defense of the kingdom, having sent all their children toward the Red Sea. For the victorious Octavian, following them, exhausted their strength in several subsequent battles. When they sought conditions of peace from him and did not obtain them, Antony, in despair (as some would have it), entered the tomb of the kings <note>The Mausoleum in Alexandria.</note> and killed himself with a sword. 

Once Alexandria was captured, Cleopatra tried in vain with her old cunning—just as she had long ago enticed Caesar and Antony into her lust—to also entice the young Octavian. Indignant when she heard she was being saved for a triumph <note>A Roman victory parade where defeated leaders were displayed in chains before the Roman public.</note>, and despairing of her safety, she adorned herself in royal finery and followed her Antony into death, and was placed beside him.

<summary>This page recounts the climax of the civil war between Octavian and Mark Antony, detailing the Battle of Actium, the flight of Antony and Cleopatra to Egypt, and their subsequent suicides following Octavian's final victory.</summary>
<keywords>Mark Antony, Cleopatra, Octavian, Augustus, Agrippa, Battle of Actium, Alexandria, suicide of Cleopatra, Roman Empire, Egypt</keywords>

--- Page 192 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the downfall of Cleopatra and Mark Antony, focusing on the various accounts of Cleopatra's death and her complex relationship with Antony's distrust.</meta>

-> . 82 . <-

Having opened the veins of her arms, she applied venomous snakes to the wounds, being resolved to die. Some say these snakes bring death through sleep; in this sleep, having been released, the unhappy woman brought an end to her greed, her lust, and her life. Although Octavian <note>Later known as Emperor Augustus, the victor of the Roman civil wars.</note> attempted to draw the poison from those wounds so that she might be preserved alive if possible, there are nonetheless others who say she had died before that, and by another kind of death.

For they say that Antony, during the preparations for the Actian War <note>The decisive naval battle in 31 BCE where Octavian defeated the forces of Antony and Cleopatra.</note>, had feared Cleopatra’s potential betrayal to win favor with Octavian; for that reason, he had become accustomed to taking neither drink nor food unless it was first sampled by a taster <term>taster: a servant, often a slave, tasked with eating a portion of food first to ensure it was not poisoned</term>. When Cleopatra noticed this, in order to prove her loyalty to him, she took the flowers from the previous day with which she had decorated festive crowns and, having smeared them with poison and placed them upon her own head, she drew Antony into a game. As the merriment increased, she invited him to "drink the crowns" <note>A banquet custom where flower petals from garlands were dropped into wine before drinking.</note>. When the flowers had been dropped into the cup and Antony was about to drink, he was stopped by Cleopatra’s hand, as she said: "My dearest Antony, I am that Cleopatra whom you fear, and against whom you protect yourself with these new and unusual tasters..."

<summary>This page describes the dramatic death of Cleopatra via venomous snakes and recounts an anecdote where she proves Antony's distrust of her is futile by nearly poisoning him with a floral crown.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Antonia Minor, the daughter of Mark Antony and Octavia, focusing on her widowhood after the death of her husband Drusus the Elder. It transitions into the life of Agrippina the Elder.</meta>

...afterward [she bore] Augustus <note>The original Latin reads "augustum," but historical records and other editions of this text (Boccaccio's "Famous Women") typically list Germanicus, Claudius, and Livilla as Antonia's children; this may be an OCR error or a reference to a title.</note> and Livilla. While her husband was occupied with the Germanic expedition, he perished (as some believe) by poison through the schemes of his brother Tiberius <note>The Roman Emperor Tiberius, who was often rumored by ancient historians to have poisoned his rivals.</note>. After his death, although she was in the flower of her youth and shone with a conspicuous beauty, she decided it was honorable enough for a respectable woman to have married only once. No one could persuade her into a second marriage.

On the contrary, she spent the remainder of her life’s span—under the watchful eye of her mother-in-law Livia—within the confines of her husband’s bedchamber. She lived so chastely and so holily that she surpassed the praise of all the matrons of the past through her celebrated widowhood. Indeed, among the likes of Cincinnatus, Fabricius, Curius, Lucretius, and Sulpicius <note>These are names of legendary Roman figures synonymous with ancient, stern virtue and frugality.</note>, it is considered most holy and splendid—even for women advanced in age and the daughters of the stern Catos—to have led a life without the stain of lust, a feat to be extolled with the highest praise.

If that is so, with what praises shall we honor this young woman, so distinguished by her beauty? Though she was the daughter of Mark Antony <note>original: "marci antonij"</note>—that most foul man—she lived not on isolated islands or in solitudes, but amidst imperial leisure and luxury. She remained virtuous amidst the most burning fires of lust and playfulness surrounding Julia (the daughter of Octavian) and Julia (the daughter of Marcus Agrippa) <note>Both Julias were famous in Roman history for their exile due to adultery and scandal.</note>. She stood firm amidst the obscenities and disgraces of her own father, Mark Antony, and later of the Emperor Tiberius, living in a fatherland that was once frugal but was now given over to every kind of wickedness. Amidst a thousand examples of desire, with a constant spirit and a brave heart, she preserved her chastity—not just for a short time or in the hope of a future marriage, but in obedience to virtue until her old age and death! 

By Pollux <note>original: "edepol"; an ancient Roman oath</note>, no words are left that are worthy enough to describe her. Perhaps there remains something for contemplation which, because it exceeds the descriptive powers of writers, those who consider her merits have left to be sufficiently honored by the sacred depths of the mind.

**A**grippina was the daughter of Marcus Agrippa and Julia, the daughter of the Emperor Octavian Caesar. Even though her son Gaius Caligula <note>The Roman Emperor Caligula (r. 37–41 AD), who was famously eccentric and cruel.</note>—once he had become the ruler of the world—loathed the humble origins <note>original: "rusticitatem"</note> of his maternal grandfather Agrippa, he used to claim that his mother was not actually the child of Agrippa. Instead, he claimed she was the result of an act of incest <note>original: "ex stupro"</note> committed by Octavian with his own daughter, Julia. He foolishly desired it to be believed that he was of a nobler birth because he was born from a mother conceived through such an incestuous union, rather than being the child of a low-born father joined in a lawful marriage. 

But regardless of whose daughter she was, she was married to Germanicus, an outstanding young man of his age and a great asset to the state. As the adopted son of the Emperor Tiberius Caesar Augustus, he brought Agrippina much fame. Yet, she became even more renowned because she resisted the treachery of a most arrogant ruler <note>Referring here to the Emperor Tiberius, her father-in-law, with whom she had a famously hostile relationship.</note> with unwavering resolve. He claimed she bore it with too much impatience that she was not permitted to rule over affairs of state. Later, after she had been provoked by many accusations brought before the Senate, he ordered her to be kept under guard as if she were a criminal. <note>original: "in fontem/insontem" – the text suggests she was treated as guilty despite her actual innocence or status.</note>

Furthermore, this remarkable woman, considering it beneath her dignity to endure the treatment she received from Caesar <note>The Emperor Tiberius.</note>, resolved to escape or end the disdain of this irritable prince through death. Since no other way was easily available, she decided with a noble spirit to summon death through starvation and immediately began to abstain from all food. 

When this was reported to Tiberius, that cowardly man arrived to see where the woman’s fasting would lead, fearing she might withdraw herself from his cruelties by such a certain path and in so short a time. When neither threats nor lashings succeeded in making her take food, he was driven to such a point—lest he lose the object of his cruelty—that he ordered food to be violently forced down her throat. He intended that, by whatever means it was thrust into her stomach, the nourishment would sustain one who wished to perish. 

But the more Agrippina was provoked by these outrages, the more sharpened her resolve became. Persevering in her undertaking, she overcame the insolence of the wicked prince by dying. She showed that while he could easily kill many whenever he wished, he could not, with all the power of his empire, keep a single person alive who was determined to die. Although Agrippina sought much glory for herself among her own people through this death, she left far greater disgrace behind for Tiberius.

## .lxxxviiij.

### On Paulina, a Roman Woman. Chapter 89.

**P**aulina, a Roman woman, achieved an almost indelible reputation through her own rather ridiculous simplicity <note>The author uses the word "simplicitas" to imply a mix of innocence and gullibility.</note>. During the reign of Tiberius Caesar Augustus <note>Emperor of Rome from 14–37 AD.</note>, she was considered as remarkable for the beauty of her face and the grace of her body as she was for being a model of renowned chastity. She devoted herself to nothing with greater zeal than to her husband, and to the service of Anubis <note>The jackal-headed Egyptian god of the dead, whose cult had spread to Rome.</note>, the god of the Egyptians, whom she worshiped with total devotion in hopes of earning his favor. 

As is often the case, beautiful women are pursued by young men—especially those women whose beauty is guarded by a careful reputation for chastity. One such young Roman, whose name was Mundus <note>Decimus Mundus, a historical figure mentioned by the historian Josephus.</note>, began to solicit her intensely: sometimes with his eyes, with charming gestures, and witty remarks; at other times with promises and gifts, or with prayers and flatteries. He did this to see if he could obtain what he so ardently desired, but it was all in vain. This most chaste woman, dedicated only to her husband, let all the lover’s efforts vanish into the breeze. 

When he persisted in his attempts and realized that the woman’s constancy blocked his path, he turned his cleverness toward fraud. Paulina was accustomed to visiting the Temple of Isis every day to appease Anubis with continuous sacred rites. When the young man learned this, his love inspired him to devise an unheard-of trick. Thinking that the priests of Anubis could be of great help to his wishes, he spoke to them and won them over to his plan with very large bribes. 

Under his direction, it was arranged that the priest who appeared most venerable due to his old age would speak to Paulina in a calm voice during her usual visit. He told her that Anubis had come to him in the night and ordered him to tell her that he was greatly pleased by her devotion, and that he desired to have a conversation with her in the temple during the quiet of the night. 

When Paulina heard this, thinking these things were happening because of her own holiness, she took immense pride in what was said. She believed it so completely that it was as if she had received the commands of the god Anubis with her own ears. She reported everything to her husband. He, being even more foolish <term>stolidior: more dull-witted or senseless</term> than his wife, granted her request to spend the night in the temple. Consequently, a bed was prepared in the shrine...

### On Agrippina, the mother of Nero Caesar. Chapter 90. ###

-> [Woodcut illustration: On the left, a woman labeled **AGRIPPINA** and a man labeled **NERO** stand together. In the center, a man labeled **CLAUDIUS** is seated at a table, being served <note>Original Latin: CLAVDIVS. This depicts the poisoning of Emperor Claudius with mushrooms.</note>. On the right, another figure labeled **NERO** stands over a scene where a woman's body is being opened on a table <note>This refers to the gruesome legend that Nero had his mother's womb cut open after her murder to see where he had been conceived.</note>.] <-

**A**grippina, the mother of Nero Caesar, was distinguished by her lineage, her high-born relations, the impiety and monstrosity of her son, and her own nature, no less than by her notorious crimes. For she was the daughter of Germanicus Caesar—a most excellent youth of praiseworthy character—and of the elder Agrippina. She was called Julia Agrippina, and was the sister of the prince Gaius Caligula. She married Gnaeus Domitius, a man of the <term>Ahenobarbus<note>The name means "Bronze-beard." The family was one of the most prestigious in Rome, though often noted for their cruelty.</note></term> family, who was most disdainful and severe. From him she gave birth to Nero, a beast famous to the whole world, who was born <note>original: "premissis ex materno vtero pedibus"</note> with his feet emerging first from his mother's womb <note>In Roman belief, a "breech" birth where the feet came first was considered a terrible omen of a life that would go against nature.</note>. But when Domitius was taken by a sudden disease while Nero was still a small child, and since she was a woman of great beauty, her brother Gaius...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the life of Agrippina the Younger, following the accounts of her mistreatment by her brother, the Emperor Caligula (Gaius).</meta>

...most filthy: he abused her with foul debauchery <note>original: "turpi stupro"</note>. After he was elevated to the position of Prince <note>the Emperor</note>, either because he disapproved of her conduct—perhaps because she had involved herself with Lepidus in the hope of seizing power <note>referring to Marcus Aemilius Lepidus, with whom Agrippina was accused of conspiring</note>—or because he was driven by some rival, he stripped her of nearly all her possessions and banished her to an island. 

When he was finally murdered by his own soldiers and Claudius was substituted in his place, she was recalled by him. In the course of time, when she heard that Valeria Messalina <note>the third wife of Claudius</note> had been struck down by her various deserving crimes, she immediately entered into the hope of gaining the world for herself and her son. Even though she was the daughter of Germanicus, the brother of the unwed Prince <note>Claudius</note>, she used her graceful beauty to compete against Lollia Paulina (who was aided by the freedman Callistus) and Aelia Paetina (favored by Narcissus). With the help of Pallas <note>an influential formerly enslaved advisor</note>, she drew Claudius into a great desire for marriage with her. 

But honor seemed to stand in the way of her wish, because she was his niece—the daughter of his brother. Truly, through a speech by Vitellius, it was arranged that he should be "compelled" into his own desire by the prayers of the senators. At Vitellius's pleading, a decree was made by the Senate which established that uncles could marry their nieces <note>original: "patruos posse neptes inducere"</note>. And so, with Claudius willing and the Senate pleading, Agrippina came to the wedding. She was eventually called "Augusta" and was carried in a carriage <term>carpento; a two-wheeled ceremonial carriage typically reserved for religious use</term> to the Capitol, an honor previously granted only to priests. She then began to vent her rage with punishments against those opposed to her. 

Finally, being a most cunning woman, she seized her moment. Although Claudius had children of both sexes, she persuaded him—at the suggestion of Memmius Pollio, then consul, and with the heavy urging of the freedman Pallas (who was her chief supporter due to his illicit affair with her)—to adopt his stepson Nero. This was something no one remembered ever happening before in the Claudian family. They also betrothed to Nero Octavia, Claudius’s daughter by Messalina, even though she had been promised to the noble youth Lucius Silanus. Once these things were obtained, she felt the "beast had fallen into her trap." Not so much out of boredom with Claudius’s constant drinking bouts, but rather terrified lest Britannicus (Claudius’s son) should reach maturity before his father’s death—and seeing Narcissus as a future obstacle to her plans as he argued much for Britannicus—she decided on the death of Claudius...

### Concerning Epicharis, a freedwoman. Chapter 91.

->**EPICHARIS**<-

[Woodcut illustration titled **EPITHARIS** depicting a scene of torture. On the left, a woman and a man are working at a block. On the right, a woman is confined within a wooden structure, possibly a rack or stocks, with her feet protruding through holes.]

Epicharis is believed to have been a foreign woman rather than a Roman. She was not distinguished by any brilliance of ancestry; born to a father who was a freedman <note>A former slave who had been granted legal freedom.</note>, she was a freedwoman herself. What is even more shameful is that in her early life she took no interest in honorable skills or virtues. Nevertheless, near the end of her life, she revealed a noble spirit with a strength considered manly. As the insolence and debauchery of Nero, the Roman prince, grew among the Romans and all the common people, it reached the point where several senators and other citizens conspired against him, led by Gaius Piso. While they were attempting to bring their plans to fruition through various secret discussions, I do not know by what means, but all the details—and the names of the conspirators—came to the knowledge of the aforementioned Epicharis. Truly...

<summary>This page concludes the account of the death of Agrippina the Younger and introduces Epicharis, a freedwoman who discovered the Pisonian conspiracy against Emperor Nero.</summary>
<keywords>Nero, Agrippina the Younger, Epicharis, Pisonian Conspiracy, Gaius Piso, Misenum, Roman Empire, freedwoman</keywords>

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Pisonian conspiracy against the Emperor Nero, focusing on the actions of the freedwoman Epicharis as she attempts to recruit military support for the plot.</meta>

Since the work of the conspiracy was dragging on too long due to their own hesitation, as if she were overcome by weariness, she withdrew to Campania <note>a fertile region in southern Italy, popular as a retreat for the Roman elite</note>. While she happened to be staying at Puteoli <note>modern-day Pozzuoli, a major Roman port city</note>, so as not to let the time pass uselessly, she met Volusius Proculus, a chiliarch <note>originally: "chiliarchum"; a military officer in command of a thousand men, here acting as a naval captain</note> and prefect of the Roman fleet. This man had previously been the murderer of Agrippina <note>Agrippina the Younger, Nero's mother, who was assassinated in 59 AD with Proculus's assistance</note>. 

Thinking it would be of great advantage to the conspiracy if she could draw him over to their side, she recounted in a long sequence all of Nero’s crimes, his disdain, his foolish behaviors, and his acts of insolence. From there, she spoke of the Emperor's ingratitude toward Proculus himself <note>The text suggests Proculus felt he had not been sufficiently rewarded for his role in the murder of Nero's mother</note>...

<summary>This passage describes the freedwoman Epicharis attempting to recruit Volusius Proculus, a disgruntled naval officer who helped kill Nero's mother, into the Pisonian conspiracy.</summary>
<keywords>Epicharis, Volusius Proculus, Pisonian conspiracy, Nero, Agrippina, Puteoli, Roman Navy, treason</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
98

...of the youths <note>The author refers here to the young men involved in the Pisonian conspiracy against the Emperor Nero</note> who, far from being willing to suffer for their own safety what this woman <note>Epicharis, a freedwoman who refused to name conspirators even under extreme torture</note> suffered for the sake of others, could not even bear to hear the names of the torture instruments. Instead, they immediately told the questioners everything they knew about the conspiracy. In this way, no man spared himself or his friends, while this renowned woman spared everyone but herself.

I might believe that the Nature of things sometimes wanders into error while binding minds to mortal bodies—specifically, by pouring into a female breast a spirit she intended to place in a man. But since God himself is the giver of such gifts, it is wrong to believe that He falls asleep at His work. We must therefore conclude that all souls are created perfect; yet does not the work itself show what we are? Men should certainly blush to be overcome in the endurance of hardships, not only by a woman of "loose" reputation but by any woman, however steadfast. For if we are the superior sex, is it not fitting that we also excel in courage? If we do not, we are rightly seen to have crossed over into an unmanly <term>effeminacy</term> <note>original: "effeminati" – in this period, the term was used as a moral critique of men who lacked the Roman virtue of "fortitudo" or courage</note> of character.

## Concerning Pompeia Paulina, wife of Lucius Annaeus Seneca, Chapter 92

->**SENICA • POMPEIA • PAVLINA**<-

<note>This woodcut shows the philosopher Seneca and his wife Pompeia Paulina sitting in separate wooden tubs. A figure in a long robe stands between them, holding Paulina’s arm and using a blade to open her veins. Seneca was ordered by Nero to commit suicide, and Paulina chose to die with him, though she was eventually saved on the Emperor's orders.</note>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of famous women, following the biographies of figures from Roman history. It focuses on the Stoic virtue of Pompeia Paulina, wife of the philosopher Seneca, during the reign of Nero.</meta>

**P**ompeia Paulina was the illustrious wife of Lucius Annaeus Seneca, the tutor of Nero. Whether she was Roman or of foreign birth, I do not recall reading; nevertheless, while I consider the glory of her spirit, I should prefer to believe she was Roman rather than a foreigner. Although we may lack certain knowledge of her origins, we certainly do not lack the testimony of illustrious men regarding the most certain example of her devoted love for her husband.

It was indeed believed by the most honorable men of that age that it was much more through Nero's cruelty than through any crime of Seneca's <note>Seneca had been Nero's advisor, but the relationship soured as Nero became more tyrannical.</note> that this happened: the same Seneca—an old and most celebrated man—was branded with the stain of the Pisonian conspiracy <note>original: "pisoniane coniurationis." A failed plot in 65 AD to assassinate Nero and replace him with Gaius Calpurnius Piso. Nero used the discovery of the plot to eliminate many of his critics.</note> (if it can be called a stain for a man to act against a tyrant). Under the shadow of this event, a path was prepared by Nero himself to vent his rage against Seneca, on account of an old grudge—and primarily because of his innate hatred of virtue.

Be that as it may, some thought that through the urging of Poppaea and Tigillinus <note>Poppaea was Nero's wife and Tigillinus his advisor; both are historically portrayed as malicious influences who encouraged Nero’s paranoia.</note>—the Emperor's sole refuge of cruelty—matters went so far that word was sent to Seneca via a centurion that he should choose his own death. When Paulina saw him preparing himself for the execution, putting aside the consoling charms of her husband by which he exhorted her to live, she determined—moved by the impulse of her most chaste love—to embrace death, and that same kind of death, with a brave spirit along with her spouse. Her intent was that those whom an honorable life had held bound together, a single death might dissolve once they were conquered.

And when she had fearlessly entered the warm water <note>The method of suicide chosen was to open the veins while sitting in a warm bath to speed the bleeding, a common practice among the Roman elite when ordered to die.</note> and opened her veins at the same hour as her husband to pour out her life spirit, she was snatched from death by slaves. This was done by order of the Prince <note>Referring to the Emperor Nero.</note>, who held no particular hatred toward her and was striving to suppress a little of the infamy of his innate cruelty by resisting her death. But her blood was not forced to stop so quickly that this excellent woman did not bear witness, through a lifelong paleness, that she had let out a great deal of her vital spirit along with her husband. Finally, when she had preserved the memory of her husband through a few years of praiseworthy widowhood—remaining, since she could not be otherwise, Seneca's wife in name at least—she ended her days. What besides the sweetness of love and...

<summary>This page tells the story of Pompeia Paulina’s attempt to commit suicide alongside her husband Seneca after he was ordered to die by Nero. Although she survived by Nero's order, her permanent paleness served as a testament to her devotion.</summary>
<keywords>Pompeia Paulina, Seneca, Nero, Pisonian conspiracy, Roman suicide, Poppaea, Tigillinus, Stoic virtue, devotion</keywords>

# .lxxxxi. <note>Original page number 91</note>

This conspicuous sign of piety and the venerable sanctity of marriage might have persuaded an excellent woman to prefer dying honorably with her elderly husband, if it were possible, rather than preserving her life (as most women do) by entering into second marriages—not without a sense of shame. 

In fact, to the great disgrace of matronly modesty <note>The author refers to *pudicitia*, the classical Roman ideal of female virtue and sexual fidelity to a single husband</note>, it is so common for some these days to enter into not just a second or third marriage (which is common to nearly everyone), but even a sixth, seventh, or eighth if the occasion arises. They carry the wedding torches <note>original: *inferre faces*; a reference to the Roman custom of leading a bride to her new home with a torchlit procession</note> into the bedchambers of new husbands so frequently that they seem to have adopted the habits of common prostitutes, whose custom it is to change bed-partners constantly by spending nights with new men. 

These women approach their frequently repeated marriage rites with no different an expression than if they were performing a most holy service to virtue itself. Indeed, it is not entirely certain whether such women should be said to be emerging from a cell in a brothel <term>lupanar: a Roman brothel, often a small "crib" or cell</term> or from the bedroom of a deceased husband. Nor would I hesitate to wonder who acts more dishonorably: she who enters <unclear>am—</unclear> <note>The sentence ends abruptly at the bottom of the page</note>

**S**abina Poppaea was a famous Roman woman of illustrious birth. She was the daughter of Titus Ollius, <note>original: "T. ollij"</note> a man who was not indeed of the highest nobility; however, she did not take her name from him, but from her maternal grandfather, Poppaeus Sabinus. He was a man of great renown, distinguished by the <term>triumphal honors<note>A prestigious public recognition awarded to successful military commanders in Ancient Rome.</note></term> and a notable <term>consulship<note>The highest elected political office of the Roman Republic and an honorary title during the Empire.</note></term>. Nor would other womanly gifts have been lacking to her, if only her spirit had been honorable.

For she possessed an envied beauty and was very similar to her mother, who, in her own years, surpassed all other Roman women in loveliness. Furthermore, her speech was charming and of a laudably sweet resonance; her intellect was excellent and versatile, had she used it for honorable arts. 

It was her constant habit to display modesty in public, while in secret she practiced wantonness <note>Boccaccio frequently includes such moralizing commentary on the perceived "hidden" vices of the women he profiles.</note> (a common vice among women). And although her appearances in public were rare, she did not lack artifice in them. For when this clever woman realized that the crowds, and especially the leading men, were most delighted by the sight of her face, she always went out with it partly veiled. She did this not indeed to hide what she desired others to covet, but rather so that she would not sate the eyes of onlookers with too generous a display; instead, she wished to leave them with a longing for what she had hidden behind the veil. And lest I should run through all her habits, since she never...

She spared her reputation <note>original: *famae parceret*</note> only so far as she directed her lust toward where profit showed the way, making no distinction between husbands and adulterers. Before long, she was even made Otho's wife. 

Indeed, Otho was either less cautious due to the fervor of his love, or perhaps he was unable to tolerate the petulant ways of the woman and therefore tried to draw Nero into desiring her. Or perhaps it was simply Poppaea's destiny at work. Rising from the Emperor's banquet, Otho used to frequently repeat that he was returning to her—the woman to whom the gods above had granted all nobility, elegance of manners, and divine beauty. In her, he claimed, resided the prayers of all mortals and the joys and pleasures of the fortunate. 

Nero’s lust was easily irritated by these words. Without long delay, a way of approaching her was found, and she, willing and desiring, came into the prince's embrace. Nor did she delay much; Nero was so ensnared by her artful caresses that he believed those things Otho used to say were most true. When this most sagacious woman realized this, she hid what she actually wanted. Seizing the right time, drenched in fake tears, she said she could no longer endure her love for him since she was bound to Otho by marital right, and she perceived that the prince was held by the favor of **Acte**, a mere little slave-girl mistress <note>original: *ancillule pellicis*—referring to Claudia Acte, a former slave who was Nero's first major love interest, much to his mother's dismay.</note>. 

The result of this was that Otho was removed from the city under the hope of an "honor," being sent as governor to the province of **Lusitania** <note>A Roman province comprising modern-day Portugal and parts of western Spain.</note> and was excluded entirely from Rome. Then Poppaea began to rail against **Agrippina**, the prince's mother, saying that the prince enjoyed neither power nor even liberty, and that he was a tiny child being pulled about by the will of a nurse. Since no one opposed these attacks due to the hatred almost everyone felt for Agrippina’s pride, it happened that Nero's...

### .Chapter 100.

**T**riaria was a woman known for no other distinction of her lineage save that she was the wife of Lucius Vitellius, brother to the Roman Emperor Aulus Vitellius <note>Aulus Vitellius was the third of four emperors who ruled in the chaotic year 69 AD</note>. Whether due to a burning love for her husband or an inherent cruelty of spirit, her ferocity was so great that she was deemed worthy of record, for her behavior was quite contrary to the usual manners of women.

Therefore, while the Emperor Vitellius and Vespasian <note>Vespasian would eventually win this civil war and found the Flavian dynasty</note> were in conflict over the leadership of the Empire, it happened that a group of gladiators under the command of a man named Julianus, along with many rowers from the Roman fleet—stationed not far from Mount Circeo under the prefect Apollinaris—had occupied Tarracina, a town of the Volsci. These men favored Vespasian’s cause. However, because they held the town with such negligence and laziness, a certain slave provided information that allowed Lucius Vitellius to enter the city by night. While they, half-asleep, were grabbing their weapons... <note>original: "arripie[ntes]" — the text cuts off mid-word here as the soldiers were surprised in their beds.</note>

## On Proba, the wife of Adelphus, Chapter 97

**P**roba <note>Faltonia Betitia Proba (c. 306–366 AD), a Roman Christian poet.</note>, most worthy of being remembered by both her deeds and her name for her knowledge of letters, was a remarkable woman. So that her nobility may not be unknown, her origin is pleasing to some, and I believe by conjecture that she was Roman; but other very famous men assert she was born in the town of Ortona and was the wife of a certain Adelphus and of the Christian religion.

It cannot be clearly perceived under which teacher she became so proficient in the <term>liberal arts</term> <note>The traditional curriculum of grammar, logic, rhetoric, arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy.</note>. But among her other studies, she became so learned and familiar with the poems of Virgil through "wakeful care" <note>original: "virgili cura" — a clever pun in the Latin text playing on the name of the poet Virgil and the word for "vigilant" or "wakeful."</note> that she seems to have always held almost his entire work in her sight and memory, as a witness to what she composed. 

While she was perhaps reading Virgil with a more sharp-sighted attention, the thought occurred to her that from those verses, the whole history of the Old Testament and the sequence of the New could be described in a calm, prompt, and robust <note>original: "succi pleno", literally "full of juice," meaning her writing was rich and substantial.</note> manner. 

Indeed, it is not without wonder that such a sublime consideration entered a woman's brain, but it was far more wonderful that she committed it to execution. Therefore, giving her effort to this pious concept, she turned now here, now there through the *Bucolics*, the *Georgics* <note>Two of Virgil's major works; the Bucolics (pastoral poems) and Georgics (poems on farming).</note>...

<summary>This page introduces Proba, a Roman Christian poet, and describes how she conceived the idea to retell the Bible using only the verses of the pagan poet Virgil.</summary>

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the Roman poet Faltonia Betitia Proba, likely from a Renaissance collection of famous women. The previous context would have introduced her noble status and her transition from secular to sacred poetry.</meta>

.Chapter 5.

...to have had such knowledge: a thing which, we note with sorrow, happens to very few men in our own age. Furthermore, this distinguished woman wanted her composed work to be called a *cento* <term>Cento: A literary work made up of various phrases from other authors, like a patchwork quilt</term>; this is a work we have often seen ourselves. And the more we consider it worthy of eternal memory, the more we find it hard to believe that the genius of this famous woman was devoted to this task alone. Rather, I believe that if she had been granted more years of life, she would have composed other praiseworthy works which—due to the laziness of scribes <note>original: "librariorum desidia"</note> and to our great disadvantage—have failed to reach us. 

Among these (as some believe) was a *Homeric Cento*, created with the same skill and from the same material as she had used for Virgil, but using verses taken from Homer <note>original: "ex omero sumptis carmibus"</note>. If this is true, it adds even greater praise to her name, implying she knew Greek literature as perfectly as she knew Latin. 

But I ask: is it not a wonderful thing to hear of a woman scanning the poems of Maro <note>original: "Maronis," the family name of the poet Virgil</note> and Homer, selecting parts fit for her own work and weaving what she read into an artistic context? Let the most learned men look upon her: she who is so distinguished in sacred letters...

<meta>This page introduces the biography of Faustina the Younger, daughter of Antoninus Pius and wife of Marcus Aurelius. It focuses on her divine beauty, her official titles, and the beginning of the discussion regarding her controversial reputation.</meta>

[Woodcut illustration: A scene depicting Faustina Augusta in bed, surrounded by several figures. One man on the left is labeled ->ANTHONINVS<-. Above the bed is the label ->FAVSTINA AVGVSTA<-.]

**[Decorative Initial F]austina Augusta**, who was also later enrolled among the gods <note>original: "inter divos" — This refers to the Roman practice of 'deification' where an emperor or empress was officially declared a deity after death.</note>, attained great glory both in life and in death. This was due more to the kindness of her husband than to her own conduct. She was, in fact, the daughter of the Emperor Antoninus Pius <note>Reigned 138–161 AD</note> and his wife Faustina [the Elder]. She was joined in marriage to Marcus Antoninus <note>Marcus Aurelius</note>, who had already been adopted as a son by Antoninus Pius. After her father’s death, she ruled alongside her husband, and by a decree of the Senate, she was titled **Augusta** <term>Augusta</term> <note>The highest title for a woman in the Roman Empire, denoting her status as Empress.</note>—a glory of no small significance for a woman at that time. For although the title "Augusta" had been given to wives by preceding Emperors, I find that to no woman before her had it been granted by an official decree of the Senate.

Furthermore, she was of such exquisite beauty that it was believed something divine was mixed into her mortal form. To ensure this beauty was not consumed by old age or death, it was arranged that her image—both as a young girl and in more advanced age—be engraved on gold, silver, and bronze coins, which endure even to this day. In these coins, although the expression of the mouth, the movement of the eyes, the vivid color, and the cheerfulness of the face are lacking, the physical features nevertheless bear witness to her great beauty. Indeed, as much as she was celebrated by the fame of the whole world, she was equally marked by the stain of shameful unchastity <note>Faustina the Younger was frequently accused by ancient historians (such as in the 'Historia Augusta') of infidelity, despite her husband’s continued devotion to her.</note>...

This was an honor that had happened to no woman among the Romans before. And since he had already called her **"Mother of the Camps"** <term>Mater Castrorum: a prestigious title given to empresses who accompanied their husbands on military campaigns</term>, Antoninus had a magnificent temple built for her in the place where she had died. He ordered conspicuous statues of her to be erected and established a group of girls as priestesses for the temple, whom he commanded to be called the **"Faustinianae"** <note>original: "faustinianas." These were likely the "Puellae Faustinianae," a charitable foundation for orphaned girls established in her memory</note>. Thus, in that place and over time, Faustina was held in such high honor that the divinity she was granted restored the reputation that her perceived luxuries <note>original: "luxuria." This refers to contemporary rumors of her infidelities and loose living</note> had seemed to diminish.

<meta>This page continues a historical account of the Roman Empire, specifically focusing on the reign of the Emperor Elagabalus (reigned 218–222 AD) and the unprecedented political influence of the women in his family.</meta>

->5.<-

She shone forth having attained dominion, made all the more radiant for this one cause. For, granted that the wicked **Elagabalus** <term>Elagabalus: A Roman Emperor notorious in historical accounts for religious eccentricity and decadence.</term> was in power, he recognized that he had been made emperor through the deeds of his grandmother, and consequently, those of his mother. He promoted her with such honors, as if by way of reward, that he did almost nothing except by her arrangement. 

On the very day he entered Rome, he held a meeting of the **Senate**. He ordered that his mother be requested to attend the session. At the consul’s invitation, she entered; and there—where a seat had been prepared for her among the other senators in the usual fashion—she gave her opinion on the matters to be debated. <note>original: "de agēdis ſentētiam dixit"</note> Memory records that such a thing happened to no other woman. 

O, what a shameful spectacle! To have seen a little harlot <note>The author uses the derogatory term "meretriculam," reflecting the deep-seated Roman prejudice against women in political spaces, often equating female power with sexual deviance.</note>, plucked but yesterday from a brothel, sitting among men of the greatest gravity. To have heard her—one accustomed to the company of pimps—delivering her opinion in the place where the fates of kings were decided. O, ancient liberty! O...

<summary>This page describes the scandalous arrival of Emperor Elagabalus in Rome, focusing on his decision to allow his mother to sit and vote in the Roman Senate, an act the author views as a supreme disgrace to Roman tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Elagabalus, Roman Senate, Julia Soaemias, Julia Maesa, female political power, Roman history, scandal, ancient liberty</keywords>

--- Page 218 ---
<meta>This page concludes the account of Julia Soaemias (referred to here as Semiamira) and her "Women's Senate" before beginning the biography of Zenobia, Queen of Palmyra.</meta>

.C.v.

...was established. Indeed, it was decreed in that assembly which clothing each woman was permitted to use and which ornaments were lawful for each. It also decided to whom one should yield the path, who should rise for another, and who should be entitled to come for a kiss <note>original: "ad osculum venire" — a formal greeting indicating social rank</note> among the matrons. Furthermore, it dictated who should travel by light carriage, who on horseback, and who by wagon, mule-drawn litter, or sedan chair, and matters of this kind. Although these things seemed rather empty and more like a game than reality—conceived mostly out of feminine vanity and the foolish judgment of the common people—they were nonetheless seen as very great matters at that time. Truly, since nothing violent is lasting, these things were easily dissolved and vanished into thin air. For while Julia Soaemias <note>original: "Semiamira," the mother of the Emperor Elagabalus</note> conducted herself with matronly ritual in the prince's court, her son was occupied with shameful and excessive lusts. It reached such a point that Elagabalus was killed by his own men as he deserved. And with him, Julia Soaemias, her shadowy splendor abandoned, was slaughtered and thrown into a sewer <note>original: "cloaca"</note>. From there, together with the corpse of her son, she was dragged into the Tiber river, so that the course of his youth might not seem to differ from its end. This is something that we, living wretchedly, hardly even consider.

## Of Zenobia, Queen of the Palmyrenes, Chapter 97

**Z**enobia was the queen of the Palmyrenes, a woman of such exceptional virtue <term>virtus: a Latin term implying not just moral goodness, but also courage, strength, and "manly" excellence</term> that, according to ancient writings, she deserves to be ranked in fame above the women of other nations. Above all else, she was distinguished by her lineage. For she claimed a noble origin from the Ptolemies, the kings of the Egyptians, though the names of her parents have not been preserved in memory. It is said that from her childhood, she utterly rejected womanly duties. Once her young body had grown into strength, she spent most of her time in the woods and groves; equipped with a quiver, she was a threat to stags and goats through her running and her arrows. Later, when she had attained more vigorous strength, she dared to grapple with bears and pursue leopards and lions; she would wait for those she encountered, capture and kill them, and drag them off as prey. Fearless, she would run through various glades and the rugged dens of the mountains, searching out the lairs of wild beasts and taking her sleep under the open sky even throughout the night. She endured rains, scorching heat, and bitter cold.

<summary>This page narrates the violent downfall of the Roman Empress Julia Soaemias and her son Elagabalus, followed by the introduction of Zenobia of Palmyra, detailing her royal Egyptian heritage and her rugged, outdoorsy upbringing as a hunter.</summary>
<keywords>Julia Soaemias, Elagabalus, Zenobia of Palmyra, Roman Empire, Women's Senate, hunting, Ptolemaic dynasty, virtue</keywords>

--- Page 219 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of Zenobia, the Queen of Palmyra, focusing on her transition from a youth of "virile" athletic pursuits to her role as a military leader alongside her husband, Odenathus, during the mid-3rd century crisis of the Roman Empire.</meta>

With wonderful endurance, she surpassed the passions of men. Accustomed to spurning <term>companionship</term><note>The original Latin "contubenia" refers here to sexual or domestic intimacy, which the text suggests she avoided in favor of a stoic or virginal lifestyle.</note> and cherishing her virginity with great effort, she put to flight all "womanly softness." They say she was so hardened into a masculine strength that she surpassed the young men of her own age in wrestling matches and in all games with all her might. At last, as she reached a marriageable age, by the advice of friends, they wished her to be married to **Odenathus**, a young man hardened by similar interests and by far the most noble prince of the <term>Palmyrenes</term><note>Citizens of Palmyra, a wealthy trading city in modern-day Syria that became a powerhouse during the Roman Empire.</note>.

She was beautiful of body, although a little dark in complexion—for thus are all the inhabitants of that region because of the burning sun. Furthermore, she was graced with black eyes and snowy-white teeth. When she saw that Odenathus—after the Emperor **Valerian** had been captured by **Shapur**, King of the Persians, and condemned to a shameful slavery, and while Valerian's son **Gallienus** grew numb with effeminacy—was intent on occupying the Eastern Empire, she did not forget her former hardiness. She arranged to hide her beauty with armor and decided to serve as a soldier under her husband.

With him, having assumed the royal name and finery, and together with her stepson **Herodes**, she gathered her forces and advanced spiritedly against Shapur, who was already occupying **Mesopotamia** far and wide. Sparing no labors—performing the duties now of a leader, now of a soldier—she not only surpassed that most fierce man, experienced in wars, by the strength of her arms, but it is believed that through her efforts Mesopotamia came back under Roman jurisdiction. Shapur's camp was captured along with his concubines and immense plunder, and he was pursued and driven as far as <term>Ctesiphon</term><note>Original: "Theliphotẽ." This likely refers to Ctesiphon, the capital of the Sassanid Persian Empire.</note>. 

Not long after, she took care with vigilant zeal to crush **Quietus**, the son of **Macrianus**, who had entered the empire of the East under his father’s name. And when they already held the entire East (which looked to the Romans for leadership) and kept it pacified as one with her husband, behold: Odenathus, together with his son Herodes, was killed by his cousin **Maeonius**. 

Some assert this was done out of envy; others believe that **Zenobia** had given her consent to the death of her stepson Herodes, because she had often condemned his "softness," and so that the succession of the kingdom might fall to her own sons, **Herennianus** and **Timolaus**, whom she had borne to Odenathus. While Maeonius was ruling, she remained quiet for some time.

<summary>This page describes Zenobia's physical strength and beauty, her military partnership with her husband Odenathus against the Persian King Shapur, and the subsequent assassination of Odenathus and his eldest son.</summary>
<keywords>Zenobia, Odenathus, Shapur I, Valerian, Mesopotamia, Palmyra, Ctesiphon, Herodes, Maeonius, Roman-Persian Wars</keywords>

--- Page 220 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of the Palmyrene Empire, following the death of Septimius Odenatus and the rise of his widow, Zenobia.</meta>

# 105.

After <note>Odenatus, the ruler of Palmyra</note> was murdered by his own soldiers within a short time, leaving the empire as if it were a vacant possession, a woman of noble spirit <note>This is Zenobia, the Queen of Palmyra (regnant 267–272 AD), who famously challenged the authority of Rome.</note> immediately stepped into the governance of the realm. While her sons were still young children, she appeared before the people dressed in the <term>imperial military cloak</term> <note>original: "imperiali sagulo"; the *sagum* was a cloak worn by Roman soldiers and generals, signaling her role as commander-in-chief</note> draped over her shoulders and adorned with royal ornaments. Governing in the name of her sons, she ruled the empire with much more vigor than was thought suitable for her sex, and not at all sluggishly. Indeed, neither the Emperor Gallienus nor the Emperor Claudius <note>Claudius II Gothicus, who ruled 268–270 AD</note> who followed him dared to attempt any action against her.

In a similar fashion, neither the eastern Egyptians, nor the Arabs, Saracens, or the Armenian peoples attacked; instead, fearing her power from the start, they were content merely to be able to protect their own borders. She possessed such great skill in warfare and such sharp military discipline that her own armies both highly esteemed and feared her in equal measure. She never addressed her troops unless she was wearing a helmet, and during military expeditions, she very rarely used a carriage; more often, she traveled on horseback.

<summary>This page describes the rise of Queen Zenobia of Palmyra following the assassination of her husband, highlighting her military prowess, her command over her troops, and the respect she commanded from both Rome and neighboring nations.</summary>

## On Constance, Empress of the Romans and Queen of Sicily. Chapter 102.

[Woodcut illustration showing a woman in bed, a baptism of a baby labeled **FREDERICK**, and two figures labeled **CONSTANCE** and **HENRY**.]

Constance shone forth upon the lands as Empress of the Romans from the world's highest pinnacle. But because the glory of her beauty <note>original: "come decus," literally "the glory of her hair," but often used to mean physical beauty or grace</note> seems to have diminished in the eyes of those looking back upon her, another cause for her renown must be sought by those wishing to understand her brilliance in our own age. This was not lacking for her; for even if she were granted no other merit, she was made conspicuous everywhere by a single birth <note>Constance gave birth to the future Emperor Frederick II at the age of 40, an event she made public to prove the child was hers</note>. She was the daughter of William, once the excellent King of the Sicilians. At her birth, as many say, a certain Joachim <note>Joachim of Fiore, a famous Calabrian abbot and mystic known for his prophetic visions</note>, a Calabrian abbot endowed with a prophetic spirit, said to William regarding the newborn...

.Cxi.

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the historical account of the Sicilian succession, specifically focusing on the prophecy regarding Constance, the daughter of Roger II of Sicily.</meta>

...of things to come. At this prophecy, the king <note>Referring to King Roger II of Sicily, who was told a prophecy that his daughter's marriage would destroy the kingdom.</note> was struck with amazement and terror; having placed his trust in the prediction, he began to ponder with anxious meditation how this outcome might come to pass. Seeing no other way for it to occur except through a spouse or a son, and grieving for his kingdom, he determined to thwart it by his own counsel if he could. To take away all hope of marriage and offspring, he compelled her—then a young girl—to be shut within monastic cloisters, so that she might promise perpetual virginity to God by a vow. This would not have been a plan to be despised, had it succeeded. But why do we present foolish and weak efforts against God, who justly punishes the wicked deeds of mortals? Indeed, even a single, tiny push is in vain against Him.

Now she, after her most holy father and brother had passed away, and since no one survived as a legitimate heir to the kingdom except herself, had spent her entire youth there. She was already seen as an old woman <note>Constance was about 30 years old when she left the convent, which was considered quite late for marriage and childbearing in the 12th century.</note>; and after the death of William, the petty king Tancred <note>Tancred of Lecce, who seized the throne in 1189 despite being of illegitimate birth.</note> had taken the crown of the kingdom, followed by his son William, still a young boy. And so it went—whether by the frequent or unworthy changing of kings—that through the factions of the nobles and wars springing up everywhere, it was seen that the whole kingdom was being dragged toward destruction by the sword. 

For this reason, it occurred to certain people who took pity on the misfortune—and this was what eventually followed—that Constance should be given in marriage to some illustrious prince, so that by his aid and power, these pestilential riots might be calmed. It was only with great difficulty and through much trickery that the consent of the Supreme Pontiff <note>The Pope; historically, this involved negotiations with Pope Clement III to release her from her vows.</note> was obtained so that Constance might reach that decision—namely, to marry—even though she remained immovable in the purpose of her religious profession <term>profession: the formal act of entering a religious order and taking permanent vows</term> and her advanced age also seemed an obstacle. 

But even as she refused, matters had proceeded so far that they could not easily be reversed, and she was betrothed to Henry, Emperor of the Romans <note>Henry VI, Holy Roman Emperor</note>, the son of the former Frederick I <note>The famous Emperor Frederick Barbarossa</note>. And so, a wrinkled old woman, having left the sacred cloisters and cast aside the polished veils of the nuns, she was adorned in royal finery. Having married and become Empress, she stepped into the public eye; and she who had vowed perpetual virginity to God [entered] the bridal chamber...

This child later turned out to be a monstrous man and a plague not only to the Kingdom of Sicily but to all of Italy, so that the prophecy of the Calabrian Abbot <note>original: "calabri abbatis"; this refers to Joachim of Fiore (c. 1135–1202), a mystic and theologian who was said to have predicted the child would be a scourge to the Church.</note> would not go unfulfilled.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of naval conflicts in the Mediterranean, likely during the 14th-century struggles between the Angevin and Aragonese dynasties over the Kingdom of Sicily. The previous page likely concluded with a change in leadership.</meta>

->.112.<-

Once [the previous leader] was removed from human affairs and Peter was substituted in his place, it happened that by the command of the King at Messina, a very large fleet was prepared under the leadership of John of Clermont <note>Jean de Clermont, a French nobleman and military commander</note>, a man who was most warlike in those times. This fleet was intended to bring aid to the people of Lipari <note>An island north of Sicily</note>, who were being besieged and were suffering from almost extreme starvation. Believe me, not only were hired soldiers brought along, but many auxiliaries—both from the coast and the inland—and nobles boarded the ships voluntarily to achieve the glory of arms.

For the town had been besieged by the vigorous man Goffredo di Squillace <note>original: "goffredus desquilatio"; likely Goffredo Marzano, Count of Squillace and Admiral of the Kingdom</note>, who was then the naval commander for Robert, King of Jerusalem and Sicily <note>Robert of Anjou, known as Robert the Wise, r. 1309–1343</note>. He had so weakened the townspeople through assaults, engines of war, and frequent encirclement that an early surrender was expected. 

Indeed, when he learned from the reports of scouting boats that the enemy fleet was approaching—a fleet far larger than his own—he gathered his ships into one place in a safe position and waited to see what fortune would bring. The enemy, however, immediately occupied the positions that had been abandoned; with no one hindering them, they brought the supplies they were carrying to the townspeople. 

John, elated by this success, challenged Goffredo to a trial of fate <note>original: "divinationem," here referring to a decisive battle</note>. Since Goffredo was a man of the most fiery spirit, he did not refuse. During the night, he strengthened his fleet with planking and towers, organized his ships and gear, and as dawn appeared, he encouraged his men to battle with a fervent prayer. Having raised the anchors and given the signal, he turned his prows toward the Sicilians. 

John, however, did not truly have the heart to take on Goffredo, nor did he expect to face such a massive weight of Sicilian ships. He had positioned his own vessels not for a fair fight, but for pursuing those he expected to flee. Seeing the passion and the preparation of the approaching enemy, his courage almost failed him and he was afraid. He regretted having sought the very thing he had least expected to actually obtain. 

Now distrusting his own situation and with a dampened spirit, yet not wanting to appear completely lifeless, the leader suddenly reformed his line of battle as much as the time allowed, and he himself gave the signal for combat. The enemy were already present, who, having raised a great shout, [approached] those coming slowly...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Camiola Turinga, a noblewoman of Messina, and her dealings with Roland, the natural son of King Frederick of Sicily, who had been captured in battle.</meta>

.114.

...if he would take her as a husband. She sent certain people to inquire whether he wished to cast off his chains under this agreement. They easily obtained his consent! And so, with all the solemnity of the law preserved, he betrothed her as his wife through the person of a proxy <note>A legal representative who stands in for a person during a ceremony.</note> by his own consent and by the pledge of a ring. Without delay, after Camiola sent and paid two thousand ounces of gold, he was released from prison and returned to Messina. 

Yet he did not go to his bride, acting as if no word of marriage had ever been spoken. At first, Camiola was merely surprised; but finally, when she realized the man's ingratitude, she was indignant. However, lest she should seem to be driven by anger rather than acting by legal right, she first had him civilly requested to complete the sacred marriage rites. When he altogether denied that any such agreement had been made with him, she brought him before an ecclesiastical judge. There, with signed documents and the testimony of proven men, she proved he was her husband. 

After he had confessed this, blushing, and the woman's kindness toward him was recognized, he was rebuked by his brothers. Moved by the urging of his friends, he was brought to the point where he assented to the woman's petition and asked for the marriage to proceed. 

But the woman of great spirit, as he was pleading in the presence of many, addressed him with almost these very words: "I have reason, Roland, to give thanks to God. For before you could diminish the integrity of my chastity under the pretext of marriage, you showed the treachery of your wickedness. And by the favor of Him whose most holy name you attempted to mock with wicked perjury, the law has struck back at your lie—which is a very great thing for me regarding you and your marriage. I think, while you were still locked up, you thought I had forgotten my own status <note>original: "conditionis"; Camiola was a wealthy commoner, while Roland was of royal blood</note>, rashly desiring a royal husband, and that I was burning with womanly lust for your beauty. You thought that once you had received your freedom at my expense, you could mock and crush me with a single denial, and keep yourself—restored to your former honors—for a more splendid marriage. Indeed, as much as was in your power, you strove to do so. But He who looks down from on high upon the humble..."

<meta>This page continues the speaker's rebuke, likely a noblewoman, against a man she rescued from prison who has subsequently betrayed her. It follows the previous page's themes of betrayal and misplaced mercy.</meta>

->.4.<-

Nor does He desert those who hope in Him; having recognized the sincerity of my mind, He acted so that, with little effort of mine, I might shatter your frauds, uncover your ingratitude, and demonstrate your perfidy. Nor did I perform this deed of mine only out of detestation for your impiety; for henceforth the brothers and all others can see what should be entrusted to your loyalty, what friends may hope from you, and what enemies should fear. I have lost gold; you have lost your reputation. I have lost hope; you have lost the favor <note>original: 'gratiam'</note> of the king and of your friends. In the guise of a noblewoman <note>original: 'specie matronae'</note>, they marvel at my magnificence and extol me with praises; you are an ignominious laughingstock to all who know you, and to those who do not, you are a slave.

In this, however, I was deceived for some time: I foolishly believed that I had snatched a royal and illustrious youth from his chains, whereas I see now that I have liberated a lying camp-follower <term>lixa</term> <note>A 'lixa' was a low-ranking civilian who followed an army, often associated with menial tasks or shifty behavior.</note>, a faithless Geryon <note>Geryon was a mythological giant with three bodies, often used by medieval and Renaissance writers as a symbol of fraud and treachery.</note>, and a monstrous beast. Nor would I wish you to judge yourself so highly as to believe that I was drawn to this for your sake alone: I was moved by the memory of the true kindnesses your father showed toward my father. If indeed your father was a man of sacred memory... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; 'sacre record' is likely 'sacre recordationis'.</note>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech of Camiola Turinga, a wealthy Sicilian widow, addressing Roland (the illegitimate son of King Frederick of Sicily) after she successfully sued him to acknowledge their marriage contract.</meta>

.114.

...now that you have become your own master <note>original: "tui iuris factus es." A legal term meaning one is no longer under the guardianship of another.</note>, since I am Camiola, who alone was mindful of you, who alone shared in your misfortune, who alone paid out my own wealth for your safety. I am Camiola, who rescued you with my money from the hands of your greatest enemy—one greater than your own kin—from chains, from prison, and from extreme misery. When you were slipping into despair, I lifted you up into hope; I restored you to your fatherland, to the royal palace, and to your former light. From a weak and shameful captive, I made you a strong and beautiful royal youth. Why do I recall to your mind things you ought to remember and cannot deny?

You indeed have repaid such memorable benefits with these "thanks": that you, as my betrothed, dared to deny our marriage—even though it was protected by honorable and holy testimonies and signed documents—and to despise and belittle <term>floccifacere</term> <note>literally "to value at a tuft of wool," meaning to consider something worthless.</note> your own redeemer, and, if you could, to stain her with shameful suspicion. You were ashamed, O man of insane mind, to have a widow born of a knightly man <note>original: "equestri viro." Refers to the Equestrian order, the social class of knights, which was lower than Roland’s royal status.</note> as your wife.

Oh, how much better it would have been to feel shame for having nullified your sworn faith, for having held the holy and terrible name of God in suspense, and for having shown your abominable ingratitude, which is so overflowing with these vices. I confess I am not of royal blood; but since I have been involved with royal maidens, daughters-in-law, and wives since my cradle, it is no wonder I have adopted royal manners and a royal spirit—which is enough to assume a royal nobility.

But why say more? I will be easy on you in this matter, in which you were as difficult as possible for me. You denied being mine when you were. Now that I have proven you are mine, I voluntarily release you from being so. Let your royal fame be stained with the mark of infidelity; let the youthful strength be yours, and your fleeting beauty. I, for my part, will remain content in my widowhood; and the fortunes God granted me, I shall leave to more honorable heirs than those fathered by you.

Go then, unfortunate youth, since you have made yourself unworthy of having me. Learn at your own expense by what arts and what deceits you may play with other women. For me it is enough that I was once deceived by you, because of which never again...
