<meta>This page continues the story of Camiola Turinga and begins the biography of Queen Joan I of Naples.</meta>

"I once intended to be with you! But now I judge that preserving a celibate life is far to be preferred to your embraces." Having said these things, she withdrew from his sight; nor from then on could she be moved from her praiseworthy purpose by any prayers or warnings. 

Roland, however, confounded and repenting of his cowardice <note>original: *ignauie*, which can imply both laziness and a lack of spirit or honor</note> too late, was shunned by everyone. With a downcast face, fleeing not only from the King but even from the sight of common people, he departed into a miserable fate, not daring to reclaim by law what he had rejected through deceit. 

The King and the other nobles marveled at the woman's noble spirit and praised her with great wonder, uncertain which was more commendable: that Camiola, contrary to the usual female greed <note>original: *tenacitatem femineam*, a reference to the medieval stereotype that women were naturally miserly or "clinging" to wealth</note>, had ransomed the young man with such a vast sum of money, or that, once he was ransomed and conquered, she had so spiritedly spurned and rejected him as unworthy.

## On Joan, Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily. Chapter 104

Joan, Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily, is a woman illustrious in our age beyond all others in her lineage, power, and character. It would have been better to remain silent about her than to write so little, were it not that silence might be mistaken for personal malice <note>original: *odium*</note>. 

She was the first daughter of that most serene prince, Charles, the renowned Duke of Calabria—himself the firstborn of King Robert of Jerusalem and Sicily, of famous memory—and of Mary, sister of King Philip of the Franks <note>King Philip VI of France</note>. If we wished to trace the grandfathers and great-grandfathers of her parents to the very end, we would not stop until, ascending through countless kings, we arrived at Dardanus <note>The mythical founder of Troy</note>, the first founder of Ilion <note>Troy</note>, whose father the ancients claimed was Jove...

<summary>This page concludes the tale of Camiola Turinga, who rejects the man she rescued for his dishonorable behavior, and introduces Queen Joan I of Naples, tracing her royal lineage back to the mythical founders of Troy.</summary>

<keywords>Camiola Turinga, Roland, Queen Joan I of Naples, Robert of Sicily, Dardanus, Troy, nobility, ransom, virtue, lineage</keywords>

--- Page 234 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of a female sovereign’s inheritance and the vast territories under her rule, following the legal confirmation of her status as heir.</meta>

.115.

The other offspring, it was decreed by law—with him also commanding it so—that she, surviving him, should be made the heir of the kingdoms upon his death. Nor indeed did her very great inheritance fall beyond the <term>torrid zone</term> <note>The equatorial region, which ancient geographers often considered too hot for habitation</note>, or among the <term>Sarmatians</term> <note>Ancient nomadic peoples of Eastern Europe and the steppes</note> beneath the icy pole; rather, it lies between the Adriatic and Tyrrhenian Seas. It stretches from Umbria and Picenum and the ancient homeland of the <term>Volsci</term> <note>An ancient Italic tribe from the region south of Rome</note> down to the mirror-like strait <note>The Strait of Messina, separating mainland Italy from Sicily</note>. Within these borders, the ancient Campanians, Lucanians, Bruttians, Salentini, Calabrians, Daunians, and the Vestini <note>original: "vestales"; likely referring to the Vestini, an ancient Italic people</note>, as well as the Samnites, Peligni, Marsi, and many others, obey her rule <note>original: "imperio"</note>. To name even greater things, there is the Kingdom of Jerusalem, the island of Sicily, and the territory of Piedmont in Cisalpine Gaul <note>The region of Northern Italy "this side" of the Alps</note>, although these are currently occupied by the injustice of certain usurpers. Likewise, those who inhabit the "seventh province" between <term>Narbonnese Gaul</term> <note>A Roman province in what is now southern France</note>, the Rhone, and the Alps, along with the County of Forcalquier, equally obey her commands and acknowledge her as their lady and queen.

O, how many famous cities are in these regions! How many noble towns, how many gulfs of the sea and refuges for sailors, how many shipyards, how many lakes, how many healing springs, how many woods, groves, pastures, and pleasant retreats, and fertile fields! Furthermore, how many numerous peoples and how many great nobles there are, and how grand is the opulence and the abundance of all things pertaining to the sustenance of life—it would truly not be easy to describe. Since this is a massive domain, and it is not customary for such to be possessed by women, it brings no less wonder than renown, if we examine it closely.

And what is far more wonderful, her spirit is sufficient for rule <note>original: "imperio"</note>, even while she maintains such a calm disposition for her years. For after she was distinguished with the royal crown, rising with strong virtue, she so purged not only the cities and domestic places, but even the Alps, remote pastures, groves, and the dens of wild beasts from the wicked hands of men, that they all fled in terror and shut themselves in high fortresses. Having sent out a column of armed men under an excellent leader, she would not lift the siege of such places until...

It emphasizes the unusual nature of a woman wielding such vast power and her successful efforts to bring order and security to her lands.</summary>

...since, after capturing the fortifications, she punished those wicked men 
with execution? This was something which none of the previous kings had either 
wanted to do or were able to accomplish. She brought the lands she possesses 
into such order that not only the poor man, but also the wealthy, 
may safely set out on a journey wherever he wishes, traveling by night 
or day. And—what is no less beneficial—she brought the 
illustrious men and the nobles of the kingdom back to a better state 
with such moderation and corrected their loose morals, so that, 
having set aside their old pride—those who once held kings in contempt—
today they tremble before the face of an angry woman. She is, 
moreover, a woman so sharp-eyed <term>oculata: literally "endowed with eyes," meaning exceptionally vigilant or discerning</term> 
that you could hardly hope to deceive her by fraud any more than by wit. 
She is long-suffering and steadfast, so that you cannot easily 
turn her from her purpose in vain. This was sufficiently demonstrated 
long ago by the assaults of fickle fortune, by which she was very 
often shaken and tossed by sharp movements and surrounded by 
various storms. For she has endured the internal discords of 
petty kings and brothers <note>original: "reguloꝝ fratꝝ discidia." This likely refers to the Castilian War of Succession and the conflicts between Isabella and her half-brother Henry IV or her brother Alfonso.</note> and foreign wars, sometimes even 
within the heart of the kingdom...

<meta>This page serves as the "Conclusio" (Conclusion) of the manuscript, following the biographies of famous women throughout history. It marks the transition from the final biographical entry to the author's closing remarks.</meta>

->. 117 .<-

### Conclusion

**I**n reaching even unto our own women / as is sufficiently apparent / among whom, though they be so few, there is such a number of shining examples / that I believe it more honorable to bring a clear end to what I have begun / than to proceed further with these leading women of today. And this is especially true / since so distinguished a queen has concluded / what Eve, the first mother of all, began. <note>original: "Eua primum omnium parens." The author, Giovanni Boccaccio, structured his work *De Mulieribus Claris* (On Famous Women) to span human history, starting with the biblical Eve and ending with his contemporary, Queen Joan I of Naples.</note> I know, however, that there will be no lack of those who say / that many women have been omitted: and these, above others, may raise other objections which / perhaps could be justly refuted. I, however (that I may first respond with humility), freely confess that I have omitted many: for / indeed, I could not have reached them all before all others: because Time, the vanquisher of fame, has taken many away. <note>The "vanquisher of fame" refers to the historical process where records are lost and names are forgotten over the centuries.</note> Nor was it granted to me to be able to see all of those surviving: and of those known, memory does not always serve the one who desires it. Truly, lest they think me entirely unmindful, I want them to believe that it was not through my oversight / that many women—Barbarian as well as Greek and Latin / and the wives of emperors and kings—have been passed over: I indeed saw innumerable ones and those...

<summary>In this concluding section, the author justifies ending his chronicle of famous women, noting that he has bookended the work between Eve and the current Queen. He acknowledges that many worthy women were left out due to the ravages of time and the limits of his own memory.</summary>

<keywords>Conclusion, Eve, Queen Joan of Naples, Time, Fame, historical omission, Greek women, Latin women, Barbarian women</keywords>

# Front Cover

The volume is bound in wooden boards covered with a red-stained material <note>likely leather or tawed parchment</note>. The surface features "blind-tooled" decoration <note>a method of stamping designs into leather without using gold leaf or pigments</note> in a pattern of intersecting diagonal lines that form a diamond-shaped grid. 

Small, faint floral rosettes have been stamped within some of the diamond compartments and near the top right corner. The cover is heavily worn from centuries of handling; the red surface has peeled away in several areas, especially toward the bottom where the vertical grain of the underlying wood is visible. A small metal fragment on the left edge indicates where a clasp <note>hinged fasteners used to keep parchment books tightly closed to prevent the pages from warping</note> was once attached. 

<summary>The front cover of the manuscript, showing a decorative diamond-grid pattern on red-stained material over wooden boards.</summary>
<keywords>binding, blind-tooling, red leather, wooden boards, book clasp, rosettes</keywords>
